#4620. Vampire: The Masquerade - Bloodlines remaster or remake rumours begin to flow, reportedly in part thanks to a metal band from the original's score

Genitorturers contributed a track to the original

Rumours of Troika classic Vampire: The Masquerade - Bloodlines potentially getting the remaster or remake treatment have surfaced. They've come from two places: one being the fairly standard source of poster on a game leak-obsessed section of Reddit, the other being rather more unorthodox. It's not often a metal band allegedly pause mid-concert to ask the crowd if they know an old game's getting a revamp.

Yet, as spotted by Perfectly Crimson Gamer and Europeanvideogamer, that appears to be exactly what happened at a gig industrial metal band Genitorturers played in Brooklyn on October 15th.

According to a Reddit post from an alleged attendee of this concert "it was great. but at one point, they asked us if we knew about VTMB - then they said they’re remaking the game". PC Gamer report that Nicholas Golden, a member of another band who performed as part of the Genitorturers gig, has corroborated the post's assertions. Genitorturer have an established link to the original Bloodlines, with their tune Lecher Bitch being played at a bar called The Last Round that's frequented by some of the game's Anarch bloodsuckers.

Meanwhile, in the equally rowdy locale of the r/GamingLeaksAndRumours subreddit, a user who claims to have previously given people a heads up that Forza Horizon 6 would be set in Japan alleged that they've heard about Bloodlines coming back via a remaster rather than a remake. "I can confirm that it is in the works. NOT a Remake, but a Remaster, with some caveats" was their exact wording. The user then went on to stress that the industry's especially volatile right now in terms of games in the works being "one slip or one economic downturn away from being cancelled". That's not inaccurate, but it also does feel a bit like someone delberately covering their basis so they can easily point to the 'plans might have changed' sign if people pull them up on their claims further down the line.

All in all, I'm inclined to treat all of this with a heavy dose of salt grains. The Chinese Room developed follow-up Vampire: The Masquerade - Bloodlines 2 came out this time last year and generally struggled to reignite the magic of the game whose name it shared. Even Bloodlines 2's former creative director eventually came out and said it shouldn't have been given the name it was. From the verdicts I've seen it's not a terrible game, but also not one you'd expect to have had a positive influence on any behind-the-scenes efforts to get a full-blown re-do of the original on the go.

That's not to say such a thing isn't happening and it'd be lovely to see a studio like Nightdive given a crack at it. There's also a chance Genitorturers were mistakenly referring to Bloodlines 2 as a remake of the original Vampire, which would be as funny as it would be tragic for fans of the series if it turns out to be the case.

#4619. People Think They Drive Better High. These Scientists Are Testing the Theory.

Inside the lab that’s decoding the dangers of driving stoned.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

A HIT OR two from a friend’s vape at a party, a low-dose gummy at a concert, a THC-infused drink at a comedy show—the average guy would be fine to drive home a few hours after doing any of this, right?

Michael Stevens, PhD, director of the Clinical Neuroscience & Development Laboratory at the Olin Neuropsychiatry Research Center, has spent roughly a decade trying to answer that question. When I asked Stevens how long he’d recommend a friend wait before getting into a car after taking THC, he joked, “How much do I like this particular hypothetical friend?” before settling on three to six hours. Yes, that’s a huge range.

That uncertainty is a big issue right now, especially as cannabis legalization spreads across the U.S., and researchers beyond Stevens are raising concerns. One 2021 analysis found a 15 percent increase in fatal car crashes following marijuana legalization, and a separate review linked legalization to increases in car accidents, injuries, or fatalities.

Recently, Stevens and his colleagues conducted a lab study involving a high-tech driving simulator. The “car” looks something like an elite gaming rig, complete with a steering wheel, pedals, and a screen that features virtual roadways.

After tracking 19 performance metrics over several hours and examining brain activity through an MRI, researchers found that six of those metrics (roughly one-third) showed significant impairment following cannabis use. Stevens and his team witnessed drivers passing cars too closely or tailgating, increasing the risk of collision. Some drivers failed to make the necessary steering microadjustments to keep their vehicle centered in its lane.

It’s probably obvious that participants who inhaled higher-potency cannabis tended to drive worse than those who received lower doses of THC. But also, THC levels weren’t a strong predictor of who drove more dangerously—and when. Some participants’ dangerous driving faded gradually, says Stevens, while others’ worsened before improving. “The other thing that jumps out is that about two-thirds of people said they’d be willing to drive, even while admitting they still felt high and after we measured they were objectively driving worse,” Stevens says.

It’s true, 35 percent of people believe they drive better when high, according to a recent AAA survey. There is some truth there, says Stevens: People can be more cautious when they’re stoned. “But at the same time, being high whacks out many cognitive abilities,” he says. In Stevens’s study, people who drove while high tended to drive a little too slow, which can disrupt the flow of traffic, encourage other drivers to make risky passing maneuvers, and cause rear-end collisions.

Given what we know (and don’t) about how cannabis works in the body, it’s unlikely that we’ll find a magic THC number that tells us who is and isn’t impaired, at least in the near future. For now, Stevens says, the safest approach to cannabis and driving is pretty simple: When in doubt, wait it out. Regardless of how high you think you are—or aren’t—just call an Uber, text a buddy, or stay put.

ALL 50 HAVE an impaired-driving law that can apply to cannabis. But determining if a person’s too high to drive is all over the place.

Some states (Illinois, Montana, Nevada, Ohio, Washington) have been trying to apply the blood-alcohol framework to gauge drunkenness to cannabis by adopting a legal threshold, ranging between 2 and 5 ng/mL THC in the blood. These roadside assessments take time and aren’t lock-tight, because THC levels can plummet within 30 to 40 minutes of use. Many states (including California, Massachusetts, New York) use an effects-based approach, which looks for signs of impairment: erratic braking, slowed responses, bloodshot eyes. But that’s subjective. “You need a cut-and-dried kind of a test where there’s little room for error,” says Michael Stevens, PhD, of the Olin Neuropsychiatry Research Center. “Think of it like a mammogram screening—you really want the right answer.” For years, tech firms have been racing to develop a reliable roadside cannabis Breathalyzer. None have solved the core problem: Measuring THC isn’t the same thing as measuring impairment. That’s because THC sticks around in body fat. So if someone uses THC frequently, they have a reserve of the main psychoactive chemical that would throw off the test.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

Rebecca Strong is a Boston-based health and wellness journalist who covers everything from nutrition and fitness to mental health, psychology, and the science behind everyday well-being. Her work has appeared in Business Insider, Men's Health, USA TODAY, Healthline, Health.com, Bustle, and other national publications. She's also the creator and host of WELL-BENT, an evidence-based wellness podcast where she explores the messy, often misunderstood questions surrounding modern wellness through conversations with clinicians, researchers, and other experts.

Rebecca is an alumna of Emerson College and The Boston Conservatory at Berklee, and her music can sometimes be heard on Boston radio stations, including 92.5 The River and Country 102.5. When she's not reporting or on the mic, she's usually chasing after her mischievous toddler, trying desperately to keep just one houseplant alive, or tinkering with her espresso machine in pursuit of the perfect iced honey latte.

#4618. It’s official: Battlefield 6 is 2025's best-selling game, but Black Ops 7 still managed to be November’s top-seller

2025 will be remembered for many things, but in the ultra-competitive games industry, it’s going to be thought of as the year Battlefield 6 did what many thought couldn’t happen.

Circan’s November 2025 video game sales report has easily been one of the most anticipated. For one, November is always one of the most lucrative for the games industry, but this year in particular has seen several major hits - including new entries in two rival series.

We’re talking, of course, about Battlefield 6, and Black Ops 7 the latest releases in their respective multiplayer shooter series. The decades-old fight to claim the top spot has long been won by Call of Duty, but the latest Battlefield has achieved what many thought was impossible.

Cricana’s report covers spending on video game software, hardware, and accessories in the U.S. November 2025 saw spending fall by 4% compared to November 2024, bringing in a total of $5.9 billion.

Software technically saw growth during the month, but declines in hardware, and accessories were mainly responsible for the drop in overall spending. Black Ops 7 continued Call of Duty’s 18-Year Streak in topping sales in its launch month.

However, this year’s game did not also top the year-to-date charts, something the series has done consistently, and more than any other top-selling game each year - with rare exceptions. Battlefield 6 is firmly in the lead there, cementing its claim as 2025’s best-selling title. Black Ops 7, fittingly, occupies in the #7 spot there.

Separately from the best-selling game title fight, November has actually been one of the worst for the industry in terms of hardware sales. The report notes that the three major platform holders - Sony, Microsoft, and Nintendo have all seen their hardware sales decline.

Spending on hardware during November amounted to only $695 million, a massive 27% decline compared to November 2024. According to the report, that’s the worst dollar amount total for any November since 2005, when it comes to hardware specifically. All three current-gen consoles totaled 1.6 million units sold, which Circana also notes is the lowest for any November since 1995.

This all doesn’t bode especially well for the industry. November has always been a key holiday shopping month, but it’s clear the constantly rising console prices - and already high game prices - have had a big impact.

#4616. Ultra-Processed Food Intake May Increase Dementia, Stroke Risk

  • Healthy adults who consume high amounts of ultra-processed foods (UPFs) may be more likely to show signs of vascular brain injury, which has been linked to stroke risk and cognitive decline.
  • UPFs account for more than 50% of daily energy intake in high-income countries such as the United States.
  • People with the highest UPF intake had higher body fat levels and greater odds of vascular brain injury than those with the lowest intake.
  • You can reduce UPF intake by making simple swaps toward whole foods, such as fruits, vegetables, and grains prepared at home.

Healthy adults who eat high levels of ultra-processed foods (UPFs) may be more likely to show signs of damage to the brain that could increase the likelihood of stroke and future memory problems, according to a study published October 15 in JAMA Network Open. 

UPFs are often defined using the NOVA classification: These foods typically have 5 or more ingredients and include substances not typically found in culinary preparations. This category of foods includes packaged snack foods, sugary cereals, soft drinks, and frozen ready-to-eat meals. 

UPFs account for more than 50% of daily energy intake in high-income countries, including the United States, Canada, and the United Kingdom. 

Previous research has linked these foods to adverse cardiometabolic outcomes. This new study adds to growing evidence linking high UPF intake to adverse health outcomes.

High UPF intake may increase the risk of vascular brain injury

This was a cross-sectional analysis of 8,783 adults between the ages of 35 and 69 without cardiovascular disease. The participants were all enrolled in the Canadian Alliance for Healthy Hearts and Minds (CAHHM) and Prospective Urban and Rural Epidemiology-MIND (PURE-MIND) cohorts. 

The researchers used MRI scans to evaluate the participants’ brains. They also set out to analyze the cross-sectional relationship between UPF consumption, body fat, vascular brain injury (VBI), and cognitive performance. 

They measured the participants’ habitual UPF intake using a food frequency questionnaire. Intake was expressed as servings based on a 2,000-calorie-per-day diet. 

UPFs accounted for an average of 25% of participants’ daily diets. For the individuals with the highest intake, this increased to 45% of everything on their plates, compared to 16% for those who ate the least. 

The researchers also noted that UPFs were not just an added part of individual diets. These foods appeared to replace whole foods.

Among people with the highest UPF intake, fruits, vegetables, grains, and other minimally processed foods accounted for 41% of their daily food intake. Among those with the lowest UPF intake, minimally processed foods made up 68% of their daily intake. 

When the researchers compared the highest intake to the lowest, they found that those with a higher intake of UPFs had 1.7% more body fat. They also found that people with a higher UPF intake had higher odds of VBI. 

“These findings are concerning, but it is important to remember that an association does not prove cause and effect,” said Michelle Routhenstein, Preventive Cardiology Dietitian at Entirely Nourished, who wasn’t involved in the study.

“From my perspective as a preventive cardiology dietitian, these findings reinforce the importance of nourishing our blood vessels to support both cardiovascular and brain health.” 

Although researchers found no significant differences in cognitive performance, the signs of vascular brain injury observed in the study have been associated with an increased risk of stroke and cognitive decline.

These findings suggest a possible link between UPF intake and signs of brain changes. However, participants were only assessed once and weren’t followed over time. This means the researchers were unable to determine whether UPFs directly caused the changes. 

“While we can’t conclude from this study that limiting UPFs would improve brain health, we have decades of literature demonstrating that higher intakes of green leafy vegetables, brightly colored fruits, whole grains, and lean protein sources are good for brain health,” said Suzanne Judd, PhD, Director of the UC College of Medicine’s Center for Public Health, who wasn’t involved in the study.  

“So focusing more on what can be brought into the diet rather than what should be eliminated might be a good way to start,” she added. 

Why might UPFs affect the brain?

The preservatives [in UPFs] may have a direct impact on the vascular system, while the loss of antioxidants results in a less robust response to inflammation in the body. 

“Additionally, sugar or salt is often added to make the food a bit tastier so people consume it in higher quantities. Over-consumption of food can lead to weight gain and diabetes, which can negatively impact brain health,” Judd said. 

Tips for reducing your UPF intake

Judd told Healthline that it is virtually impossible to completely avoid UPFs. Even some “health” foods, like protein powder and some yogurts, are considered ultra-processed. 

It is more important to look at diet overall and emphasize consuming foods as close to their natural state as possible, such as fruits, vegetables, grains, and meats purchased raw or frozen and then cooked at home.

Routhenstein recommended starting simply with one manageable swap rather than trying to overhaul your entire diet. 

She said that you should identify an ultra-processed food you eat regularly and consider a more nutrient-rich alternative. It is important to account for convenience, food preferences, budget, and lifestyle so that the change feels realistic and is sustainable over the long term.

“I want people to feel empowered, not fearful, when making food choices,” said Routhenstein. “Healthy eating does not mean having to cook everything from scratch. What matters most is building consistent, nourishing habits that support both heart and brain health.”

#4615. GTA 6 squeaks into eligibility for The Game Awards 2026 by the smallest of margins - but can it break Clair Obscur: Expedition 33's record number of wins?

This generation's mega-game, Grand Theft Auto 6, will be eligible for The Game Awards 2026 by a matter of one day.

As highlighted on X by industry watcher Shinobi602, the cut-off date for this year's The Game Awards is 20th November, meaning GTA 6 - which arrives on 19th November - can be nominated across the show's various categories in 2026 and can potentially win. "Games must be made commercially available to customers on or before Friday, November 20, 2026, at 9pm PT," The Game Awards' website FAQ states.

The fit is so snug it might appear an exception has been made for Rockstar's game, but as Shinobi602 also noted, cut-off dates have been pretty similar each year, varying only by a day or two.

With GTA 6 in contention, the question becomes one of how much it could dominate proceedings. Rewind to Rockstar's last big release - Red Dead Redemption 2 in 2018 - and that game took home four awards: Best Narrative, Best Audio Design, Best Score/Music, and Best Performance (won by Roger Clark for his portrayal of protagonist Arthur Morgan).

The current TGA record holder - the game to beat - is Clair Obscur: Expedition 33, which last year took home nine wins, including the coveted Game of the Year award. Before Clair Obscur, the previous leader was Baldur's Gate 3, with six wins. GTA 6 is an order of magnitude larger in terms of budget, scope, and public anticipation than either game, especially Clair Obscur. The weight of expectation is on Rockstar, that's for sure.

The Game Awards 2026 takes place on 10th December, and usually bookends the year's industry activity, after which we all frantically try to decide what our personal games of the year are. Eurogamer, for full disclosure, is a member of The Game Awards jury, along with many other gaming websites.

Meanwhile, Rockstar released a new launch trailer for GTA 6 this afternoon, highlighting Jason and Lucia's steamy relationship - literally, as they frolic in the shower - and the strain of being in a high-stakes crime environment such as theirs.

Rockstar is also currently embroiled in an employment tribunal in Scotland revolving around the sudden firing of 31 staff from Rockstar North last year. Those affected call it union-busting, whereas Rockstar insists employees were fired for gross misconduct. The verdict is now not expected until well into November, almost bang on when GTA 6 releases.

#4614. Top-Ranked Mechanics Tool Sets We Recommend

Our car experts choose every product we feature. We may earn money from the links on this page.

Whether you're an expert technician or a driveway mechanic—or buying a gift for one—this guide will help you choose the perfect tool kit to keep the work running smoothly.

This article was updated in October 2026.

Working on your own vehicle can be wildly satisfying—and extremely frustrating without the right tools. A well-equipped set helps you work more efficiently, protect your car’s fasteners and components, and avoid turning a routine repair into an afternoon of stripped nuts and creative language.

Whether you’re a seasoned mechanic or a weekend DIYer, these are the tools we reach for to get the job done.

If you already own some tools and want to expand your collection, or if you’re just starting out, we’ve selected our favorite tool sets for mechanics below. Some are pricey and comprehensive; others are affordable and have everything you need and nothing you don’t.

No matter what—or who—you’re shopping for, if you’re looking for the best mechanic’s tool set you can buy, we’ve got you covered.

Expert Tip: Don’t spend money on more tools than you need. If you only occasionally do light auto maintenance at home, you don’t need a complete, comprehensive “master mechanics” toolkit.

An affordable yet complete 168-piece tool set, the DeWalt DWMT73803 is ideal for anyone getting started with DIY vehicle maintenance, or those who want a complete set to augment their collection. Even better, it won’t break the bank (DeWalt always seems to be on sale at Amazon) so it makes a great gift.

It includes 1/4-inch, 3/8-inch, and 1/2-inch ratchets and sockets both metric and SAE, plus combination wrenches, hex keys, and a nut driver with driver bits. It all comes in a custom-molded case with an integrated handle and double latches, and the entire kit comes backed by DeWalt's limited lifetime warranty.

Another tool brand that always seems to be marked down at Amazon, the Craftsman 216-piece mechanics tool set is an all-in-one kit designed for automotive enthusiasts and professionals alike. The set includes a variety of sockets, wrenches, ratchets, specialty bits, hex keys, and a magnetic nut driver, making it ideal for both automotive mechanical and home repair tasks. The set is neatly organized in a heavy-duty, 3-drawer Versastack toolbox featuring built-in organizers for easy access.

The most comprehensive kit on our list doesn't come with a carrying case or toolbox, but Husky’s 815-piece master mechanics tool set has nearly every tool you could imagine for day-to-day tinkering and in-depth auto repair.

The set includes 13 drive tools, 177 standard sockets, 141 deep sockets, 117 specialty sockets, 20 adapters and extensions, 145 wrenches, and 202 additional tools such as screwdrivers, hex keys, and socket rails. It features both 6-point and 12-point sockets, both deep and standard, as well as 1/4-, 3/8-, and 1/2-inch ratchets with quick-release and flex-heads. Other tools include breaker bars, a T-handle, a bit driver, and a spinner handle.

The Gearwrench 232-piece mechanics tool set features a wide assortment of tools, including full-polish chrome standard and D sockets, combination wrenches, Allen hex keys, quick-release ratchets, and a magnetic bit driver. All the tools meet or exceed ASME standards, and the kit comes with Gearwrench's standard lifetime warranty.

The tools are housed in a durable 3-drawer storage box with independent sliding drawers and a steel-hinged lid.

Every garage needs a dependable socket set, and this 201-piece Amazon Basics kit covers ground without demanding a pro-tool budget. It includes SAE and metric sockets, ratchets, extensions, and driver bits, all organized in a molded case. The chrome-vanadium steel tools are built for routine maintenance and repairs, making it a smart, affordable starting point for DIY mechanics.

Before you start searching for the best tool kit, you'll want to consider a few things. Here's what you should be looking for:

Tool Selection: You'll want a kit that covers all your bases, from sockets and wrenches to pliers and screwdrivers. Ideally, you'll want a kit with both SAE (standard) and metric tools and sockets, so it'll last for years regardless of your vehicle.

Quality and Durability: Nobody wants a flimsy tool that breaks or wears down quickly. Look for kits with high-quality materials so you can confidently tackle those tough jobs. This includes both the carrying case and the tools themselves.

Case Design and Portability: A solid, well-organized case can be a lifesaver when you're on the go. Search for tool kits with sturdy, easy-to-carry cases that keep your tools neatly arranged and ready for action. In theory, this should prevent you from losing your 10mm socket, but we all know it'll inevitably go missing.

Budget and Value: Let's face it: we all have different budgets. Remember your budget, but don't sacrifice quality for a bargain. It's all about finding that sweet spot between price and performance. It's better to get only the tools you need with a higher-quality set than to get more than you need at the expense of quality.

Brand Reputation: Trustworthy brands often deliver reliable, high-quality tools. Better-known brands also have better warranties and customer-service support, which should make life easier if anything breaks down the road.

A quality mechanic tool set should include a variety of sockets, ratchets, wrenches, screwdrivers, pliers, hex keys, and possibly a hammer. More comprehensive sets may include additional tools like nut drivers, extension bars, adapters, and even specialized automotive tools.

Sockets typically come in two types: standard (SAE) and metric. The type you need depends on the measurements used in the vehicle or machinery you're working on. Most tool sets include both types, although some do stick specifically to one format or the other. There are also different drive sizes (1/4", 3/8", 1/2") and styles, such as deep sockets (for inset nuts) or impact sockets (which are made of a softer metal to prevent shattering when used with an impact wrench).

Quality tools are typically made of various steel alloys for their strength, toughness, and resistance to wear and tear. Tools are often chrome-plated for easier cleaning (and, honestly, who doesn't like shiny tools in their garage?).

It depends on your needs. A complete set often provides the best value if you're starting from scratch. But if you only need a few specific tools or high-quality tools for professional use, buying individually might be the better option to spread out the investment.

Always clean your tools after using them. This can be as simple as wiping them down with a rag to remove dirt, grease, or oil. For rusty tools, use a wire brush or a specialized cleaning solution. Store your tools in a dry place to prevent rust and consider using a rust-preventive spray.

#4613. Life has once again found a way in Athanasia, an "immersive" dinosaur FPS that feels like comfort food for Dino Crisis and System Shock players alike

Good news, people who figure they are going to die of old age before they get another Dino Crisis! Good news also, people who can't get enough throwback immersive sims! Even as Capcom forge ahead with their Resident Evil remake ouroboros, plucky Momentum Games and horror publishers Black Lantern Collective are releasing Athanasia - a first-person homage to both Dino Crisis and System Shock, in which you play intrepid researcher Carmen Flores, who has been sent to an ominous abandoned scientific facility in South America.

One of these days, I'm going to turf up to a collapsing laboratory complex in a game and discover nothing but stationery, old rubber gloves, and unfiled expense claims. I imagine a lot of laboratories are abandoned not due to (for example) gene-modded dilophosaurus outbreaks, but because the funders got cold feet long before Project X reached the prototyping stage. But that day is not this day. Today, there are dinosaurs! Argh, my face and/or intestines! Here's a trailer.

As System Shockables go, this is playing the hits: gun-metal corridors with strip lighting and waist-high vents; hackable panels and inventories crammed with tools and craftable objects; audio logs and secret routes and much talk of [REDACTED] in the world-building. Hopefully, whatever [REDACTED] proves to be will introduce some novel flourishes. Could be the title is a hint: athanasia means "deathless". Aww, say it isn't just zombie dinosaurs, eh? The Steam page promises "a terrible truth and ancestral nightmares that have plagued us for longer than we have been human".

The dastardly reptiles do not appear especially smart in the trailer, but then again, it's a montage of killshots. It'd be nice if the velociraptors knew how to play decoy, at least.

Athanasia launches in early access today, with the full release expected sometime in 2027. The initial early access version will encompass three of six planned areas, with most of the enemy and item types. "Depending on community feedback and the pace of development, we may seek to add some additional areas, items and/or enemies beyond our current plans, but our main focus is to deliver what we've already promised," the devs write on Steam.

If you need your imsim fix sooner, the recent RetroSpace is pretty solid. If you like your dinosaurs to have more animation cycles and skin nobbles, keep an eye on The Lost Wild - a plumper, shinier survival horror from a team including Alien: Isolation vet Gary Napper.

#4611. A Handful of Pistachios May Help You Sleep Better, Review Says

  • A narrative review of current evidence suggests that pistachios may help support sleep.
  • Pistachios contain nutrients associated with sleep health, including tryptophan, vitamin B6, magnesium, phytonutrient melatonin, fiber, and polyphenols, which may influence sleep-related biological processes.
  • Studies of tryptophan supplements and dietary polyphenols suggest potential sleep benefits, but these findings do not establish that eating pistachios improves sleep.
  • More human clinical trials are needed before pistachios can be recommended as a sleep aid.

Could a handful of pistachios before you go to bed really help you sleep better? 

A review of studies published in Nutrition Research Reviews examines how the nutrients and plant compounds in pistachios might influence sleep health. 

Pistachios contain several nutrients linked to sleep-related processes, prompting an investigation into whether they might offer benefits beyond their nutritional value.

While the findings suggest that pistachios may support sleep, the evidence is inconclusive and warrants further study.

“Pistachios are compelling for sleep research because they bring together several nutrients and plant compounds connected to sleep physiology within one whole food,” said study author Marie-Pierre St-Onge, PhD, professor of nutritional medicine and director of the Center of Excellence for Sleep & Circadian Research in the Department of Medicine at Columbia University Institute of Nutrition, in a news release. 

“Rather than focusing on a single ‘sleep nutrient,’ this review provides a rationale for studying how the complete pistachio food matrix may affect sleep-related pathways,” she continued.

Key nutrients in pistachios may impact sleep

The narrative review examined existing research on pistachios and sleep-related biology.

The researchers explored whether the combination of nutrients in pistachios could promote quality sleep.

Rather than identifying pistachios as a sleep remedy, the researchers outlined biological pathways through which their nutrients and bioactive compounds might influence sleep.

“We were particularly interested in pistachios because they are particularly high in phytomelatonin, polyphenols, and carotenoids, which we noted were plant-derived components that had been shown to have sleep benefits in preclinical studies,” St-Onge told Healthline.

“We also noted their high content of tryptophan and vitamin B6, which are relevant to melatonin production in humans,” she continued.

Pistachios contain several nutrients associated with sleep, including melatonin, which helps regulate the sleep-wake cycle. 

  • Tryptophan: A 1-ounce serving of pistachios contains about 71 milligrams (mg) of this essential amino acid, which the body uses to produce serotonin and melatonin.
  • Vitamin B6: This vitamin helps the body process amino acids and supports pathways involved in neurotransmitter production.
  • Magnesium: Pistachios provide about 31 mg per ounce. Magnesium can help support several bodily functions, including nervous system regulation.
  • Phytomelatonin: Pistachios contain plant-based melatonin, but its absorption and effects in humans remain uncertain.
  • Fiber and polyphenols: These compounds may influence gut microbes and the gut-brain axis, potentially affecting sleep regulation.

Other studies examining the effects of some of these nutrients on sleep offer further context.

A 2021 systematic review and meta-analysis of tryptophan supplementation found that it may reduce wakefulness after sleep onset, particularly at doses of at least 1 gram (g) daily. However, an ounce of pistachios supplies only about 71 mg, which is much less than the doses examined by the researchers. 

Additionally, a 2023 review of dietary polyphenols led by St-Onge identified potential links between plant compounds and sleep outcomes, although results varied across studies. 

Neither review established that polyphenols or tryptophan from pistachios specifically affected sleep quality. 

“I was not surprised that pistachios may help support sleep, given their combination of nutrients involved in sleep regulation,” said Michelle Routhenstein, MS, a preventive cardiology dietitian at EntirelyNourished, who wasn’t involved in the study.

“Pistachios provide tryptophan, an amino acid the body uses to produce serotonin and melatonin; vitamin B6, which supports these pathways; and magnesium, which plays a role in nervous system function and sleep regulation,” she told Healthline.

Combined effects of nutrients may support sleep

The new review highlights the concept of the “food matrix,” or how nutrients and bioactive compounds interact within a whole food.

Instead of investigating tryptophan, magnesium, or melatonin individually, researchers were curious about the combined effects of consuming them together in pistachios.

Laboratory and simulated-digestion research offers clues about how fiber and plant compounds in pistachios and peanuts interact with gut microbes. However, these findings do not establish that eating pistachios changes sleep quality in real-world conditions.

Broader nutrition research also points to a relationship between diet and sleep.

A 2021 review in the Annual Review of Nutrition examined evidence linking dietary patterns and individual nutrients to sleep outcomes. The findings suggest a relationship between sleep and diet, but note that more research is needed to determine cause and effect.

In a nutshell, the current evidence builds a compelling case for pistachios as a sleep aid.

However, it’s too soon to recommend pistachios as a sleep remedy, as many factors may affect sleep quality and insomnia.

“Sleep difficulties can have different underlying causes,” Routhenstein noted. “If inadequate intake of these nutrients is a contributing factor, pistachios may help, but they may not address sleep issues related to other nutrient deficiencies or other causes.”

Pistachios are a healthy snack, not yet a sleep aid

Much of the supporting evidence cited in the review comes from studies of individual nutrients, other foods, laboratory experiments, or biological mechanisms rather than from trials involving whole pistachios. 

Researchers have also reported significant variations in the melatonin content of pistachios, making the amount in a typical serving difficult to pinpoint.

Additionally, the review received support from American Pistachio Growers, and the corresponding author, St-Onge, serves on its advisory board. Larger studies and clinical trials are needed, ideally without industry funding, to ensure well-rounded results.

Indeed, the authors call for randomized controlled trials examining whether eating pistachios affects sleep outcomes, including sleep quality, duration, and melatonin levels. 

“This was a review; we noted that the complete profile of pistachios would make them well-suited to support healthful sleep, but research is needed to evaluate this,” St-Onge told Healthline. 

“This is the next step, and something we are actively working on. We also need to better understand what dosage and frequency of consumption would be optimal.”

Regardless, a handful of pistachios is still considered a healthy choice as part of a balanced diet.

“A small handful, about 1 ounce of shelled pistachios, is an appropriate portion,” Routhenstein said. “Enjoy them during the day or as a light afternoon or evening snack when you’re hungry.”

Other ways nutrition may help promote sleep

If nutrients like tryptophan help promote sleep, it’s possible that other nuts could yield similar results.

“Other nuts, such as almonds and walnuts, may offer similar benefits because they also contain nutrients involved in sleep regulation,” Routhenstein said.

“Early research is encouraging, but it’s too soon to say how much they might help.”

Other foods, like turkey, also contain tryptophan.

“For someone allergic to nuts, foods such as tofu, beans, oats, eggs, dairy, and fish can provide tryptophan, which the body uses to make serotonin and melatonin,” Routhenstein said. “The goal is to meet their nutrient needs rather than rely on one food to improve sleep.”

Sleep health depends on multiple factors. As such, Routhenstein recommends a personalized approach to getting better sleep, from what you eat and when you eat to addressing any nutritional gaps.

“I would also encourage regular, balanced meals throughout the day to support blood sugar management, especially for those with diabetes, while avoiding large meals close to bedtime,” Routhenstein said. “Caffeine later in the day and alcohol in the evening are also worth considering as possible sleep disruptors.”

#4605. 2027 GMC Hummer EV SUV

See all results for new 2027 GMC Hummer EV SUV for sale near 151-0053

Few adjectives can adequately capture the GMC Hummer EV, but "ludicrous" comes close. With its towering stance, 8,660-pound curb weight, and a wheelbase that's easier measured in yards rather than inches, this five-seat SUV is a hulking brute with mega capabilities both on- and off-road.

Despite its planetary mass, two mighty electric powertrains give the Hummer EV SUV enough firepower to break out of its own gravitational field. The 570-hp 2X trim is no slouch, but the 870-hp 3X trim delivers truly physics-defying thrust. All-wheel drive comes as standard, and rear-wheel steering allows for surprising maneuverability while parallel parking or navigating winding OHV trails. It's surprisingly efficient, too, making use of GM’s Ultium EV Platform that grants it more than 300 miles of range and rapid recharge times—though the sheer size of the battery makes juicing up expensive.

Expert Tip: If the SUV doesn’t quite satisfy your definition of ludicrous, GMC also makes a Hummer EV Pickup that is longer, even more powerful, and comes with a five-foot cargo bed sprouting out of the back.

Compare the 2027 GMC Hummer EV SUV with the top vehicles in this segment with our new compare tool.

Changes are minor for the 2027 model year. A handful of new color options have been added, including Dark Ridge, Azurite Blue, Dark Ember, Deep Void Matte, and ICON Yellow. The ICON exterior color harks back to the Hummer H2's signature school bus yellow hue and is available on a limited-run ICON|25 model that also features black exterior accents, serial badging on the instrument cluster, and unique infotainment graphics.

If this year's model is largely unchanged and you're comfortable with a lightly used car, you may want to consider shopping previous model years. A one-to-three-year-old model will likely have a warranty and it'll cost less than buying new.

The 2X has a dual-motor setup that generates 570 horsepower. The 3X has a trio of motors (one up front and two out back) with 830 ponies. Compared with the pickup truck Hummer, the SUV has a shorter wheelbase (126.7 inches versus 135.6), which makes it more maneuverable in parking situations and out on the trails. It also inherits our favorite off-road goodies from the truck version, including four-wheel steering with CrabWalk—which is just what it sounds like—and an adjustable air suspension with Extract mode that can raise it six inches.

GMC also pulls at our heartstrings with a full-size spare tire mounted on the rear gate. Plus, there's an optional Extreme Off-Road package that brings special hardware that includes 35-inch all-terrain tires on 18-inch wheels, extra underbody protection, low-mounted cameras for improved visibility while maneuvering, enhanced axles with heavy-duty components, a locking front differential, and rear motors that can electronically mimic a locked diff.

In Comparison: The last GMC Hummer EV SUV we tested rocketed to 60 mph in just 3.4 seconds—a mind-blowing result for an 8,660-pound behemoth. That makes the Hummer EV quicker than both the Cadillac Vistiq and Lucid Gravity Touring.

The EPA estimates the Hummer EV SUV can go for up to 315 miles between charges, but adding optional mud terrain tires drops that estimate down to a max of 289 miles. We only saw 250 miles of range from its 170-kWh battery pack in our 75-mph highway test.

Recharging can be quick, however. The SUV’s 800-volt architecture gains 100 miles of range in 14 minutes using a DC fast-charger. In our testing, the Hummer SUV required 128 minutes to charge from 10 to 90 percent and averaged a pull of 78 kilowatts. Otherwise, GMC says that a level 2 charger recharges at up to 19.2 kilowatts, providing 30 miles of range in an hour.

The EPA rates the most efficient Hummer EV 2X SUV at 59 MPGe city, 47 MPGe highway, and 53 MPGe combined. In our real-world highway testing, a Hummer EV SUV 3X managed 45 MPGe—just one shy of its government estimate. MPGe stands for miles per gallon of gas equivalent, an EPA metric that estimates PHEV and EV vehicle fuel efficiency. For more information about the Hummer EV SUV's fuel economy, visit the EPA's website.

First Row: The interior of the Hummer EV SUV is part rugged and part sci-fi. Its busy dashboard boasts both vertical and horizontal air vents and a pair of large displays that include a 12.3-inch digital gauge cluster. Some interior materials feel a bit too utilitarian; those lower down on the dash and door panels look and feel out of place in a vehicle this expensive.

Second Row: As to be expected of a vehicle this monstrous, the Hummer EV's second row is about as spacious as a typical living room. Even the tallest rear passengers will find that they have more than enough head and leg room to lounge comfortably.

Cargo Capacity: Along with storage under the load floor, the SUV Hummer boasts an expansive 82 cubic feet of cargo space with the rear seat folded, but the high cargo floor makes it tough to load heavy items. In our testing, we fit 15 carry-on suitcases in the Hummer EV SUV's cargo bay; with the rear seats folded, we fit a total of 33.

The Hummer's cutting-edge technology is complemented by a large 13.4-inch touchscreen infotainment system and extensive connectivity options. The system can handle navigation, both on- and off-road. A 14-speaker Bose sound system comes standard, as does a subscription-based Wi-Fi hotspot and a wireless smartphone charging pad. The Hummer EV SUV is also available with a 6.0-kW onboard generator that can be used to power all sorts of equipment and even charge other EVs.

The Hummer EV SUV comes with an enhanced version of GM's Super Cruise hands-free driving system, as well as a host of driver-assistance technology that currently graces many mainstream models.

For information about the Hummer EV's crash-test results, visit the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) and Insurance Institute for Highway Safety (IIHS) websites.

The Hummer EV SUV comes with a similar warranty to other General Motors products, which means three-year/36,000-mile bumper-to-bumper coverage and five-year/60,000-mile powertrain coverage.

Each year, we put hundreds of vehicles through our rigorous instrumented testing regimen. If the data in the chart below is for a different model year, that's because this vehicle hasn't changed since we last tested it, and the test results remain accurate.

#4604. ACOG Strongly Recommends Hormone Therapy for Menopause Symptoms

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

The American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG) published updated recommendations for menopause management in Obstetrics & Gynecology on October 15.

These expanded recommendations are the most comprehensive guidance to date. They include new clinical guidelines for menopause hormone therapy (MHT), nonhormonal medications, and nonpharmacological treatment of vasomotor symptoms (VMS), which include hot flashes and night sweats, and sleep and mood changes.

According to the ACOG guidance, 50%–92% of females experiencing menopause report VMS.

Around 16%–66% of females in the United States report menopause-related sleep issues, and more than one-third of perimenopausal and postmenopausal females report experiencing depressive symptoms.

“In recent years, misconceptions about the risks of hormone therapy have been widespread, which has made some clinicians reluctant to prescribe it and some patients hesitant to use it,” Bruce Pier, MD, clinical professor and associate fellowship director at the University of North Carolina and collaborator on the ACOG clinical practice guidelines, told Healthline.

“This guidance offers a comprehensive review of the evidence to support individualized counseling and shared decision making. Our hope is that it clears up those misconceptions and expands access to treatment.”

The ACOG is also developing companion guidance for genitourinary syndrome of menopause, expected in 2027.

The ACOG strongly recommends the use of MHT with estrogen and progestogens (or estrogen alone for people without a uterus) for both VMS and vasomotor-related sleep disturbance, in individuals who can use hormones.

It also conditionally recommends conjugated estrogen plus bazedoxifene (Duavee) as an alternative for these issues.

According to a meta-analysis from 2021 that was cited by the guidelines, estrogen therapy, with or without progestogens, significantly reduced VMS frequency and severity compared to a placebo or no treatment. A 2017 meta-analysis ranked transdermal estradiol plus a progestogen as the most effective option for reducing VMS frequency.

A 2022 systematic review and meta-analysis, also cited by the guidelines, found that MHT was associated with improved self-reported sleep quality. Once again, transdermal therapies showed some of the largest effects.

The ACOG recommends initiating MHT before age 60 or within 10 years of the onset of natural menopause..

It also suggests that the decision to start MHT for VMS and vasomotor-related sleep disturbances should be based on a shared decision making discussion about an individual’s symptoms and the benefits and risks of therapy. Consideration should also be taken regarding an individual’s:

Research shows that factors such as race, culture, socioeconomic status, gender identity, and sexual orientation can influence how individuals experience menopause and the care they receive.

According to the SWAN study, Black women reported more menopause symptoms than white women but were less likely to be treated for them.

“This updated document shines a light on these inequities. Clinicians can proactively help right now by asking every patient in midlife about symptoms, rather than waiting for them to bring it up. Long term, we hope this document drives more research funding to understand these gaps and begin closing them,” said Pier.

The ACOG stated in the guidelines that, “The available evidence is insufficient to recommend for or against the use of estrogen–progestogen or estrogen-only MHT for the treatment of menopause-related depressive symptoms.”

It recommends that perimenopausal and postmenopausal individuals who meet the diagnostic criteria for major depressive disorder (MDD) or those whose most noticeable symptom is depression should be treated according to depression management guidelines.

Current treatment guidelines for depression include therapy, medication, or a combination of the two.

Psychological treatments for depression include psychotherapy, such as cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), while antidepressants are among the most commonly prescribed medications.

The ACOG strongly recommends the use of selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) and serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) for the management of menopausal symptoms like VMS and depression in individuals who don’t want to use MHT or can’t use it.

Currently, paroxetine is the only antidepressant that is approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) for the management of VMS.

The ACOG also strongly recommends the use of neurokinin receptor antagonists to manage VMS and vasomotor-related sleep disturbances in people who don’t desire hormone therapy or can’t use it.

There are currently two neurokinin receptor antagonists approved by the FDA to manage menopause symptoms. Fezolinetant (Veozah) was approved in 2023 to help manage hot flashes. In 2025, the FDA approved elinzanetant (LYNKUET) for the management of moderate to severe VMS due to menopause.

Another nonhormonal treatment for VMS that the ACOG conditionally recommends is gabapentin.

There are various nonpharmacologic treatments that the ACOG conditionally recommends for the management of VMS, vasomotor-related sleep disturbances, and mood changes. These are treatments that don’t involve the use of medications.

The nonpharmacologic treatments conditionally recommended include:

It is generally recommended that these interventions are used alongside hormonal or nonhormonal medications.

The ACOG noted that there is not enough evidence to support the efficacy or safety of phytoestrogens, such as soy isoflavones, or herbal supplements, which are not regulated by the FDA in the same way medications are for safety, purity, or efficacy.

“Menopause is finally getting the attention it deserves, and this document shows what evidence-based care should look like in this space. It lays out a range of safe, effective options for managing menopause symptoms. My message to anyone struggling with these symptoms is simple: talk to your clinician. You don’t have to suffer through them,” said Pier.

#4603. 30 Years of Legal Weed: Has It Made Us Any Healthier?

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The very highs and bizarre lows of America on medicinal marijuana.

EARLIER THIS YEAR, during a brief and mercifully abandoned period when I was considering doing stand-up as a hobby, I went to a comedy show in downtown Austin. I was legitimately shocked to find, as part of the two-drink minimum bill of fare, the option for several different flavors of $5 THC seltzers. This wasn’t “take a pull off your vape or smoke a roach in the alley before the show” territory. No, marijuana drinks were on the menu.

In the past few years, my only social activities have been poker, competitive trivia, and going to the movies, all activities people do stoned, but not activities where you can literally order drugs from the bar alongside your chicken wings. The comedy club, on the other hand, considered marijuana products as a profit center.

This was one of those lightly attended weeknight shows where the comedians banter with the audience because they haven’t prepared much material. So, during a Q&A, I brought up the fact that there were THC sodas available to order, in laminate. “Sir, this is 2026,” the host said.

This is where we are now, a full three decades after California legalized medical weed. But is that good for us?

As I write this, it’s been eight-and-a-half years since I quit marijuana, a drug that at one point made pretty much anything—listening to music, shopping for groceries, cleaning the house—fun for me.

But really, I’ve been sober since I stopped using THC, since I was hardly just smoking there at the end. I was vaping, eating, drinking, dabbing, and otherwise gorging myself on what has become an extremely potent, psychosis-inducing drug. And I wasn’t having fun.

My breaking point, which I recounted in my 2020 memoir Pothead, came at Game 2 of the 2017 World Series at Dodger Stadium, where I was so wasted on weed that I had a screaming meltdown in front of security when I found out the ticket I’d bought was fraudulent.

It was a low point, a bottom so deep and so pathetic that I should have quit right there. But I went on for another three weeks before I finally crawled to a Marijuana Anonymous meeting and asked for help.

Now, nearly a decade later, I’ve emerged from my cocoon to find that THC is everywhere. I live in a state where it’s still technically illegal, but you can find some sort of THC variant in most convenience stores and in most bars and nightclubs.

My friend is an attorney who represents the Texas Alcoholic Beverage Commission. He says his client is in a near panic. Sales are down for every beverage except for nonalcoholic mixers and those THC seltzers. THC is the beer, wine, and vodka of the present day, combined.

And so we find ourselves in a bizarre situation where people are drinking way less alcohol because it’s unhealthy for you, but they are instead ingesting THC products—either through soda, gummies, or any number of other concoctions—from unknown manufacturers, with unknown formulas and malleable dosages.

They’re also vaping, which emerging research is showing is kind of awful for our lungs. A University of Michigan study recently showed that teens who vaped cannabis were twice as likely to experience respiratory symptoms like “wheezing or whistling” in the chest than those who vaped nicotine or smoked traditional cigarettes.

Unlike traditional marijuana, this stuff isn’t “from the earth.” Who knows where it’s really from—or where it’s going?

When I quit weed in 2017, and somewhat loudly and publicly announced myself as an addict, I got the requisite amount of sympathy, but also a tremendous amount of pushback. Marijuana isn’t dangerous, people said. It isn’t addictive. It’s actually good for you.

In 2016, Gavin Newsom, then California’s lieutenant governor, spearheaded the legalization push for recreational marijuana. Cannabis sales have produced more than $8 billion in tax revenue for California since retail sales began in 2018, with a decent proportion of that funneled into teen substance-abuse prevention programs. But the state has also seen an increase in children suffering from cannabis poisoning.

It’s been a massive shift, moving from medically administered weed for veterans and cancer patients to powerful THC beverages you can buy at your bodega.

Legalization has created a new frontier of intoxicants without a centralized warning system for users. Data from a 2025 national study of U.S. adults stated that 15 percent of respondents reported daily or near-daily cannabis use.

Among those daily users, 18 percent reported experiencing symptoms consistent with cannabinoid hyperemesis syndrome, a disorder characterized by cyclic, severe nausea, intractable vomiting, and gut pain alleviated temporarily by hot baths or showers.

This translates to an estimated 7.2 million people in the United States alone, many of them teenagers, experiencing these symptoms. That number is probably vastly underestimated, given that fewer than 12 percent of sufferers have received a formal diagnosis.

Welcome to the darker side of legalized weed, a world of cannabis-induced psychosis, “scromiting” (yes, screaming while vomiting), and concoctions like “shatter” or “wax,” which contain up to 90 percent THC, as opposed to the 3 to 5 percent in joints from previous decades.

So I find myself in a somewhat odd situation. I was a relatively early victim of modern weed’s potency, as well as a relatively early unadopter, and a very early voice in the wilderness warning the world that marijuana might not be the answer to all our health concerns. But now I find myself, like a patient waking up from a long coma, in a world where THC is as prevalent as, and cheaper than, whiskey. Yet still, I find myself thinking it should be legal. So which is it? Maybe we still don’t have enough data.

Thirty years since the start of legalization might feel like a long time, but it’s really not. In terms of scientific analysis, we’re still in relatively undiscovered country. If you’re suffering from chronic pain, or dying of cancer, marijuana is a healthier option than, say, oxycodone. But for the rest of us? No one really knows.

The only thing I can be truly sure of is that it’s no longer for me.

#4602. Tested: 2027 Lexus NX 350h Is Nimble But Not Exciting

Mild updates improve the NX enough to conceivably make it the best-selling Lexus model.

This is the not-all-new but pretty-newish 2027 Lexus NX 350h. It hasn’t grown or shrunk. And it has the same structure as before, carried over with minimal tweaks. But it looks cleaner, has improved tech, and is now available either as a hybrid or a plug-in hybrid. There isn’t a pure combustion version.

Call this the third-generation NX crossover. Or the second and a half. The NX has been Lexus’s second-best-selling model just behind the larger RX crossover, and this Poppin’ Fresh update could be enough to propel its sales beyond those of its larger sibling.

The NX is, like so many Toyota products, built on the bones of the TNGA component set. Precisely, it’s the same TNGA-K platform as the Toyota RAV4’s, with which it shares a production line at the Toyota Motor Manufacturing Canada plant in Cambridge, Ontario.

All NX 350h models are powered by the same drivetrain found in the new ES 350h sedan, a 2.5-liter inline-four with both port and direct fuel injection driving an unsurprising continuously variable automatic transmission. On its own the four-pot makes 186 hp, and when combined with the front 160-hp electric motor it nets out at a system peak of 244 hp. All-wheel-drive versions also get a rear-mounted 54-hp electric motor, but the peak output remains the same. The hybrid battery can carry a modest 1.1 kWh of charge.

Our expert editors test every vehicle we review. Read more about how we test and review cars here.

EPA MPG (City/Highway/Combined): 42/34/38

Lexus has tuned the new hybrid system so well it was hard to tell that it’s all fed through a continuously variable automatic transmission. And that’s a mighty compliment right there. The new system leans into the electric motors earlier than previous iterations, and that smooths out torque delivery noticeably. It also means the four-cylinder engine is almost never climbing up past its torque peak, calmly adding its effort to the whirring electric motors. It’s very civilized, and not that inspiring.

With the strain taken out of the internal-combustion engine, the new NX is quieter than before. And with the mechanical activity tamped down so effectively, other sounds are more apparent. Tire noise from the rear was irritating on some of the diverse pavement around Elora, Ontario, where the press event was held. While 18-inch wheels are standard on the Premium and Premium+ NX 350h models, the F-Sport and Luxury models run 20s. I spent virtually all my time in a Luxury-grade NX.

Keeping the same 105.9-inch wheelbase as the previous NX, this new one stretches out 184.6 inches long overall. That’s 1.1 inches longer than before, and all of that is forward of the front wheels. Yeah, it’s a got a long schnoz. And it still has a monstrous maw of a grille. This NX is also close in size to the original 1998 RX 300 which had a 103.1-inch wheelbase and was 180.1 inches long. It’s 29 years later, but in historical terms, this NX isn’t small.

But the comparison I came away from my drive wasn’t with another SUV but a sedan. The NX drives an awful lot like the new ES with which it shares so much of its mechanical substance and hybrid system. The ES’s wheelbase is more than 10 inches longer, making its ride smoother and more composed than the respectably smooth, composed NX. But the crossover’s shorter wheelbase meant it felt nimbler when asked to change direction and also around the parking lots of Canadian Starbucks outlets.

Untested are the mileage claims. Lexus reports EPA ratings of 44 mpg in the city and 40 mpg on the highway for front-drivers. All-wheel-drive versions are at 42 mpg city and 38 mpg highway, with the F-Sport model one mpg behind that in the city cycle. With a 14.5-gallon fuel tank, total range should approach 600 miles when the right circumstances arise.

This isn’t an expansive interior. The front seats are wonderfully shaped, but there’s not a lot of elbowroom or hiproom. The rear legroom is adequate but not generous. Cargo space is modest. Some buyers will find this all wonderfully intimate. Others will be looking at the RX and thinking that a couple thousand more bucks will be worth it.

It’s the interior driving environment that makes the NX such a pleasant on-road companion. Oversize screens are necessities in this market segment, and the NX has a 12.3-inch display in front of the driver and a well-proportioned 14.0-incher alongside it at the dash center. Neither screen is so large that it overwhelms the cockpit with aggressive graphics or retina-scorching brightness. And the tech interface has evolved for the better.

The NX’s interior is designed to be oh-so soothing, and Lexus has gone minimalist with many controls. There’s a barrel-like knob for the brilliant Mark Levinson–branded sound system, but the ventilation is managed by indistinct capacitive switches that are easy to miss without taking the driver’s eyes off the road. Also, while there’s a wireless phone-charging pad, the USB-C outlets look as if they were stuck below the dash as an afterthought.

The NX is designed with an obsessive focus on everyday usability as an upper-middle-class, suburban driving tool. It’s a well-balanced machine that sacrifices some ultimate utility in favor of nimbleness and cozy confines. Lexus has a lot of other crossovers and SUVs built around different balances for buyers who prefer different compromises.

There’s nothing radical about the new NX and a lot that is very, very familiar. But it’s a precision-crafted sales missile. For many buyers looking at this and the RX next to each other on the polished floor of a sparkling showroom, it’s the more logical and better size choice, before factoring in any consideration of money.

Pricing hasn’t been announced, but it’s unlikely there will be a vast increase from the 2026 edition. The Premium model should start around $49,000 after destination is included, with the Luxury at around $55,000.

The NX’s close relationship with the Toyota RAV4 means the same mechanical package is also available with a nonluxury price tag, but the NX feels special enough that many buyers will be entirely happy to pay extra.

Lexus calls it the Sensory Concierge, which is intended to “provide all occupants with a space as relaxing as their own living room.” It’s a kind of new-age zone-out that adds a performative edge to the climate controls, seat temperatures, and audio system for a relaxing time while parked—and then throws in a fragrance component to be extra silly. It can be tuned to particular moods. There’s Inspire, Radiance, and Revitalize. Not available: Rage, Calamitous Self Doubt, or Knockout Punch.

#4601. Earth’s first fully-marine amphibian stalked the seafloor with a chunky, bony body

Published Oct 26, 2026 12:00 PM EDT

Nearly 237 million years ago, a spiky and toothy bottom dweller skulked the Late Triassic oceans that once covered present-day China. What sets this newly discovered giant salamander-sized prehistoric amphibian apart is not its impressive body–it’s that it lived in the ocean. The newly described Gerrothorax maritimus is the first example of a fully marine amphibian in the fossil record, according to a study published today in the journal Nature Ecology & Evolution.

There are currently over 8,000 species of living amphibians, and none have been known to live in fully marine (salt water) environments. Less than two percent like crab-eating frogs (Fejervarya cancrivora) and some spotted salamandars have any degree of salt tolerance. Other four-limbed animals, including birds, reptiles, and mammals, all have species who can survive without any food or water from land. There was also no evidence of a fully marine amphibian in the fossil record. Until now.

In this study, a team from the University of California, Davis in the United States and Peking University, and the Geological Museum of Guizhou in China analyzed two nearly complete skeletons found in southern China’s Guizhou Province. The fossils were found in an area once covered by ocean and hundreds of miles from any large land mass.

“This area is part of an ecosystem that we’ve been looking at and trying to reconstruct,” Ryosuke Motani, a study co-author and paleontologist at UC Davis, said in a statement. “We’ve been mostly studying marine reptiles and the fish from this place, but now we also have this amphibian and it’s a sort of very unusual member of the lineage.”

The well-preserved skeletons helped the team reconstruct what Gerrothorax maritimus looked like and offer clues to its marine life. Notably, it had a flattened body covered in bony armor. This armor likely defended it against predators like the 10-foot-long ichthyosaur that stalked these seas millions of years ago. Gerrothorax maritimus itself was roughly three feet long with a disc-shaped body.

“If you think about today’s amphibians, this animal is unusual because it’s big,” Motani said. “It’s probably, body mass-wise, about the same as the giant salamander, but what’s different is that giant salamanders are elongated whereas this animal is really chunky.”

Based on Gerrothorax maritimus’  bony armor and the shape of its skull, the team believes that it was a bottom-dwelling suction feeder, similar to the wobbegong, a family of 12 bottom-dwelling carpet shark species alive today.

“They would probably just wait at the bottom and then when prey comes around, they would just suck it in,” said Motanti.

Modern amphibians can’t live in marine environments because their skin does not tolerate salt. The skin is permeable, and allows them to use it for multiple purposes like respiration. Amphibians don’t drink or breathe through the same mouth opening since they can do both just using their skin.

Due to this salt-intolerant biology, they must live in or near freshwater. While some modern species like the crab-eating frog have some degree of salt tolerance, they will lose weight in brackish waters and must recover in freshwater.

The researchers propose that bony armor and a more fish-like body helped Gerrothorax maritimus circumvent this limitation. The armor would have kept its skin from absorbing water, meaning that it needed to drink salt water with its mouth, and then absorb it through its digestive tract. The creature’s gills would have allowed it to filter out the salt. Other species in the Gerrothorax also have gills.

According to the team, the fossils’ location within deep sea sediments and the fact that they were nearly complete led them to believe that Gerrothorax maritimus was an ocean-dweller.

“Carcasses at sea, unless they sink to the bottom right away, tend to dry out and lose limbs prior to sinking to the bottom,” said Motani. “But these animals had their fingers preserved, so that means they probably died near where they lived.”

#4600. Jagex cancels RuneScape Reignited less than two weeks after it was announced, due to "a ton" of player feedback

RuneScape: Reignited, a shiny new version of the long-running British MMORPG, has been cancelled less than two weeks after it was revealed.

RuneScape Reignited - a major reveal during RuneFest earlier this month - was to be a game mode which would allow players to start fresh characters with a separate player-economy from regular RuneScape accounts, meaning they could only buy and sell items between themselves. Reignited accounts would also be prohibited from tackling bosses with regular RuneScape accounts. It was an attempt to court new players to the game. However, it was immediately met with fierce feedback from long-time RuneScape players.

Jagex announced the cancellation of RuneScape: Reignited in both a blog post and YouTube video, in which associate creative director Mod Ryan explained why the sudden U-turn had occurred: "We received a ton of different perspectives from all of you, and we truly appreciate everyone sharing their views.

"It is super important to us because the last time we didn't listen to player feedback, combat changed forever. And it has taken us over a decade to recover from that. Now I said on stage that we would not repeat the same mistakes of the past, and I truly stand by that. Considering this, we have made the decision to cancel Reignited."

The question on the mind of the RuneScape layperson is: why was the feedback so strong against Reignited? The core reasons come down to splitting the playerbase, and Reignited's potential to leave loyal players stranded.

Because of Reignited characters' totally separate player economies to pre-existing RuneScape accounts, and given their fresh-start nature, they would have been the obvious avenue for newcomers to the MMO. As such, the community would have been split in two, and non-Reignited accounts would have been starved of new blood that could keep it healthy and active in the months and years to come.

There was no option to transition an old RuneScape account to a Reignited one, either, so for veteran players who wanted to hop into this new exciting version of RuneScape, they'd have to throw away potentially years of progress and memories to do so, leaving their old accounts to wilt. We're talking thousands of hours and heaps of cash here.

However it's also worth noting Jagex did do this for a reason. The in-game economy of RuneScape has been hyper-inflated for a while now, making the process of creating a new character and progressing through to the endgame a daunting progress due to gargantuan prices. For example, a Bond (exchangeable for a month of premium membership) sells for 11.2m coins on Old School RuneScape. On regular RuneScape, a bond sells for 141.3m coins.

Reignited accounts would have - for all their faults - solved this problem, providing an easy on-ramp for those curious to check the game out. With Reignited's cancellation this problem still persists, leaving Jagex with the same hurdle it had in front of it a month ago, just with an extra bruise or two.

Thankfully for Jagex, RuneScape: Reignited was not the only reveal from RuneFest, and as Ryan went on to mention, RuneScape is getting an Adventure Guide update and other additions in December, and a free trial. In perhaps more exciting news, RuneScape 4, an entirely new MMO, was also revealed at the show.

#4596. Audi Will Invest $4 Billion into Its First U.S. Factory in Georgia

The factory will produce SUVs designed for the U.S. market, likely including the Q7, Q8, and Q9.

The Trump administration’s tariffs sent automakers scrambling in 2025, with profits plunging as many companies opted to eat the costs of the import taxes rather than risk alienating customers with higher prices. But that approach couldn’t continue forever, and automakers began plotting how to better shield themselves from future trade wars. To that end, Audi has now announced that it will build its first U.S. factory in Georgia, with production at the new site expected to start in the early 2030s.

A report first emerged in June 2025 that Audi was considering a new production facility in the United States, although the company declined to comment at the time. Now we know that Audi’s first U.S. factory will be in Macon, Georgia, with construction set to begin in 2027.

See all results for used Audi for sale near 100000

Audi will invest around $4 billion in the new factory and says that the project will create roughly 4500 new jobs. The new facility will focus on building SUVs designed for the North American market, with a planned annual capacity of up to 200,000 vehicles. While most of the vehicles will likely be destined for sale in North America, some SUVs are also expected to be exported.

Production is still quite a ways away, but we wouldn’t be surprised to see future iterations of the three-row Q7 and the even-larger Q9 shift from the Bratislava, Slovakia, plant to Georgia. Reuters reports that a U.S. official said the production plans will include the Q7, Q8, and Q9 SUVs. The official also reportedly stated that Audi plans to build 25,000 vehicles per year at the new $2 billion plant that’s being built in Blythewood, South Carolina, for new VW Group subsidiary Scout Motors, although this has not been publicly confirmed by Audi.

Audi says the new factory is a key part of its long-term growth strategy, given that the U.S. is the world’s second-largest premium market. It will also insulate the brand against future tariffs, since Audi currently imports all of its U.S. models from Europe or Mexico. Tariffs cost the VW Group $4 billion in 2025, with $1.4 billion of that loss coming from Audi. While the factory won’t be able to ease that burden in the near term, it will provide insurance against future trade wars. According to Automotive News, a U.S. official also said the Trump administration will hold talks with VW over some form of tariff relief in exchange for the commitment to building a new factory in the U.S.
