#4595. Christina Applegate ‘Not Happy at All’ After Learning She Was Misdiagnosed With MS

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Christina Applegate shared an unexpected health update on her return to the MeSsy podcast with co-host Jamie-Lynn Sigler.

The two actors have detailed their experiences with multiple sclerosis (MS), a neurological disorder that can lead to debilitating symptoms affecting daily functioning and quality of life.

Applegate, 54, has been raising awareness about the condition since her initial diagnosis in 2021.

In two podcast episodes released October 20, Applegate revealed that doctors had told her she was misdiagnosed with MS.

“She came into my room and said, ‘Congratulations, you don’t have MS,’ ” Applegate said as she fought back tears. “I was, like, not happy at all. Everybody else was so happy.”

The “Anchorman” and “Dead to Me” actor explained that her symptoms have continued to worsen, leaving her without an explanation for what she’s experiencing as doctors investigate the cause.

Being misdiagnosed with MS is relatively common. Applegate’s revelation highlights a difficult reality for people living with unexplained symptoms resembling MS. Her experience raises questions about how MS is diagnosed, why misdiagnoses may occur, and what people can do when their symptoms don’t match their diagnosis.

While MS can be difficult to diagnose, an accurate diagnosis can help people with the condition gain access to appropriate treatment. A misdiagnosis, on the other hand, may lead to unnecessary interventions and medication, and take an emotional toll over time.

“MS can be challenging to diagnose because there is no single test that confirms it in every case,” said Steve Allder, MD, consultant neurologist at Re:Cognition Health.

“An incorrect diagnosis can have significant consequences. Someone incorrectly diagnosed with MS may experience considerable anxiety, undergo unnecessary investigations, or receive treatments with potential risks and side effects. Equally, if MS is missed, treatment may be delayed, potentially allowing further neurological damage,” Allder told Healthline.

A 2024 systematic review and meta-analysis underscores that MS misdiagnoses remain a concern.

Research suggests that around 15% of people diagnosed with MS may actually have another condition.

However, estimates vary across studies, with some reporting rates ranging from 5–41%.

While an accurate diagnosis can guide patients toward appropriate treatment, a mistaken one may lead to unnecessary medication, emotional distress, and years of uncertainty.

New diagnostic guidelines offer hope for greater accuracy, but they also raise important questions about how doctors can adequately distinguish MS from other conditions.

“Research has shown that MS can be both overdiagnosed and underdiagnosed,” Allder said.

“Diagnosis relies on a combination of symptoms, neurological examination, MRI findings, and sometimes, additional tests. It is also important to recognize that diagnoses can evolve as new evidence emerges. As neurologists, we must remain open to revisiting a diagnosis when symptoms or test results do not fit, ensuring patients receive appropriate treatment and support.”

MS symptoms may vary widely and overlap with several other conditions. These may include:

Because these conditions share certain features with MS, healthcare professionals should consider a patient’s full clinical profile rather than relying on a single symptom or scan.

“MS can cause numbness, tingling, muscle weakness, visual disturbances, balance problems, and fatigue. However, these symptoms are not unique to the condition,” Allder said.

“For example, numbness and tingling may indicate peripheral neuropathy, sometimes associated with diabetes or vitamin B12 deficiency. Weakness or altered sensation may result from a trapped nerve or spinal cord compression. Migraines can cause visual disturbances and sensory symptoms, while inner-ear disorders may affect balance,” he continued.

Allder noted that fatigue is particularly nonspecific and may be associated with:

He explained that, as a neurologist, he considers the pattern, duration, and progression of symptoms alongside examination findings.

“No single symptom automatically indicates MS. A thorough assessment is essential to identify the underlying cause and ensure the patient receives appropriate treatment,” he said.

There is no single test that definitively confirms every case of MS.

Diagnosis typically involves reviewing symptoms, conducting neurological examinations, and evaluating MRI scans and laboratory results.

MRI findings may be tricky to interpret. Some white-matter spots resemble MS lesions but may result from migraine, aging, or other conditions. Misapplying diagnostic criteria or overlooking alternative explanations can also lead to mistakes.

“MRI abnormalities alone do not confirm MS,” Allder said. “The diagnosis must be based on the overall clinical picture, with alternative explanations carefully considered.”

Research also suggests that MS misdiagnosis rates may differ by sex.

Research shows females are 2.1 times more likely to be misdiagnosed with MS than males, highlighting the potential for diagnostic bias and differences in how symptoms are evaluated.

The updated MS diagnostic criteria published in 2025 offer a framework for improving diagnosis.

The updated guidelines introduce several important changes.

For instance, the optic nerve (the nerve connecting the eye to the brain) is now included as a fifth anatomical location for assessing evidence of MS.

The revised criteria also include new MRI biomarkers, such as the central vein sign and paramagnetic rim lesions. These features can help doctors distinguish MS-related abnormalities from other conditions.

These changes may help doctors diagnose MS earlier and more accurately.

If you experience persistent or recurring neurological symptoms, seek medical advice. Your doctor can assess your symptoms and, if applicable, refer you to a neurologist.

Sudden weakness, vision loss, or difficulty speaking requires urgent medical assessment, Allder said.

“Depending on the findings, investigations may include MRI scans of the brain and spinal cord, blood tests, and, in some cases, a lumbar puncture,” Allder noted.

“It is important that results are interpreted alongside symptoms and examination findings, as no single test should determine the diagnosis in isolation.”

Allder said that if you’re unsure about a diagnosis, you can ask your neurologist what evidence supports it and whether alternative health conditions have been considered.

“A second opinion or follow-up assessment may be appropriate. The priority is an accurate diagnosis that leads to the right treatment and support,” Allder said.

#4594. Weed Addiction Is a Real Thing. The World's Leading Cannabis Researcher Explains Why.

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

KEVIN HILL, MD, the guy when it comes to this stuff. He’s the chief of the Division of Addiction Psychiatry at Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center. We talked to him about all the questions you probably have about weed and addiction.

Dr. Hill: Absolutely. Most people who use cannabis don’t develop a cannabis use disorder or cannabis addiction. But there has always been a subset of individuals who do. Five years ago, the data indicated about 10 to 30 percent of adults develop a cannabis use disorder. Now the published data says 30 to 35 percent. With the rise in potency, we’re seeing a rise in the number of users who are developing problems.

MH: Does cannabis use disorder differ from other addictions?

Dr. Hill: It affects a lot of the same neurotransmitters that other substances of abuse might affect. If you use cannabis, you’re going to have a surge in dopamine. Similarly, if you use opioids, you’re going to have a surge in dopamine, but the scale of it is different. It’s why a lot of medications we use to treat addictions operate on common pathways.

MH: Is there a genetic risk of developing a weed addiction?

Dr. Hill: Yes. The genetic aspects of cannabis use disorder are probably less well developed than for other addictions, but if people have a family history of addiction of any kind, then it makes them more likely to have another substance use disorder, which could be cannabis.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

#4593. Man Steals Mazda From Dealership, Tries to Sell It to Another Dealer for Less Than Half Its Value: Police

The suspect allegedly asked for just $15,000 for a car worth $35,000, but before jumping at the bargain, the dealer checked the VIN.

Nobody expects to get top dollar for their used car when they trade it in at a dealership. After all, dealers want to maximize their profits, so they're generally going to offer the lowest amount they think a customer will accept. But one man in Oregon recently offered a deal so good, even the dealer didn't want to take it.

According to the Eugene Police Department, on October 21, 31-year-old Lakhwinder Singh Gangar offered a Eugene, Oregon car dealership his 2025 Mazda sport-utility vehicle (the specific model wasn’t specified) for $15,000. There were two problems with that offer. The first problem was that the dealer estimated the Mazda was worth about $35,000. The second problem, cops say, was that the car didn’t belong to Gangar.

To the dealer’s credit, they didn’t immediately jump on the unusually low offer. Authorities say they first ran the VIN, and discovered the car was registered to another dealer about an hour away in Salem, Oregon; a call was placed to the Salem dealer, who confirmed the car had been stolen. So instead of making a deal with Gangar, the Eugene dealer called the police on him.

Police arrived at the dealership on Highway 99 to investigate. According to authorities, Gangar apparently decided he wasn't interested in sticking around to negotiate on the Mazda deal any longer once the police showed up; he left the dealership on foot, but officers located and detained him shortly afterward.

Unfortunately for Gangar, the allegedly stolen Mazda apparently wasn't the only thing that landed him in trouble. Police reported finding a baggie containing a white crystalline substance and a pipe in his shirt pocket. The substance field-tested presumptive positive for methamphetamine.

Officers also discovered a disassembled key fob in Gangar's possession, along with additional pieces of the fob inside the Mazda. Police didn't explain how the car was originally stolen, or why the key fob had been taken apart.

So, to recap: Gangar allegedly tried to sell a Mazda that was stolen from a dealership, to another dealership just an hour away, for less than half of market value, all allegedly with a pocket full of meth. Unsurprisingly at this point, Gangar was arrested and transported to the Lane County Jail on charges of possession of a stolen vehicle and unlawful possession of methamphetamine.

#4592. The 2026 Mama Bear Award goes to a black bear with a mullet

Published Oct 26, 2026 1:08 PM EDT

While Fat Bear Week has come and gone, there is another group of Alaskan bears currently getting their due. The United States Forest Service team at Tongass National Forest are doling out the Anan Bear Awards to the 39 bears spotted at the Anan Wildlife Observatory in southeastern Alaska.

Tongass is the nation’s largest national forest and covers most of Southeast Alaska, including the capital city of Juneau. It is home to Mendanhall Glacier (a popular destination for Alaskan cruises), numerous hiking trails, eagles, and plenty of bears. One of the hotspots is Anan Creek, which is home to one of the largest runs of pink salmon in Southeast Alaska.

According to Friends of Anan, there are 39 known bears that feast on the salmon at Anan Creek. Thirty-one are black bears (Ursus americanus) and eight are brown bears (Ursus arctos). Like their counterparts further to the north in Katmai National Park, the bears return here every summer to eat salmon and teach their young how to fish and survive. And also like the bears of Katmai, every bear here has their own habits.

The U.S. Forest Service tracks and documents every bear and explore.org’s cameras broadcast live streams of their antics.

Osso! The adult female black bear was first spotted in 2022 and has a white chest patch shaped like the letter I. She usually has a mullet of hair on the top of her head at the end of the summer season. Her name is spelled either Osso (Italian for bone), because her chest patch also resembles a bone or Oso (Spanish for bear). She had one cub in 2019, two in 2024, and three this year.

Mother bears show fearless aggression when sensing any danger to their young, and the Forest Service says Osso is a prime example of this tenacity.

“Feeding and watching out for three cubs was more than a full-time job, especially with so many other bears about,” the team writes. “Scuba Sue, a female brown bear, got too close for comfort on this occasion and Osso didn’t hesitate before sending her cubs up a tree and taking a swipe at Sue. Not all heroes wear capes, some pop their jaws and raise their claws.”

Unfortunately, Osso was only seen with two cubs in August. While it is not clear what happened to her third cub, the Forest Service assumes she did her best to protect the cubs.

#4591. Endless pile of Needle In Haystack games begins to emerge, as viral hit imitator's devs claim their code was nicked by a genAI-using clone

In a metaphor far too on the nose to turn into any form of fiction, at least without the writer getting mercilessly cyberbullied, a game about finding a needle in a haystack has now become the genuine needle at the heart of a pile of imitators. A pile so large, in fact, one copycat game stands accused of ripping code from another.

NoGlyph, developers of Needle In A Haystack, have claimed that their code's been nicked by A Game About Needle In A Haystack devs Little Field Studios. The former game was released on September 29th this year. The latter came out on October 16th. Both offer players a chance to go hunting for metal implements in piles of horse food, and both appear to have been inspired by the as-yet unreleased Needle In A Haystack Simulator, which gained notoriety after serving as the basis of a viral tweet in August.

These are just three of the needle-in-haystack games which've massed on Steam since that clip got a bunch of eyeballs, as various people look to fill the world's apparent desire to hunt stiching implements on a farm.

"Yesterday, on October 16th a game called 'A Game About Needle In A Haystack' by Little Field Studios came out on Steam," a Needle In A Haystack dev called Jahvin wrote in a Steam post over the weekend. "It contains code copied from our game. To be clear, this is about our actual code, not a similar idea or similar mechanics.

"The haystack in our game, meaning how it's drawn, how you dig into it and how gold, lapis and diamond hay work, is something that took a long time to get working properly. When we looked through their game's files, we found 13 of our classes inside it with the exact same names. More than half of the functions in that part match ours exactly. Some of our own error messages are still in there word for word, including one that points to a shader from our project that doesn't exist in their game.

"The copied code matches the versions of our free demo we put out on September 20 and 21, a few weeks before their release. From what we can tell, the rest of their game (the art, the sounds, the other systems) is their own work. Our problem is the hay code."

So, not an apparent copycat calling out another apparent copycat solely for its premise, but an accusation that things have evolved to the point of a studio directly ripping specific code and assets. The Needle In A Haystack devs added that they've filed a copyright notice with Valve and saved off a copy of their findings as proof.

A Game About Needle In A Haystack, the accused, boasts a genAI disclosure in its Steam description, but this only makes clear that the tech was used to produce the game's logo. If the hay code situation is as Jahvin described, that could suggest the potential for AI-assisted decompilation to have been at play in the alleged copying, but there doesn't seem to be any definite confirmation of that as of writing.

Dev concerns about AI being used to easily replicate games have been on the rise, following the recent boom in genAI-assisted decompilation. Just last week, the makers of a game called Drunken Goddess Reflux announced they'd cancelled plans for a public demo in favour of limited-access playtesting sessions, citing concern that "the demo's content could be used to quickly imitate or replicate the game's presentation and gameplay with AI or other tools, and that such works could be released before our launch".

Honestly, I'm just terrified that the ever-growing mound of needle-in-haystack variants will eventually grow to the point of causing Steam's vast barn of games to burst, sending horse-shaped westerns, pitchfork-shaped fantasy RPGs, and water trough-shaped games aimed at thirsty horndogs flying across the plains of the net.

#4590. Heartopia finally gets a release date... on everything but Steam

Heartopia may very well be the first big game you play in 2026.

Heartopia developer XD Games has delivered the news anyone who tried out the life sim game had been waiting for. The Animal Crossing-like cozy multiplayer game will finally arrive on January 7 across Android, iOS, and PC - with one caveat.

The Steam version won’t actually launch on that date, and will instead go live at some other point in the future. You will be able to play it on PC through TapTap, which is the same emulator you may be familiar with from beta.

Pre-registration is open on the official website for all platforms. If you throw in your email address, you’ll receive a bundle of in-game items at launch. You’ll get 4,000 Gold, 20 Wishing Stars, 400 Moonlight Crystal, and 60 Exhibition Passes.

Interestingly, XD Games isn’t saying what you can expect from the launch build - in terms of content - just yet. Those details will be revealed closer to the January launch, so perhaps expect them in the first few days of the year.

If you created a character in either (or both) of the recent closed beta tests, you’ll also receive the Blueberry Crossbody Bag in-game cosmetic. If you made it to level 18 in either test, you’ll also get the Star Mascot Head cosmetic. Both items will be available to claim at launch.

We’ve spent a lot of time playing the game’s recent tests, and we’ve put together some early guides on how to get Roaming Oak Timber, how to unlock and upgrade hobbies and more. We also have a few cooking recipes to share with you.

#4589. Control Resonant becomes the fastest-selling game in Remedy's history, outpacing Alan Wake 2 and the original Control

The latest game to pass a 1 million copies sold milestone is Control Resonant. Developer Remedy Entertainment broke the news yesterday, roughly three weeks after the game's release, revealing that Resonant is now the fastest-selling game in the Finnish studio's 31-year history.

Remedy didn't share a digital/physical split, nor say which platform led sales, but these are good numbers for a Remedy game, and even more impressive considering the unusually packed September release schedule Resonant released into.

Remedy CEO Jean-Charles Gaudechon, who took over as boss of the company in February, called this a "very strong start". "Control Resonant is Remedy's first self-published release of this scale, launched into one of the most competitive windows in years alongside the industry's biggest names," Gaudechon said.

"We have always believed Control deserved an even bigger audience, and this launch shows what's possible: we see the game connecting with long-time fans while bringing a significant number of new players into the world of Control, which strengthens its long-term potential."

Gaudechon's comments are particularly notable as the executive - who previously led a sports betting platform - recently had to assuage fans who feared his leadership would take the studio in a different direction.

To that end, Gaudechon said Remedy would continue to build on its two "established" franchises in Control and Alan Wake, adding that: "Our focus is to build on what makes them special, support them, and grow them even further. There is a lot more to come from these worlds."

Although we don't have like-for-like sales data for the original Control, the game sold 2 million copies by the end of 2020, 15 months after launch. Alan Wake 2 - previously the fastest-selling Remedy game - by contrast took about two months to reach the 1 million milestone in 2023. Incidentally, Resonant is the lowest-rated of all three. Alexis Ong awarded it three stars in the Eurogamer review, saying it " feels like it's chasing the hallmarks of a live-service game while trying, only partly successfully, to echo the very deliberate structure and style of the genre storytelling that worked so well in the first game".

Control Resonant joins the likes of The Blood of Dawnwalker, Onimusha: Way of the Sword, Star Wars Zero Company, and Ace Combat 8: Wings of Theve as recent releases that have all sold over 1 million copies.

If you're already playing Remedy's latest, or want to do so soon, the Eurogamer Control Resonant walkthrough is a helpful companion that'll make navigating the game's open world and making sense of its many character upgrades a breeze.

#4587. View Exterior Photos of the 2027 Lamborghini Revuelto SV

See the exterior of the 2027 Lamborghini Revuelto SV from every angle.

Read the Full Review | See Interior Photos

The new 2027 Revuelto SV is Lamborghini’s latest V-12–powered supercar to receive the higher-performance Super Veloce treatment.

Enhanced handling prowess, rather than raw horsepower, is the plug-in hybrid SV’s priority.

But it still has a lot of ponies: a total of 1050, to be exact, thanks to a larger battery that complements the carryover 6.5-liter V-12.

A deeper front splitter and a fixed rear wing, among other aero tweaks, help it generate up to 80 percent more downforce than the regular Revuelto.

Suspension upgrades include new manually adjustable passive dampers and revised rear suspension geometry.

Those exhaust cannons pump out a lot of heat but also emit glorious V-12 noise.

Clever electronics, including a new five-stage traction-control system, help make the SV surprisingly easy to drive hard.

Massive, Formula 1–inspired carbon-ceramic brakes add to its capabilities.

Also standard are new Bridgestone Potenza Race R track-oriented tires.

The cost: an estimated $753,000, and only 1963 will be built.

#4586. RFK Jr.’s Revised Vaccine Schedule May Cause 83% Surge in Hepatitis B Infections

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s proposed changes to the childhood vaccine schedule have raised alarm among public health experts and researchers.

Concerns include a rise in preventable infectious diseases such as measles, polio, and hepatitis B.

A new study suggests that delaying or foregoing the hepatitis B shot for newborns could increase chronic infections among children.

In December 2025, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) ended its recommendation for universal hepatitis B vaccination at birth for infants whose mothers test negative for the virus. Instead, the federal health agency recommends individual-based clinical decision making.

After reviewing the evidence, the CDC, which is overseen by the HHS, concluded that newborns “face little risk of contracting hepatitis,” which deemed the vaccine, typically administered at birth, unnecessary.

Experts say this shift in public health policy wasn’t based on science, and researchers warn of rising infection rates.

“Hepatitis B immunizations have been proven to protect individuals against a serious and significant disease,” said Graham Tse, MD, pediatrician and chief medical officer of MemorialCare Miller Children’s & Women’s Hospital in Long Beach, CA.

“The hepatitis B vaccines have several decades of history and have been proven safe and effective,” Tse said. “There has been no new data or evidence presented or uncovered to support delaying the newborn dose of hepatitis B.”

Here’s what parents should know about making informed decisions with their child’s healthcare team.

A U.S. modeling study published in Annals of Internal Medicine on October 20 projected that delaying the first vaccine dose until 2 months could increase chronic hepatitis B infections among infants by 19% under one scenario.

With shared decision making and lower vaccine uptake, projected increases ranged from 83% to 225%, compared with universal birth-dose vaccination.

The researchers also estimated that up to 1.8 million young adults could remain susceptible to hepatitis B by age 20 under shared decision making, compared with 232,000 under universal birth dosing.

Another study published in JAMA Pediatrics in late April projected hundreds of additional chronic infections annually if the hepatitis B birth dose were delayed or omitted.

However, these figures are model-based projections, rather than verified outcomes. Actual results will depend on vaccination rates and other factors.

In December 2025, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) adopted a recommendation shifting away from universal hepatitis B vaccination at birth for infants whose mothers test negative for hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg).

Under the new approach, parents and healthcare professionals can decide together whether to give the first dose at birth or defer it until the baby is 2 months old.

The change followed an 8 to 3 vote by the Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices (ACIP), marking a departure from longstanding U.S. guidance.

However, infants born to mothers who test positive for hepatitis B or whose infection status is unknown should still receive the hepatitis B vaccine and hepatitis B immune globulin (HBIG) within 12 hours of birth, according to the updated guidelines.

Hepatitis B is a viral infection that affects the liver.

Although some infections resolve, newborns and infants face a higher risk of developing lifelong hepatitis B infection compared with adults.

Up to 90% of infants infected during their first year develop chronic hepatitis B.

“We have actual world evidence that vaccinating newborns for hepatitis B prevents hepatitis B infections later in life and the serious complications of that disease,” Tse said.

Over time, chronic infection can lead to cirrhosis, liver failure, or liver cancer.

“The danger of delaying the newborn hepatitis B shot is that individuals do not get vaccinated and thereby do not have protection against hepatitis B,” Tse said.

Early vaccination helps protect young infants before potential exposure occurs, including situations in which maternal screening misses an infection.

“Newborns can be infected with hepatitis B from a mother who is not aware they have a hepatitis B infection or from exposure later in life,” Tse said.

“The risk is developing a hepatitis B infection, which can potentially lead to liver cancer, fulminant hepatitis, or severe end-stage liver disease.”

In contrast to the CDC, the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) still recommends a universal hepatitis B vaccine dose within 24 hours of birth, followed by additional doses during infancy.

The leading pediatric medical organization has raised concerns about the potential consequences of reduced vaccination rates.

The hepatitis B vaccine has decades of safety and effectiveness data.

Tse said the AAP’s vaccine schedule and recommendations are based on decades of study, use, and expert input, and provide the best protection against vaccine-preventable diseases.

Parents who have questions about potential side effects, timing, or their baby’s individual risk should discuss them with a qualified healthcare professional.

“Parents are confused, given the recommendations from CDC, HHS, and Kennedy differ from established and respected groups such as the American Academy of Pediatrics, the Infectious Disease Society of America, and several states,” Tse said.

“Parents desire to keep their children safe, and having differing opinions and confusion will lead to reduced vaccination rates as parents experience doubt as to whom to listen to or believe.”

While the updated CDC policy offers more flexibility for some families, lower vaccination uptake could lead to an increase in chronic hepatitis B infections.

Ask your pediatrician about the benefits of vaccination against hepatitis B at birth, the risks of delaying the first dose, and the recommended schedule for completing the series.

You can also ask how maternal screening results affect your baby’s protection.

An informed conversation with your healthcare professional can help you weigh the evidence and choose a vaccination plan for your baby.

“I see no argument whatsoever for changing the established vaccination schedule for hepatitis B,” Tse said.

“There have been no new studies, no data, nor evidence to justify Kennedy’s proposed changes to the childhood immunization schedule. Delaying vaccinations or skipping vaccinations puts your child at risk for serious and significant diseases, which are all preventable,” Tse said.

#4585. Read censored texts from around the world at this Minecraft library

Published Oct 26, 2026 2:15 PM EDT

High-end graphics, cinematic plotlines, and challenging boss battles aren’t what made Minecraft one of the most popular video games of all time. After nearly 15 years, millions of people still play it every day because it doesn’t offer any of those features. Instead, Minecraft hinges exploring, building, and experimenting inside a virtually infinite digital universe. For many players, logging onto a server provides a sense of freedom that the real world sadly denies them.

It’s not symbolic, either. Minecraft’s digital freedom produces tangible benefits that extend far beyond the game itself. In 2020, the international press advocacy organization Reporters Without Borders (also known as Reporters Sans Frontières, or RSF) announced the Uncensored Library, an online repository filled with books, essays, and articles by individuals routinely silenced by their authoritarian governments. But instead of stashing the collection in an unassuming corner of the game, RSF and the Minecraft design collective BlockWorks built a massive, gorgeous neoclassical library located on a picturesque, pixelated island.

“Freedom of expression is a fundamental human right, and yet, there are countries that seek to censor their citizens or prevent them from accessing certain information,” RSF explais. “This is why The Uncensored Library exists; a grand digital library meant to combat censorship – and it’s all accessible through Minecraft.”

Even six years after its debut, the project regularly goes viral on social media, and receives routine updates from RSF and BlockWorks. There is even a map to guide visitors through the digital institution’s multiple wings that are organized by country. Until recently, the library hosted materials from writers contending with particularly repressive governments across eight nations, including Iran, Saudi Arabia, Russia, and Egypt. Earlier this year, RSF added a section for the United States following the Trump administration’s pushbacks on the press.

“Often, suppressing stories takes a less overt form than direct censorship,” RSF North America executive director Clayton Weimers said at the time, adding that the decision to include the U.S. was intended to highlight that “fully authoritarian regimes do not have a monopoly on free press violations.”

The Uncensored Library is accessible either by downloading the files to your personal computer, or accessing the visit.uncensoredlibrary.com server while playing Minecraft.

#4584. This Is Your Brain on Weed. It’s Actually Kind of Amazing.

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

WHEN YOU USE weed, your brain kind of goes all over the place. You likely know about how marijuana can chill you out, make you forget yourself, and induce the munchies. But there are other fascinating ways that the drug can alter your mind.

When THC binds to receptors in your hippocampus, the compound disrupts signals and impairs memory. That’s why you may forget what you were saying mid-sentence or where you put your keys when stoned.

THC also binds to receptors in the amygdala, which can help you feel chill when high—and paranoid or anxious when you’re too high.

The back of the brain, the cerebellum, is important for balance, motor skills, and time perception. When THC gets involved, you might feel clumsier and as if time is slowing.

THC disrupts the prefrontal cortex, which oversees critical thinking, problem-solving, and processing information from your senses. As a result, you’re going to find it harder to multitask, feel more impulsive, and zone out.

THC also stimulates the release of feel-good dopamine in the nucleus accumbens. If you’re a chronic, heavy user, cannabis can dull dopamine response and potentially rewire circuitry within the nucleus accumbens, making you more susceptible to addiction.

Your prefrontal cortex and limbic system contribute to imaginative ideas. THC can bind to receptors in these regions and may reduce self-critical noise, allowing for greater free expression.

Scientists believe that the anterior cingulate cortex is central to the perception of pain, and THC seems to dampen its ability—in a good way.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

Jocelyn Solis-Moreira, MS is the associate health & fitness for Men's Health and has previously written for CNN, Scientific American, Popular Science, and National Geographic before joining the brand. When she's not working, she's doing circus arts or working towards the perfect pull-up.

#4583. HWA Teases Mercedes-Benz AMG Hammer Redux for Next Project

The Mercedes-Benz W124 E-Class ‘Hammer’ brought AMG into the world’s view, and now HWA looks to continue that car’s story.

The folks at HWA are expanding their restomodding footprint. A new teaser from the German company on Instagram suggests that, on top of their awesome Mercedes-Benz 190E Evo reincarnation, it’s now working on a W124-gen “AMG Hammer”’ recreation.

Created by AMG back in the 1980s, the modified W124 E-Class got its “Hammer” nickname from its brutal acceleration on tap thanks to the big V-8 engine under the hood. This vehicle is seen by many as the defining car for AMG as an independent tuning house, before it officially went into business and became one with Mercedes-Benz. Now, HWA—which was started by one of the original people behind AMG—looks to be readying a reboot.

“Following the HWA Evo, we are revisiting another extraordinary chapter of our engineering heritage,” HWA writes in its Instagram post. “Staying true to its origins while exploring the possibilities of modern technology, a familiar idea is taking on an entirely new form.”

That heritage traces back to AMG co-founder Hans Werner Aufrecht, who is also the founder of HWA—his initials being the origin of the name. The teaser seen here sure looks a whole lot like the original W124 Hammer’s silhouette; we can already see some of the modern touches being applied to the recreation in the form of LED headlights and taillights. HWA did the same for its 190E remake, bridging the gap between the original and modern tech.

The Evo uses a turbocharged V-6 engine, but we suspect any Hammer redux would need to house a V-8 under the hood, much like the original did. HWA doesn’t explicitly say anything about the powertrain, but suggests “more will be revealed” in due time. Road & Track asked HWA for more information, and were told that additional information will be coming in the ensuing weeks. We sure are hopeful to see this next project from HWA take shape.

#4582. Crusader Kings 3's merchant republics expansion, Silk and Silver, will release during the very quiet time of mid-November 2026

Volvy’s Adventure: Reslimed gets a shield-sibling in the war to do a PC thing during THAT week

The second big expansion coming to Crusader Kings 3 this year finally has a proper release date. Silk and Silver, the gold-clinking sibling of recently released Church-centric DLC By God Alone, will arrive on November 17th. Just a couple of days before that which shall not be named, but has resulted in a lot of studios opting to give mid-November a wide berth release-wise.

Part of CK3's fifth chapter of DLC, Silk and Silver does for merchant republics like Venice what By God Alone did for realms ruled by Popes and priests. This time, the route to power is starting a successful trading company and building up your patrician family to the point of being able to rule as a palm-greasing Doge.

You'll be able to influence what areas your republic specialises in, such as naval trade, bread and circuses populism, or establishing a deep class of nobles to send out across Europe in the hopes of currying favour. In terms of laws and customs, there's a constitution you can try to shape to maximise your own ends or deliver good for the people. In terms of foreign policy, it'll be up to you which trade routes to pursue control of and whether you're after a monopoly or are happy to work with other republics for mutual benefit in trade leagues.

Sadly, it isn't set to introduce naval warfare to CK3 despite its maritime trading focus. "We know this is a topic people care about and relate to the republics strongly," Paradox wrote in deep dive about Silk and Silver earlier this year. "but between a whole lot of balancing and a whole lot of new systems that we are adding to the game, we chose not to spend time and energy on trying to pack in another large feature that we would not be able to give it enough love and time to do well."

All in all, I'm not sure the marchant republics will have as much meme potential as playable Popes have for CK3, even in cases that don't see the game glitch out and let you marry three ladies you shouldn't before having divorce them all in a single day. Then again, being a medieval business tycoon could allow for plenty of other hijnks and tomfoolery that'll make the decades roll by in funny fashion. Plus, it's something else to stop us PC folks being starved of new releases as the console lot crack into, well, you know.

#4581. CD Projekt Red says its mysterious Hadar game is its "biggest project to date", but even after "several good years" it's seemingly a long way off

"We're not quite at the pre-production stage yet"

CD Projekt Red has described its mysterious new game, which is currently known by its codename Hadar, as its "biggest project to date", revealing it's taken "several good years" to reach its current state, and it's still not even in pre-production.

That's according to CDPR joint-CEO Michał Nowakowski, who told Polish outlet PAP Biznes (translation by DeepL), "At this point we can say that we have a coherent vision of [Hadar's] world and its key elements. The team has started working on the game, although we're not quite at the pre-production stage yet. We're currently developing small prototypes, and we're also testing gameplay elements."

In the four years since its announcement, CDPR has said little about Hadar. However, we do know it won't be based on any existing property, unlike CD Projekt's other major titles - The Witcher having been created by Polish author Andrzej Sapkowski, and Cyberpunk 2077 by TTRPG maker Mike Pondsmith. (Incidentally, I've talked to both Sapkowski and Pondsmith about their work in this regard.)

Earlier this year, Nowakowski shared "an important development update" regarding Hadar's status. "Work on the project is progressing dynamically, and the team has established the foundations of this entirely new IP," Novakowski said in March. "Now, we have clearly defined distinguishing features of this world, and established key pillars that will not only provide the foundation for the game itself, but also, potentially, for other future products." He also mentioned that prototypes were being made in Unreal Engine.

We know from CDPR's most recent financial report in September that there were 33 people working on Hadar as of 31st July, so it's a small project compared to the studio's other titles. 519 people were working on The Witcher 4 at last count, 184 on Cyberpunk 2, and 84 on multiplayer Witcher game Project Sirius. However, Project Sirius was recently hit by layoffs.

Elsewhere in the interview, Nowakowski spoke more about The Witcher 4, which he called "our most ambitious project to date". He also said the studio wanted to "avoid repeating ourselves", so each game should "introduce new narrative and gameplay solutions" as well as "innovations" in main and side quests. "We want players to be confident that when they sit down to play this title, it pushes the boundaries of what an RPG can be and is technologically advanced," he said. The Witcher 4 was recently given a release target of 2028.

He also referenced the recent Remastered update for The Witcher 3, saying the company was "pleased" with sales, which "remain at a higher level than before the remaster", even now the launch discount period has ended. The Witcher 3 broke its Steam concurrent player records after the Remastered edition was released.

Finally, Nowakowski discussed the impact of Sony's decision to phase out PlayStation disc production starting 2028 - putting a possible Witcher 4 physical release in jeopardy. He reiterated much of what he's said before on the matter, saying that because console manufacturers make the discs, there isn't much CDPR can do if they decide to cease production. "A studio doesn't simply decide to release a game and then distribute the physical version itself," he said. "If a particular manufacturer completely shuts down the option to release games on physical media, they simply won't be available."

#4580. Chris Mottalini and His Son, Nino, Rediscover the Magic of Seeing the World Through a Child’s Eyes

Sal Charabi's temporary installation of lanterns in Giza

A lamp from his show Behind the Moon Mountain

Against the backdrop of Giza, architect/designer Salem Charabi just debuted fifteen lanterns in his first exhibition in Egypt—titled Behind the Moon Mountain and presented in the courtyard of his home/studio in the rural village of Al-Haraneya. Here, this past year, the Danish/Egyptian architect has made the Ramses Wissa Wassef Art Centre (founded in the early 1950s as a weaving school for children) a place of self-discovery. “The exhibition is the result of trying to uncover the landscapes that surround me,” says Charabi, who worked between Cairo and Copenhagen before moving to Egypt full-time in late 2025. “One of the luxuries of being from multiple places is the agility it gives you from a young age—the ability to rapidly adapt to your surroundings.” At the three-day installation, Charabi invited guests to observe the sun set and the lanterns come to life: The show’s title is inspired by the fable that the Nile springs from the Moon Mountains, an undefined range south of the country. Light in Egypt, he explains, is invoked continually as a blessing. When admiring something made by hand, one says, “May God illuminate your hands.” The 15 lanterns are made from hand-chiselled diorite from Aswan; palm reeds from date palms in Saqqara; and handmade paper made from the stems of banana palms in the Nile Delta. The lights also reference scenes of Cairo, a city of a million satellites, pigeon towers, and other improvised vertical structures. “I wanted to create an array of objects, derived from a type that changes and evolves with each iteration—illuminating the night, all exploring different proportions and scales. —Sam Cochran

Egypt's improvisational architecture inspired the lighting series.

Catalpa/Lightbulbs, a still life by AD contributing photographer Chris Mottalini and his son, Nino.

Pablo Picasso famously asserted, “It took me four years to paint like Raphael, but a lifetime to paint like a child.” The Spanish artist had lots to say about the struggle to recapture the magic and innocence of youth. Photographer Chris Mottalini, a frequent contributor to AD, found a shortcut in that journey, enlisting his son, Nino, as a collaborator on a project that has now spanned four years and two continents. “In 2022, when Nino was only five, I had the idea to task him with making sets out of paper, cardboard, and found objects for a new series of still-life images. I was just curious to see what he’d come up with,” Mottalini recalls. Nino accepted the challenge with gusto, unearthing oddities in the basement of the family’s home in the Hudson Valley, and foraging for foliage and flowers in the surrounding forest. The enterprise continued in Bangkok, the home of Nino’s maternal grandparents, where the budding artist discovered a wealth of antiques and toothsome Thai fruits. The results of their intriguing creative partnership are currently on view through November 15 at Available Items in Tivoli, New York, in an exhibition appropriately titled “Nino + Dad (Home Studies).” —Mayer Rus

A tablescape featuring Tabitha Simmons's new collection for Cabana

Fall is a time for leaf-peeping. The holidays are moments to raise a glass. Both traditions come to life in Tabitha Simmons’s new line of tableware for Cabana. Drawing upon classic English country house motifs, her own family memories, and the blazing colors of New England fall, the star designer has conjured a series of instant heirlooms. Oak leaves and acorns ring hand-painted plates. Embroidered placemats and napkins bear playful scalloped edges and foliate motifs. And a tablecloth reveals dense botanicals in a saturated hues of browns and crimson. You can all but smell the crisp autumn air. –Sam Cochran

#4579. Legendary game creator Vince Zampella has passed away

Vince Zampella, co-creator of Call of Duty, and a veteran studio head responsible for some of the biggest franchises in the world, is gone.

Vince Zampella, known for co-creating the Call of Duty series, among many other roles, has died. Zampella, 55, died in a car crash in Los Angeles on Sunday. The fatal crash killed him, along with the occupant of his car.

According to NBC, Zampella’s Ferrari hit a concrete barrier after leaving a tunnel in the San Gabriel Mountains, north of Los Angeles. Zampella was unfortunately trapped in an ensuing car fire, and the passenger was ejected.

Vince Zampella is one of the industry’s most prolific creators. His storied career is long, and is responsible for some of the biggest shooter franchises in history. It started with Medal of Honor in the 90s, a game that changed WW2 shooters forever.

Zampella later co-founded the legendary Infinity Ward in 2002, the team that would go on to redefine multiplayer shooters forever. Infinity Ward is the studio responsible for the Call of Duty series, which remains one of the biggest games for publisher Activision, and the entire industry.

Zampella then left the studio he founded in 2010 following a major fallout with Activision. He, along with longtime collaborator, Jason West, went to rival publisher EA to form Respawn Entertainment.

Respawn went on to create the Titanfall series, and later Apex Legends. The studio is responsible for non-shooters, of course, like the third-person action game series Star Wars Jedi. Zampella was instrumental in making the game possible, having hired God of War 3 director Stig Asmussen to helm a team within Respawn out of a desire to diversify the studio’s output and prove he can do more than make shooters.

Zampella had only recently met with journalists and peers at The Game Awards, where Battlefield 6 was nominated for multiple awards. Battlefield 6 is the most successful game in series history, in part as a result of Zampella’s leadership. The legendary leader was given the reigns of the struggling multiplayer shooter franchise in 2021, with a goal of finding a viable future.

Zampella’s sudden death has shaken the industry, and many mourn his loss as the news started spreading.

“We are heartbroken by the loss of Vince Zampella, a creative leader whose work shaped generations of players and helped define what modern shooters and action games could be,” EA said in a statement.

“While his impact reached far beyond any one game or studio, we will remember Vince for how he showed up every day, trusting his teams, encouraging bold ideas, and believing in Battlefield and the people building it. We are deeply grateful for his leadership, his generosity, and the care he brought to everything he touched, and we will carry that forward with us.”

Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

#4577. Why Living with Endometriosis May Double Your Odds of Migraine

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

In a new analysis, researchers found females with endometriosis were 2.3 times more likely to also have migraine, and they said the two conditions may share a pain pathway involving nerves and inflammation.Recognizing that connection earlier could lead to coordinated care for both conditions, the researchers said.The analysis, presented at the American Neurological Association (ANA) Annual Meeting in San Diego on October 20, included more than 13 million females. The research hasn’t yet been published in a peer-reviewed scientific journal.

Endometriosis occurs when tissue similar to what lines the uterus grows outside the uterus, Mallory Stuparich, MD, a gynecologic surgeon at MemorialCare Miller Children’s & Women’s Hospital in Long Beach, California, told Healthline. Stuparich was not involved in the study.Yohannes Woubishet Woldeamanuel, MD, lead author of the study and assistant professor of neurology at Mayo Clinic Arizona, told Healthline that migraine also involves more than one part of the body.“Migraine is more than a headache,” he said.Headache is one symptom, Woldeamanuel said, but migraine can also cause sensory, motor, and balance symptoms.“Migraine is a little complex. It’s a multiple-nervous system involving a condition,” he said.

Stuparich said endometriosis pain typically develops in 2 ways. The first starts in the lesions themselves.“There are inflammatory markers that are created by the endometriosis lesions themselves, and those inflammatory markers can irritate the nerves that live close by the lesions,” she said.The second involves the nervous system, Stuparich said. Scar tissue can grow its own nerve supply, and years of chronic pain can change how the nervous system responds. That adaptation can “perpetuate pain or make people feel sensations that normally wouldn’t be painful,” she said.Stuparich said the inflammatory and immune processes involved in endometriosis have “pretty significant overlap in the setting of migraines.”

One possible link between the two conditions is calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP), a chemical messenger that helps transmit pain signals, the researchers said.CGRP plays a key role in migraine attacks, Woldeamanuel said, and blocking it reduces those attacks. CGRP medications work by blocking the sites where CGRP attaches or by binding to CGRP itself.Researchers have also found CGRP in endometriosis tissue.

A 2024 study published in Science Translational Medicine found CGRP in endometriosis lesions from both people and mice. In mice, blocking CGRP signaling reduced pain and reduced lesion size.

Other studies point to shared biology between the two conditions:

Stuparich said she was “not surprised at all” by the finding because it matches what she sees in her own patients.“We see this overlap of patients who have endometriosis also having migraines,” she said.Stuparich said the science still has gaps. She noted that nothing has been proven conclusively in peer-reviewed research yet, but the way both diseases behave supports a connection.“It makes sense that there would be overlap,” she said.

Endometriosis and migraine are treated by different specialists, so the overlap is easy to miss, Woldeamanuel said.For the analysis, researchers at Mayo Clinic Arizona reviewed 35 studies published over 50 years, comparing migraine rates among 207,675 females with endometriosis and 12,963,120 females without the condition.Short-term health records miss up to 90% of that overlap, according to the analysis. Migraine rates in females with endometriosis changed depending on how each study identified migraine.“This data is hiding,” Woldeamanuel said.

“These two clinics are siloed. One clinic doesn’t ask about the other condition. They just focus on their own specialty,” Woldeamanuel said.The findings of the analysis have not been published in a peer-reviewed journal.

Some treatments already overlap, Stuparich said. Hormonal birth control “can sometimes be used to treat menstrually associated migraines,” she said.Stuparich said she has heard anecdotally of patients whose endometriosis symptoms improved after they started CGRP medications for migraine, though long-term data are still lacking.The researchers said the findings support studying whether existing CGRP-blocking migraine medications could also relieve endometriosis pain, potentially offering one treatment for both conditions.Woldeamanuel leads a planned Mayo Clinic trial testing one of those medications, rimegepant (Nurtec ODT), for endometriosis pain. The trial will also track migraine days in participants who have both conditions. It’s expected to finish in 2031, according to ClinicalTrials.gov.

People who suspect they have both conditions can take steps now to get them treated together, Stuparich said.Patients can raise the connection themselves, Stuparich said. She suggested a starting point for each appointment:

Patients can also ask for an early referral to the other specialist so both conditions get coordinated care, Woldeamanuel said. “The earlier it’s diagnosed, the better it’s managed,” he said.Stuparich said doctors are moving away from viewing endometriosis as only a gynecologic disorder because it can affect the whole body.“It is a whole body systemic neuroinflammatory neuroimmune disorder,” she said.

#4575. The best pizza ovens

Updated Oct 26, 2026 10:20 PM EDT

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn more ›

The best pizza oven for most people is the Gozney Arc ($799.99 at Gozney as of October 2026), a 14-inch propane oven with a thermometer in the box that I’ve cooked on myself. If space or budget is tight, its smaller sibling, the Gozney Arc Lite ($399.99), heats quickly, holds its heat, and packs away easily. If you’d rather cook over live fire, the Ooni Karu 2 ($449) burns wood or charcoal out of the box. Plenty has changed since our last update: Gozney replaced the Dome we tested with the Dome Gen 2, Ooni renamed the Karu 12G to Karu 2 and put our old favorite, the first-generation Karu 12, on clearance, Ooni now sells a stone that rotates your pizza for you, and the ALFA oven we recommended for big pies is discontinued. This guide covers outdoor ovens plus one indoor pick; if you only cook inside, our guide to the best indoor pizza ovens goes deeper.

I’ve cooked on the Gozney Arc and Arc Lite myself, and PopSci tested the Ninja Woodfire, the Nexgrill Ora, and the original Gozney Dome for earlier versions of this guide. The rest of the picks are research-based. We didn’t run a new comparative test round for this update. Instead, we combined our earlier hands-on testing with current manufacturer specs and measurements from named independent reviewers, and we focused on heat-up time, cooking consistency, ease of use, capacity, and fire management.

For the researched picks, we read the manufacturers’ spec pages and help centers, checked every price at a named retailer on October 21, 2026, and read reviews from TechRadar, T3, Livingetc, and Bob Vila, crediting their measurements by name. Maximum temperatures are labeled as manufacturer ratings because a stone rarely reaches them in use. We also checked each brand’s lineup for successors and discontinued models, which is how the old Dome, the first-generation Karu 12, and the ALFA 4 Pizze ended up reworked or cut.

We favored ovens you can buy new from the manufacturer or a major retailer today, and we cut picks that only turned up through third-party sellers, which is what happened to our old countertop pick.

Pizza ovens split along fuel lines. Propane is the easiest to live with, wood and charcoal require more attention but give you a live-fire cooking experience, and electric models trade top-end heat for convenience. Our picks range from $149 to $2,299.99 and cover all three, plus an indoor oven. Maximum temperatures are manufacturer ratings, and each card’s specs list how we evaluated it: tested, researched, or predecessor tested (we tested the previous version and researched the current one).

Prices and availability were checked from October 21 to October 26, 2026, at the retailers named and may change.

Why it won: The Gozney Arc keeps the rolling side flame of the Arc Lite but adds room for 14-inch pies, a thicker stone, and a thermometer in the box.

I’ve cooked on both of Gozney’s compact Arc ovens, and the regular Gozney Arc is the one I’d recommend first. It costs $799.99 at Gozney, twice the price of the Arc Lite below, and the extra money addresses the Lite’s two biggest limits: the Arc takes a 14-inch pizza instead of a 12-inch one, and it comes with a thermometer, so you’re not guessing at the oven’s temperature.

On paper, the rest of the upgrade is about holding heat. Gozney lists a 20mm cordierite stone, thicker than the Arc Lite’s 12mm, inside a two-layer insulated body, and that extra stone mass is what you want when you’re launching pizzas back to back. The heat comes from a lateral gas burner on one side that Gozney says recreates a wood-fired rolling flame, the same layout as the Lite, with an adjustable flame control. The box also includes a hose and regulator for a standard propane tank, a burner guard, and a stone adjustment tool.

The tradeoff is bulk. Gozney lists the Arc at 47.5 pounds and about 19 by 22 inches, nearly twice the Arc Lite’s weight, so it’s still a tabletop oven but less of a grab-it-and-go one. It runs on propane only, and Gozney extends the warranty from one year to five if you register within 60 days. The built-in thermometer reads the oven, not the stone, so an infrared thermometer is still useful. If you want 16-inch pies, the Arc XL costs $999.99.

Why it won: The Arc Lite heats fast and holds its heat in a 26.4-pound body that costs half as much as the Arc.

I’ve been cooking on the Gozney Arc Lite too, and it’s the one I’d pick if storage space or budget matters more than pie size. It heats up quickly, it holds that heat well from one pizza to the next, and it cools down relatively quickly once you turn off the gas, which matters when you’re the one waiting to put it away. It also feels like a much more expensive oven than it is. Gozney launched the Arc Lite in March 2026 at $399.99, which is still its price (it dropped to $349.99 during Gozney’s Labor Day sale).

Size is the reason it gets its own slot. Gozney lists it at 26.4 pounds and about 17 by 19 inches, with a half-inch cordierite stone sized for 12-inch pies and a rolling flame along one side of the chamber, controlled by a dial on the front. It fits on a small patio table or a shelf in the garage between cooks. That footprint may also suit some balconies, provided your building rules, local restrictions, and Gozney’s clearance requirements permit a propane oven. Bob Vila’s reviewer, writing in May 2026, needed 20 to 30 minutes of preheat and finished most pizzas in 2 to 3 minutes.

My one real gripe is the missing thermometer. The Arc Lite has no built-in temperature readout, so you can’t see when the stone is ready, and an infrared thermometer is strongly recommended if you want repeatable results. Gozney sells its own infrared thermometer for $49.99, and our guide to the best infrared thermometers has other options. Gozney offers an extensive accessory lineup (covers, a stand shared with the bigger Arc models, peels), although the extras add up quickly. If you want 14-inch pies and a thermometer in the box, step up to the Arc above; the Arc XL goes to 16 inches for $999.99.

Why it won: The Karu 2 burns wood or charcoal out of the box and takes a $120 gas burner later, so you don’t have to pick a fuel up front.

Propane is the easy way to make pizza. Wood is the more involved way, and the Ooni Karu 2 is the oven we’d buy for it. It burns hardwood or charcoal right out of the box, and the optional gas burner (a $120 add-on) gives you propane for weeknights when you don’t feel like tending a fire. Ooni lists it at $449. If the name is new to you, it shouldn’t be: Ooni’s own FAQ says the Karu 12G and the Karu 2 are the exact same oven, so the Karu 2 is a rename, not a redesign.

Wood takes more attention than gas. You’re feeding small splits into the rear tray, watching the flame, and adjusting airflow to keep the stone hot, and how close you get to Ooni’s 950°F rating depends on dry fuel and a well-fed fire. The Karu 2’s built-in thermometer tracks the oven temperature, but an infrared thermometer is still useful because it tells you when the stone itself is ready for a pizza. Live fire can add some smoke character, especially on longer cooks, though for a pizza that bakes in about a minute or two the difference versus gas may be subtle. Most of the appeal is the fire itself.

Our previous best overall, the first-generation Ooni Karu 12, is still on sale at Ooni for $249 (down from $349). At 26.5 pounds, it’s lighter and $200 cheaper than the Karu 2, and it’s a good buy while the clearance stock lasts.

Why it won: The Dome Gen 2 adds a built-in air-and-floor temperature console and room for a 16-inch pie to the design we tested.

We tested the original Gozney Dome for an earlier version of this guide, and it impressed us. In gas mode it passed 930°F in under 20 minutes without any tending, and it took us three or four pies to dial in a classic Neapolitan, after which it was easy to repeat. That Dome is gone now. Gozney replaced it with the Dome Gen 2 in fall 2025, its old product link now forwards to the Gen 2 lineup, and Gozney’s help center says the cheaper Dome S1 is no longer made. What follows about the Gen 2 comes from Gozney’s specs and other outlets’ reviews, not our own testing.

The Gen 2 is a bigger, smarter oven. Gozney’s specs call for one 16-inch pizza or two 10-inch pies on a 30mm cordierite stone, 136.7 pounds for the oven alone, and a built-in digital console that shows air and floor temperature, runs a cook timer, and takes two meat probes. It comes in separate propane and natural-gas versions and can also burn wood or charcoal. Gozney’s optional Wood-Fire Control Kit ($199.99, sold out when we checked) adds powered airflow and finer control over a live fire. Gas mode works a lot like a propane grill: light it, then manage the pizza’s distance from the flame.

It’s a project, though, and the stand is extra. TechRadar’s December 2025 review lists a 40- to 45-minute heat time, says the oven does best with little or no wind blowing into the mouth, and notes that the Gen 2 drops the original’s steam injector attachment for bread. T3’s reviewer couldn’t get the igniter to spark even after two battery changes and lit it with a match instead. It was $1,999.99 during Gozney’s Labor Day sale, and the Dome XL Gen 2 fits an 18-inch pie for $2,799.99.

Why it won: We tested the Ninja Woodfire, and it’s the only pick here with a built-in pellet smoker and a dehydrate mode.

We love pizza as much as the next person, but you have to eat something else occasionally, and the Ninja Woodfire 8-in-1 Outdoor Oven handles most of it. It’s about the size of a microwave and runs on electricity. You plug it in, add the right rack or the pizza stone, set the temperature, and let it preheat. Next to the gas and wood ovens on this list, it’s close to hands-off.

It has five pizza settings: thin crust, pan, New York, Neapolitan, and frozen. In our testing, the Neapolitan and New York modes took about 15 minutes to hit max temperature, then we slid in the pizza and closed the door. There’s almost no learning curve and no turning or babysitting, and when we opened the door we had a well-cooked pie. The chamber is snug, though. Our roughly 12-inch pizza just barely fit and took some nudging to keep it from oozing over the sides. The stone lifts out, but Ninja says not to get it wet: let it cool completely, scrape it gently with a nonmetal tool, and wipe away debris with a soft cloth.

Once you’re done with pizza, it roasts, broils, bakes, dehydrates, and smokes. It held a steady 225°F to 250°F in our testing, and the pellet box on the side adds smoke flavor the way a pellet grill does. SharkNinja’s own store showed the Woodfire out of stock at its $399.99 list price, while Best Buy had it for $329.99 with a 4.1-star average across 864 ratings. SharkNinja’s newer Ninja Artisan MO201 ($249.99 on sale, down from $299.99) keeps the 12-inch capacity and 700°F ceiling but drops the pellet smoker.

Why it won: For $149, the Nexgrill Ora’s rotating-stone knob gave us a more even bake than turning pies by hand.

Cooking homemade pizza in a 900°F oven can be intimidating, and the Nexgrill Ora 12 makes it easier to get good results on your first try. A knob on the right side rotates the central pizza stone, turning your pie for an even cook. With most ovens, you turn the pizza yourself with a peel, and at these temperatures 10 seconds can be the difference between a well-cooked pizza and a charred one.

Setup was easy. The oven comes fully assembled; all we had to do was drop in the five pieces of its stone, which felt like a big, easy puzzle, then connect a propane tank. Gas is beginner-friendly because the heat is steady without feeding wood. A chamber on top can hold wood if you want a little smoke. Turning it on was as simple as twisting the power knob to the flame icon. The flame sits toward the back, so rotating matters, and the oven was ready in about 10 minutes in our testing (Nexgrill rates it to reach 900°F in about 20). Our one complaint is the lack of a built-in thermometer.

Pizzas went in and out the same way as in other ovens: flour the dough on a peel, top it lightly, launch it, and pull it after about 90 to 120 seconds. The difference was a more even cook every time, thanks to the knob, which has enough resistance that you won’t send the pie spinning. Nexgrill sells it for $149, though The Home Depot listed it at $206.12 the same day, where it averages 3.9 stars across 116 reviews and a couple of owners mention the knob occasionally sticking.

Why it won: The Koda 2 Max is rated for a 20-inch pie or three 10-inch pizzas at once for about $1,000 less than the Dome Gen 2.

Feeding a backyard full of people one 12-inch pie at a time gets old fast. The Ooni Koda 2 Max is our pick for that party. Ooni rates its 24-by-21-inch cordierite stone for one 20-inch New York-style pie, two 12-inch pizzas, or three 10-inch pizzas at once, and two independently controlled burners let you run the two sides at different heat.

It’s gas only, in separate propane and natural-gas versions, and Ooni includes its Connect digital temperature hub and a food probe, so you’re not guessing at stone temperature the way you are with the Arc Lite. We replaced the ALFA 4 Pizze with it in this slot. The ALFA we used to link is discontinued, and the current wood-fired top ran about $2,800 to $3,000 at BBQGuys and Embers Living, before you build a base for it.

At 95 pounds, it needs a permanent spot on a stout table. Ooni lists it at $1,299, though the page showed it out of stock with preorders open, so check the delivery estimate before you plan a party around it. It also works with Ooni’s rotating stone, sold separately or in a $1,649 bundle.

Why it won: This is the least expensive way to get Ooni’s motorized stone, which turns the pizza so you don’t have to.

Turning the pizza is the skill that separates a leopard-spotted crust from a scorched one, and Ooni’s rotating stone takes it off your plate. Launched in March 2026, it replaces the standard stone in Ooni’s Koda 2 ovens with a motor-driven one powered by a battery pack under the oven floor. Wave your hand or a peel in front of its sensor and it starts or stops. Ooni says it can spin continuously or turn 90 degrees at timed intervals, and that four AA batteries last about seven hours of rotation.

We picked the bundle with the standard 14-inch Koda 2 because it’s the least expensive of Ooni’s three rotating-stone bundles: $799 (regular $828), versus $499 for the oven alone and $329 for the 12-inch stone on its own. You still launch the pizza yourself. Livingetc’s reviewer, writing in May 2026, got an uneven first pie because she moved it too soon, then got even results by turning the flame down to about half; each pizza took 90 seconds to two minutes.

The rotating stone caps the base Koda 2 at 12-inch pies (the standard stone that’s also in the box handles 14), and the base Koda 2 has no thermometer. The cheaper way to rotate is the Nexgrill Ora’s manual knob for $149. Ooni showed the bundle out of stock with preorders open, so check the ship date.

Why it won: The Volt 2 is the successor to the indoor oven we tested and liked best, and Ooni rates it for 850°F from a standard outlet.

No yard, no balcony, or no interest in standing outside in February? The Ooni Volt 2 brings high-heat pizza indoors. Ooni says it’s made for indoor use only, plugs into a standard 120-volt outlet, and reaches 850°F. It replaced the original Ooni Volt, which PopSci tested and named the top pick in our guide to the best indoor pizza ovens. We haven’t tested the Volt 2 itself, so this pick is research-based.

Ooni calls the control system Pizza Intelligence. In practice, it’s presets for Neapolitan, thin-and-crispy, and pan pizzas that manage the top and bottom elements for you, plus a Boost mode that Ooni says drives the top element hard enough for a 90-second Neapolitan. A proofing mode drops as low as 70°F for dough, and the smoke and grease filter goes in the dishwasher.

It costs $699 at Ooni, and Ooni extends the warranty to three years if you register it. The Charcoal Grey version was sold out when we checked, while Polar White was listed at the same price. At 38.8 pounds, it wants a permanent spot on the counter.

Every oven on this list can make a good pizza. The differences come down to fuel, size, and how much help you want while you learn.

Propane is the default for a reason. You turn a knob, wait for the stone to heat, and the flame stays steady all night. Wood and charcoal take more attention, since you’re feeding the fire and managing airflow; some people want that ritual, and live fire can add some smoke character, though on a pizza that bakes in a minute or two it may be subtle. Electric ovens like the Ninja Woodfire and Ooni Volt 2 are the easiest of all, though Ninja rates the Woodfire to 700°F, below the 900°F to 950°F ratings on the gas ovens here. The Dome Gen 2 and Koda 2 Max are sold in separate propane and natural-gas configurations, so choose the version that matches your setup.

A 12-inch pizza capacity is enough for most households, and it’s what keeps the Arc Lite and Karu 2 portable. The 14-inch Gozney Arc is the middle ground at 47.5 pounds. Bigger stones let you cook larger pies or several at once, but the weight climbs fast: their makers list the Koda 2 Max at 95 pounds and the Dome Gen 2 at 136.7 pounds. Measure your table, and decide whether the oven lives outside full time or goes back into storage after each cook.

Stone temperature is the key measurement for deciding when to launch a pizza, and several ovens here don’t show it. The Arc Lite, the Nexgrill Ora, and the base Koda 2 have no built-in readout, so budget about $50 for an infrared thermometer. The Karu 2, Gozney Arc, and Arc XL have built-in thermometers, the Dome Gen 2 has a digital console, and the Koda 2 Max comes with Ooni’s temperature hub, but an infrared reading of the stone is still useful on all of them.

Most outdoor ovens put the flame on one side or at the back, so the side nearest the fire cooks faster and you have to turn the pie regularly with a turning peel. If that sounds stressful, the Nexgrill Ora’s manual rotation knob and Ooni’s motorized rotating stone handle it for you.

Neapolitan-style pizza needs intense heat to cook in a couple of minutes or less, which is why most gas and wood ovens here are rated to 900°F or 950°F. Those are manufacturer ratings; the stone itself usually reads lower in use, especially on wood. In our testing, pies on the Nexgrill Ora took about 90 to 120 seconds. New York-style and pan pizzas do better a bit cooler and a bit longer.

Gas is better for most people because the heat is steady and it takes almost no fire management. Wood and charcoal are more hands-on and can add some smoke character, though on a fast-baking pizza the difference may be subtle. Multi-fuel ovens like the Ooni Karu 2 burn wood or charcoal out of the box and take an optional gas burner, so you don’t have to choose up front.

It’s strongly recommended for any oven without a built-in readout, including the Gozney Arc Lite, the Nexgrill Ora, and the base Ooni Koda 2, and it’s still useful on ovens that have one. An infrared thermometer reads the stone’s surface temperature, which is what tells you when to launch. Gozney’s costs $49.99 as of October 2026.

No. Propane, wood, and charcoal ovens burn fuel and produce carbon monoxide, so they belong outside with open air around them. Follow your oven’s manual for clearances. If you want to cook indoors, use an electric oven designed for it, like the Ooni Volt 2, which Ooni says is for indoor use only.

The Karu 12G and Karu 2 are the same oven; Ooni’s FAQ says so directly, and the Karu 2 is the current name. The Karu 12 is the first-generation model, which Ooni was selling for $249 (down from $349) as of October 2026.

The original Gozney Dome has been replaced by the Dome Gen 2, which launched in fall 2025 starting at $2,299.99, and Gozney’s old Dome product page now forwards to the Gen 2 lineup. Gozney’s help center says the Dome S1 is no longer manufactured. A larger Dome XL Gen 2 costs $2,799.99.

Yes. On July 9, 2026, the CPSC announced a recall of about 12,660 Cuisinart Propel+ Four Burner 3-in-1 Gas Grills with a pizza oven (model CGG-6331), because the oven’s tempered glass window can shatter. They were sold at Lowe’s, Walmart, and Cuisinart.com from December 2024 through May 2026. A search of the CPSC recall database on October 21, 2026, found no recalls for the specific models recommended here.

The deepest discounts we documented in 2026 came during holiday sales. During Labor Day 2026, Gozney cut the Arc Lite to $349.99 and the Dome Gen 2 to $1,999.99, and Ooni’s Memorial Day 2026 sale had the first-generation Karu 12 at $249. We’ll update this guide after Black Friday.

The Gozney Arc is the best starting point for most buyers: it takes 14-inch pies, comes with a thermometer, and costs $799.99. If space or budget is tight, the $399.99 Gozney Arc Lite heats quickly, holds its heat, and stores easily; add a $50 infrared thermometer. Choose the Ooni Karu 2 if tending a live fire is part of the appeal, the Nexgrill Ora 12 if price and easy turning matter most, or the Ooni Volt 2 if your pizza oven has to live in the kitchen. For crowds, the Ooni Koda 2 Max handles 20-inch pies, and the Gozney Dome Gen 2 is the one to build an outdoor kitchen around.

#4574. Wardogs devs rush to fix XP exploit allowing players to quickly unlock tanks and stomp competition on Season 2's first day

The launch of a new live season is hard, and subtle changes to a game can have massive consequences when players get their hands on it. Wardogs proved this true with the launch of its second season this week, as players were able to unlock tanks on day one.

The good news (or bad, depending on whether you feel you've missed out on a head start) is that this exploit has already been fixed. Players could drive just outside of their HQ, drop off pallets, and return to base to earn a sizable amount of XP. This, paired with faster XP gains for the Driver role allowed people to ascend rapidly through the Driver progression track and unlock tanks.

This was a problem, as unlocking anti-tank equipment via other progression paths took far longer than the measly few hours of dedicated grinding these Driver XP exploiters sat through, meaning the vast majority of players were unprepared to deal with the tanks - unless they made use of the exploit themselves and got their own.

In a post made to the Wardogs Discord, Bulkhead co-founder Howard Philpott said: "We're holding our hands up and we got a few things wrong". He went on to explain that a backend update was being pushed through that fixes a progression bug for the driver XP path, stomps out that aforementioned exploit, and also removes the Lonestar (blue team) cash and XP bonuses.

In a video released alongside the Discord, post technical designer Mark Tadros explained that players who were using the exploit "extremely" would be receiving 24 hour bans, though the number of players actually guilty of this falls below the 1,000 mark. Everyone else who partook in the practice, to a less absurd degree, appears to have been left alone.

It's the right call, and in all honestly a tragic result for what otherwise looked to be a direct, laser-targeted set of changes meant to take down some of Wardogs' trickier problems. In the first season, Driver XP was among the most time-consuming to get, so a change to improve that was a great idea. Likewise, the now-removed XP and cash bonus provided to the blue team was a smart solution to that faction, which struggled to win games back in season one.

Even so, the reaction in the community has been mixed. Some, understandably, are a little sad they missed out on the good XP gains. Others noted that this all happened while they were at work - the woe of the employed gaming enthusiast.

Nonetheless, Wardogs continues to prove its merit as a popular and enticing military FPS - and one of the breakout successes of the year. In our early access Wardogs review, we said the game's mix of competitive shooters' best bits with something entirely of its own made it "a brilliantly refreshing take on the genre".
