#4573. Is This the Best Thanksgiving Turkey Recipe Ever?

A 5-Ingredient Brine. Cajun Rub. Two Hours in a Smoker. Perfection.

BACK IN 2021, D’Andre Carter was maxed out. The fine-dining chef had opened Soul & Smoke in Chicago, a barbecue-based food truck, and it incinerated his free time, which caused his weight to tick upward.

Then came the hypertension diagnosis, a wake-up call that he needed to act fast. He started jogging and eventually worked up to 60-minute runs five days a week, plus bodyweight exercises.

His diet now is loaded with lean protein (salmon, chicken) and nutrient-dense smoothies. He’s down 20 pounds from 220, his hypertension is in check, and he’s motivated. “Living healthy gives me the stamina to manage a busy life, be present for my family, and lead in the kitchen,” Carter says.

That means cooking well, for everyone, and especially on Thanksgiving, when he turns to this way-more-delicious holiday feast.

“This is my go-to method for a juicy, flavorful whole turkey—perfect for holidays or any gathering,” says Carter. “It’s all about the prep, the brine, and that smoke.”

1. In a large pot, stir 8 quarts of warm water with 1 cup of kosher salt, plus the sugar, onion, garlic, and thyme, until dissolved. Chill till cool, submerge the turkey in the liquid, and brine in the fridge overnight.

2. Remove the turkey from the brine, pat it dry, and rub all over with the olive oil. Season with the rub and 1 Tbsp coarsely ground black pepper.

3. Preheat a kamado smoker (like a Big Green Egg) or arrange a grill for indirect cooking at 350°F. Add the wood chunks. Place the turkey on the grill, breast side up, tucking in the wings underneath. Smoke, lid closed, for 1 hour, open the lid, baste the bird with the butter, and close the lid. Smoke until the internal temp reaches 170°F in the thickest part of the thigh, another 1 to 2 hours. Let the turkey rest for 30 minutes before carving. Feeds 10, with leftovers

The key to amazing barbecued anything is in the spice blend. This one works great on turkey, but also chicken and vegetables.

In a bowl, mix ¼ cup smoked paprika, 2 Tbsp kosher salt, 2 Tbsp garlic powder, 2 Tbsp onion powder, 2 tsp ground black pepper, 2 tsp dried thyme, 2 tsp dried oregano, 1 tsp cayenne pepper (adjust to taste), 1 tsp ground white pepper, and 1 tsp brown sugar. Makes roughly ¾ cup

Photographs by Julia GartlandFood Styling by Rebecca JurkevichProp Styling by Megan Hedgpeth

Paul Kita is a Deputy Editor at Men's Health, where he has covered food, cooking, nutrition, supplements, grooming, tech, travel, and fatherhood at the brand for more than 15 years. He is also the author of two Men's Health cookbooks, Guy Gourmet and A Man, A Pan, A Plan, and the winner of a James Beard Award.

#4572. Love and Deepspace 5.0 update brings together all five love interests for the first time

If you can believe it, Love and Deepspace is almost two-years-old, and Infold Games is celebrating the moment in a few major ways. The Throne of Eros update brings the game up to version 5.0, and it’s one for fans of all love interests.

#4571. "Sexy sorceresses" and "bare-chested fighters": Dragon Age's original artists wanted to base it on Conan the Barbarian, much to the outrage of the designers

Former lead writer David Gaider on the perils of not having a "vision keeper"

Buried in the vast and sooty-fingered Summer Eternal anthology, there's an interview with former BioWare developer and Dragon Age lead writer David Gaider, who is now creative director at Stray Gods outfit Summerfall Studios. The interview is a podcast-length account of BioWare's transformations over Gaider's career, stretching back to Baldur's Gate 2. Amongst other things, it explains why the Dragon Age games feel so different from sequel to sequel.

Gaider discusses the original trilogy's fortunes under RPG-phobic parent company Electronic Arts, commenting that 2009's Dragon Age: Origins made it to release partly thanks to "sunk cost" thinking after EA bought the studio. He also talks about Dragon Age's lack of a properly defined vision, early on, which led to a lot of overcorrection between releases. As regards Origins, there was something of a faction war initially between departments over what exact flavour of fantasy fiction the new RPG should draw on.

"When we started, Dragon Age's big premise was 'it's D&D, but it's not," Gaider began. "We'll just file off the serial numbers enough to make it our own. That was not great because there are different kinds of fantasy."

Specifically, a rift emerged at BioWare between developers who wanted to channel Tolkien, and those who wanted to invoke Robert E. Howard's Conan the Barbarian stories. "If you look back at the E3 Expo, where we announced the existence of Dragon Age, and you watch the video, you will see," Gaider went on (the screening in question was behind closed doors, sadly, so all I can do is point you towards IGN's coverage at the time). "It was mostly made by our art department. They were part of the Conan group. And if you watch it, you'll see sexy sorceresses, the fighter is this bare-chested barbarian riding around… it looks very Conan.

"And that part of the team was determined that their vision was set out," he said. "The design department was mostly like, 'no, this is Lord of the Rings, man, okay? Fuck Conan.' But Art didn't want to listen to us, so we're like 'If you're going to ignore us, we'll ignore you.' And that's really why the Dragon Age that came to be is the way it is. If you look at Dragon Age: Origins, if I said to you, this is the art style of a Conan world with the writing style of a Lord of the Rings, then you'd be like, 'Oh.'"

When teams are at odds in this way, it's helpful to have a third group intervene to thrash out a compromise. The producers, for example. Unfortunately, Gaider continued, Dragon Age's production team had their own wild ideas about the project's direction - they thought it should be Diablo. This did, in fairness, have some kind of unifying effect. "Both the art department and the design department completely ignored them. Like, no, we're not doing Diablo."

Nonetheless, the producers persisted. "They wanted that if you hit creatures, they'd just explode in blood," Gaider said. "We actually had presentations where the people in charge were like, 'Yeah, Dragon Age, we want that the theme is blood! Dripping with blood!' And I'm like, 'That's not a theme.' They did get their way a little bit, Dragon Age was pretty bloody. People did almost explode in blood. We had to tone it down a bit because that just didn't feel right."

I realise there's a serious argument being made here about the necessity of knowing your game's heart, before you commit to knocking out scripts, models and trailers, but I am also having fun imagining the inter-departmental squabbles. I picture Gaider bumping into one of the Dragon Age artists outside an elevator in Edmonton, sometime in 2004. The artist's eyes narrow. "Tell me, David," they growl. "What is best in life?" Gaider's knuckles whiten. "To take the ring to Mordor, throw it into Mount Doom, and hear the lamentation of Sauron," he hisses. At this point the elevator doors open and a producer appears, apoplectic and roaring: "Ahh, fresh meat!"

According to Gaider – who would eventually become "vision keeper" or "educator" for Dragon Age as a series - BioWare did a much better job of getting the band together with Mass Effect, mostly thanks to project director Casey Hudson.

"Mass Effect went through much the same kind of struggle," he said. "It's science fiction. It's 'their Star Wars,' is what that was supposed to be. But there's so many different kinds of science fiction. And they had the benefit that Casey Hudson was the responsible person, and realised sooner than Dragon Age did that we need a singular vision. And he eventually put his foot down and said, 'This is what it is.'

"I think, honestly, Mass Effect as a series had a much stronger throughline of vision," Gaider concluded. "This is a game that is very clear on what it is and what it isn't. Whereas Dragon Age started off kind of a jumble and we figured it out as we went. With Dragon Age: Origins, versus Dragon Age II, versus Dragon Age: Inquisition, you've clearly got a team that is figuring out the world of the series by the seat of their pants and reacting with each release."

To address the Qunari in the room, here: Gaider left many years before Dragon Age: The Veilguard, whose development was a whole 'nother layer of troubled. EA - who are now owned by a consortium consisting mostly of Saudi Arabia's sovereign investment fund - appear to have paused the series in the wake of Veilguard, though modders are trying to keep the flames alive.

What's your poison, then? Conan, Tolkien or Diablo? While we're talking office politics at giant corporate developers, the Summer Eternal anthology also harbours a lengthy chat with Dragon Age's former executive producer, Mark Darrah. He has words of warning about "over-specialised" studios and $100 million projects that need to sell ridiculous quantities to break even. As far as I can remember, Darrah doesn't spend any of his chinwag lamenting the great Diablo-like that might have been if it weren't for the Gandalf-loving pinkos in the writing team or those Cromulent numpties in art.

#4570. Watch Jon Bernthal Explain the Ford F-150 Raptor’s Origin Story

Our car experts choose every product we feature. We may earn money from the links on this page.

The commercial, directed by Raptor owner Ben Affleck, stars Bernthal—who also owns one—and many of the people who brought the truck to life.

The Ford F-150 Raptor has long since emented itself as an icon of off-road performance. Now, the Blue Oval is celebrating its origin story on the heels of the latest Raptor Rally event in Lake Havasu, California. And who better to tell the story than Raptor owners Jon Bernthal and Ben Affleck?

“How do you make a monster? It is not always loose brains and lightning,” Bernthal says in Ford’s latest commercial spot. Titled “Project Raptor: Based on a Ford Story,” the commercial tells the abridged story of how the first F-150 Raptor came to be. The Raptor development process started in 2006, and Ford Racing engineers Derek Bier, Tim Smith, and Chris Paiva join the actor in the film. But the timeline from idea to genuine production was hardly straightforward, as the ever-explanatory Bernthal explains while driving an early version of the F-150 Raptor.

“The project got killed six times. But each time, these engineers never stopped,” Bernthal—who, among his many credits, played Ford executive Lee Iacocca in the 2019 film Ford vs. Ferrari—says. Baja was the inspiration, and mass-market adoption the mission. The commercial spot shows off just how capable even the first-generation F-150 Raptor was; tall jumps and big power slides in the sandy desert are, naturally, the action shots of the commercial.

And Bernthal didn’t accept this opportunity simply for the money, either. “I’ve been driving a Raptor for years, and I know just how much of a beast it really is,” Bernthal said. “It’s honestly the most insane, capable truck you can drive—period. When I was approached about doing this ad, it was a natural fit for me as a true Raptor fan, and I’ll jump at any chance to show it off and celebrate the engineers who built it.”

It was Bernthal who actually introduced commercial director and Academy Award winner Ben Affleck to the high-performance pickup, according to MotorTrend. Bernthal and Affleck went on a rapid ride in an F-150 Raptor while shooting The Accountant in 2015, prompting Affleck to buy not one but two Raptors since then. As Ford continues to celebrate 125 years of Ford Racing, the commercial spot will air this Saturday, October 24, on Apple TV during the Ole Miss vs. Texas football game, as well as during the United States Grand Prix in Austin on Sunday.

After learning to ride at 17, Emmet worked in the motorcycle industry before joining Autoweek in 2022 and Road & Track in 2024. #4569. In Brooklyn Decker and Andy Roddick’s North Carolina Home, Southern Hospitality Meets Modern Charm

The home was, decidedly, not love at first sight for Brooklyn Decker and Andy Roddick. “One of the rooms had caved in; there was water pouring in through the ceiling,” Decker says of the first time the couple saw the Charlotte, North Carolina, Georgian-style house.

But even in its dilapidated state, the 1960s-era home was still intriguing: It was located in the favored neighborhood for the young family, and its traditional, elegant proportions were still evident through its disrepair. At first it was Roddick, the former number-one men’s singles tennis player, who urged Decker to keep the house on their short list. Soon model and actor Decker, who grew up in nearby Matthews, warmed at the chance to rehabilitate a proper Carolina home with details like dental crown molding and classic brick. “We’d moved so many times [before this house],” she says. “And we were excited to try something new. For us, that meant a historic house with some old Southern bones.”

In the entry, Roddick is seated on a vintage sofa covered in a Zak and Fox fabric. Decker stands next to the walnut staircase built by Masterpiece Staircase and finished by Tony Montognese. The custom chandelier is by Kalin Asenov, the rug is vintage, and the artwork is by Kieran Brennan Hinton.

The family’s living room, clad in mahogany paneling, is Decker and Roddick’s favorite space in the whole home. The fireplace was designed by Decker, fabricated by Tyler Ernsberger, and stained by Tony Montognese, with animal carvings done by Stan Dunham. The 1930s coffee table is by Paul László, and the light fixture is a vintage Longobard for Poliarte. The chairs, clockwise from top right, are by BDDW, vintage Swedish sourced from PRB Collection, and a vintage Guglielmo Ulrich.

The home’s foyer received the biggest glow-up, with the staircase being entirely redesigned from a curving one. Chandelier and flush-mount light designed by Decker and Kalin Asenov, sideboard by BDDW, the mirror and sofa are vintage, millwork by Bill Truitt Woodworks (with finishes by Tony Montognese), and the walnut flooring is by Richard Marshall Fine Flooring.

Decker and Roddick were no strangers to home renovation, but as evidenced just from their first walkthrough, this latest undertaking would be on a scale they had not yet explored. The home had been little touched since its construction in 1964, and the couple would be only the second owners. Aside from obvious fixes like leaking ceilings and sagging floors, the layout, which was chopped up into a warren of tight quarters, needed a full gut to allow the space to breathe and gain more light. “We were parents of two young kids,” Decker says. “So the question was, How do we respect the grandeur of the house but also make it feel hospitable to a young family and one that can live up to the demands of everyday life?”

The dining room shows off many of the home’s thematic colors, including red, yellow, and green. Drapery fabric by Kravet, the pink chairs are vintage Kerstin Hörlin Holmquist, dining chairs by Studio Valle de Valle, antique farm table sourced from Obsolete, painting by Tyler Buck Guinn, and the pendants are by Of Enso, made with a vintage silk gingham from Decker’s personal collection.

The first-floor front room continues the nature theme thanks to its hand-painted custom mural. The sofa is a vintage Carl Malmsten upholstered in a Rose Uniacke fabric; the blue club chairs are vintage and sourced from Eneby Home; the leather chair is a vintage Hans Wegner; coffee table designed by Decker and fabricated by Dunham and Ernsberger Custom Cabinetry; and the armoire is a vintage walnut wet bar sourced from 1stDibs.

The kitchen hall’s allover paneling was fabricated by Bill Woodworks and installed by Blockhaus. Flush mounts by The Urban Electric Co.; the painting is a vintage William Skilling; sideboard by Bicyclette; and the rug is vintage found on Etsy.

The kitchen is painted in a muted shade of Decker’s favorite color: green. The double islands were designed by Decker and Dunham and fabricated by Ernsberger Custom Cabinetry; the pendants are by Roman and Williams Guild; the fittings by Waterworks; and the painting is an antique signed by Adelaide Briggs.

The breakfast nook brings the garden inside with its collection of plants and green accents. The table was custom-designed by Decker and Dunham and fabricated by Ernsberger Custom Cabinetry. The chairs are by Studio Ashby and upholstered with a Lee Jofa fabric, and the light fixture is a vintage Hans-Agne Jackobsson.

To help with the transformation, the couple engaged both Ruard Veltman Architecture for the architectural design and Stanwick Dunham for contracting. The group transformed the nearly 7,000-square-foot structure into a home that celebrates its lineage without being mired in the past. Nowhere is this effort more evident than in the gracious entryway, whose original carpeted staircase curved up from stark black and white tiles. And while that design wasn’t in poor taste, its severity read as both formal and cold. The solution was twofold: both to square-off the staircase and to clad the entire area in rich walnut paneling. The result not only allows sunlight to flow down from the second story, but showcases warm millwork. “We wanted it to look like it predated our arrival, and also be a bit more humble in its materials,” Decker says.

The primary bedroom’s wood floors and cream walls are softened up with the addition of a full wall of drapery, with fabric from William Morris. The chandelier is a vintage Carlo Scarpa sourced from 1stDibs, the bed from The Expert, linens from Nordic Knots, chair by Commune for George Smith, and the painting is by Alexandra Valenti.

Another view of the bedroom spotlights the family’s dog finding his light. Art by Tyler Hays Studio, nightstands by BDDW, vintage daybed from 1stDibs, and the vintage chair is sourced from Evan Reid.

The couple’s closet displays shoes almost in a museum-like setting, inside an antique French shoe cabinet sourced from 1stDibs. Ottoman from John Derian, the paint color is Farrow & Ball White Tie, and the chandelier is from RH.

The home’s primary bath was completely gut-renovated, but Decker wanted the design to appear original to the house. Tile from Waterworks, tub from Water Monopoly, side table by BDDW, painting by George Buckner of The Florida Highwaymen, and the pendant is an antique French copper lantern sourced from 1stDibs.

For the home’s interior design, Decker had no need of hiring a professional: She’s become a decorator herself. Over the last six or so years and mostly by word of mouth, the actor has spearheaded a variety of design projects (her latest, along with her design partners at By Idiots, being the interiors of Intown Golf Club’s new properties in Houston and Raleigh). For her family home, the brief was to keep the Georgian influence while updating the aesthetic. And it remains an evolving one: “Whenever I see that furniture dolly out, I know I’ll be involved at some point,” Roddick says.

Decker achieved the cozy yet elevated mix through color, using muted shades of green (her color), red, blue, and yellow; wallpaper with natural images like leaves or rivers; and plenty of vintage patterned rugs to soften up the wood floors. “It was originally old English meets early Georgian meets Shaker,” she says. “But when our kids became older, we really wanted to incorporate and take seriously what they wanted. Because, how cool to be eight and actually have some say in the space around you? So now the house is a hodgepodge of explosive color, a lot of which is totally inspired and dictated by them.”

The children’s playroom has a vintage feel while still being cozy. Sofa by RH, mohair chair and ottoman by Sam Klemick, wallpaper by Charles Voysey, and the shades are Fabricut fabric.

The couple’s daughter wanted her new room to match her softball team’s colors of red, white, and blue. Bed designed by Decker and fabricated by Liven UP, vintage chandelier sourced from PRB Collection, drapes from Anthropologie, and the rug is sourced from Hall Pass Vintage.

Decker’s office adds a bit of whimsy with a ceiling papered in a Pierre Frey design. Rug by Studio MRS for Nordic Knots, sofa by Coley Home, the desk is vintage, and the chandelier is by Apparatus.

An upstairs landing is one of Decker’s favorite places to snuggle with the family pets. Pendant by Roman and Williams Guild, dresser by Orior, the chairs are vintage Paolo Buffa upholstered in a Rose Uniacke fabric, table is vintage sourced from PRB Collection, and the pillows were made by Chancery Custom with vintage tapestry fabric sourced by Decker.

#4568. ADHD Medications May Help Ease Menopausal Brain Fog

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Medications commonly used to treat attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) may help reduce certain symptoms of menopausal brain fog, according to a review presented at the 2026 Annual Meeting of the Menopause Society. The findings have not yet been published in a peer-reviewed journal.

The research suggests that both lisdexamfetamine (Vyvanse) and atomoxetine (Strattera) may improve self-reported executive function in perimenopausal and postmenopausal people. However, they showed little to no effect on objective cognitive tests.

While this scoping review showed promising results, experts caution that it may be too early to begin recommending these medications to females experiencing menopause.

Sara Vaughn, MD, Chief Medical Officer at Clair Health, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that these findings are interesting and hypothesis-generating, but they do not yet establish ADHD medications as a treatment for menopausal brain fog.

Many middle-aged females report experiencing forgetfulness, slow thinking, reduced concentration, and difficulty finding words. This is often referred to as “brain fog,” and is a common occurrence during various stages of menopause.

These symptoms of brain fog also mirror the symptoms of ADHD.

Brain fog may be due to declining or fluctuating levels of estrogen during perimenopause, which affects dopamine and norepinephrine signaling in the prefrontal cortex and the hippocampus. This can impact memory, focus, and executive functioning.

Despite how disruptive these symptoms can be to quality of life, there is currently no medication approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to manage them. This often leaves clinicians in a position of trying to differentiate between brain fog from menopause and undiagnosed ADHD.

The review analyzed five studies involving females ages 45–60 with their last menstrual period within the past 10 years. The studies all focused on how ADHD medications affect outcome measures of cognition and ADHD severity.

The researchers found that with lisdexamfetamine, scores on the Brown Attention Deficit Disorder Scale (BADDS), a self-report rating scale that measures ADHD symptoms, dropped from 35.7 to 21.2, compared with 29.8 with a placebo. With atomoxetine, BADDS scores decreased from 38.6 to 25.5, compared with 30.1 with a placebo.

There was no significant benefit observed on objective cognitive tests (ex., memory and processing speed).

These findings suggest that certain ADHD medications may improve perceived cognitive symptoms during menopause, although benefits were not demonstrated on objective cognitive tests.

Jennifer Tat, a medical student at the University of Tennessee Health Sciences Center and lead author of the study, noted, “In the population data, symptom severity, not a formal ADHD diagnosis, predicted menopausal burden. Many women were never diagnosed, so relying on a chart diagnosis misses the women most at risk.”

“We have evidence that the menopause transition is associated with measurable brain changes,” said Vaughn. “But no specific brain signature of menopausal brain fog has been established. To study it rigorously, we first need to define what the term means and measure it consistently.”

Limitations of this review include the small number of studies and the small sample sizes. Most of the measures were also self-reported, which leaves room for error.

Also, the FDA has not approved these types of medications for menopausal brain fog, meaning using them for these purposes would be off-label.

“ADHD medications are not without risks, and I think it is too early to use them routinely for menopausal ‘brain fog,’” said Vaughn.

ADHD medications can carry risks, including cardiovascular side effects. Stimulants such as lisdexamfetamine also have the potential for misuse and dependence. These medications should only be used with medical supervision.

“I am a strong proponent of treating the underlying disorder rather than treating isolated symptoms. ADHD and menopausal cognitive symptoms may look alike, but that does not mean they share the same underlying mechanism—or require the same treatment,” noted Vaughn.

Further research, including additional long-term prospective studies, is needed before clinical guidance can be changed

“We hope this opens up the conversations with patients and providers. We recommend clinicians to screen patients for ADHD and, for those who screen positive, refer them to our psychiatric colleagues who have the knowledge and resources to perform a diagnostic test so that those women who have gone undiagnosed with ADHD can get the treatment they need and deserve,” said Tat.

According to the National Council on Aging (NCOA), your brain requires exercise to function optimally, just like your muscles do.

Ways to help keep your brain sharp and manage menopausal brain fog include:

The NCOA also suggests keeping your brain well-exercised by:

You can also speak with your healthcare professional about how menopause, estrogen levels, hormone therapy, and modifiable risk factors like a sedentary lifestyle or high blood pressure can affect cognitive health.

“I want clinicians to know that they must take brain fog seriously. Ask whether the symptoms are new or a lifelong pattern that menopause has worsened. ADHD in women often goes unrecognized for decades,” said Tat.

#4567. Attack Mode: 2027 Lamborghini Revuelto SV Driven

Racing-inspired hardware, clever electronics, and more usable power up the Revuelto’s game on track.

V-12 Lamborghinis are an intimidating presence. They’re brazen statements of wealth and power, nowadays often seen spitting flames on social media. Yet for all the symbolism and viral clicks, the current Revuelto is by far the most sophisticated and approachable machine of its kind. It’s even a plug-in hybrid. You might assume that Lamborghini applying its go-faster Super Veloce (SV) treatment, the equivalent of waving a red flag at the biggest bull, would make it a wilder, unwieldy beast. The new Revuelto SV is just the opposite.

This latest SV-badged wedge seems cautious at first, prioritizing handling fidelity over raw firepower, though it’s hardly short on the latter. It retains the standard Revuelto’s 814-hp 6.5-liter V-12 and trio of electric motors (one nestled in the eight-speed dual-clutch rear transaxle plus one at each front wheel, giving it all-wheel drive). And despite weight-reducing measures throughout its chassis and body, those savings are mostly negated by a larger 7-kWh battery, up from 4 kWh. We estimate it compresses scales at close to 4300 pounds, or roughly the same as the last Revuelto we tested.

See all results for used cars for sale near c1098 aaw

However, that new battery can now contribute up to 254 electric ponies, versus the smaller pack’s 187-hp max, and with more consistency under heavy use. The extra boost is most noticeable when the car’s front end seemingly yanks it out of tight corners. Altogether, system output is up a modest 49 horses and 7 pound-feet of torque, for respective totals of 1050 and 1051. We reckon that should cut a tenth from the regular Revuelto’s 2.2-second 60-mph time, though the SV will still lose a drag race to some blue-collared Chevy Corvettes. For what it’s worth, the EPA likely will grant more EV range than the base car’s five-mile estimate.

Quoted top speed drops from 217 mph to 214, thanks to the increased drag of the SV’s pointed front bumper, deeper front splitter, and fixed rear wing that give it the profile of an angry lawn dart. More important is the added stability from its 80 percent bump in maximum downforce over the regular Revuelto. Additional racing-derived kit includes a switch from adaptive magnetorheological dampers to manually adjustable passive units, plus tweaks to its rear-suspension geometry. New Formula 1–inspired Brembo CCM-R Plus carbon-ceramic brakes (with whopping 16.5-inch rotors in front and 16.1-inchers in back) sit at each corner, as do SV-exclusive center-locking wheels with standard Bridgestone Potenza Race R track-oriented (yet street-legal) tires, sized 265/35ZR-20 in front and 345/30ZR-21 at the rear.

We can’t yet speak to the SV’s on-road behavior. But it did exhibit excellent poise around Italy’s Vallelunga circuit, shrugging off curbs and deftly hugging the bumpy pavement on the fast, winding front straight. Spinning the Revuelto’s naturally aspirated V-12 through the gears is as intoxicating as ever, its entire cabin resonating with a mechanical wail as the ticking digits in its speedometer turn to a frantic blur. Just as breathtaking is the awesome stopping force of the upgraded brakes and the exceptional pedal feel they maintain lap after lap.

See all results for used cars for sale near c1098 aaw

The SV’s setup only magnifies the Revuelto’s already-shocking agility for its size. Chuck it into a turn, and the car’s front-axle torque vectoring, rear-wheel steering, and massive amounts of predictable grip help you gather up any waywardness and still hit the apex. Working in the background is an enhanced vehicle dynamics sensor that better orients the car’s central nervous system at speed. A new Pilota function in the sportiest Corsa drive mode brings additional flexibility by unlocking the SV’s five-stage traction control, allowing drivers to work up to the car’s limit without fully deactivating its safety net.

While we appreciated the SV’s tech on an unfamiliar track, the Pilota mode’s control knob on the steering wheel is slow to respond and further clutters the Revuelto’s busy helm. Accidentally activating the windshield wipers with your thumb is as uncool as it is distracting when you’re in the heat of a hot lap. We also wish the car’s waist-high exhaust cannons, which can cook your arm from six feet away, were even louder, because a Lamborghini should make you feel 12 years old again. Of the SV’s near-endless personalization items that will quickly inflate its estimated $753,000 base price—about 140 grand more than the standard car’s—the most compelling may be the low-mounted, fixed-back racing seats that offer slightly more headroom.

With production capped at 1963 units (an ode to the company’s founding year), only a privileged few will have the opportunity to flog the SV on track. They won’t be disappointed. In focusing the Revuelto’s aggression with stronger grip and better aerodynamics, Lamborghini made its big brute even easier to drive at a higher level. It still intimidates, but it’s also more enjoyable to tame.

#4566. Fortnite is coming to Linux so we may soon be able to play it and other games like Apex Legends on Steam Deck and Steam Machine

Epic Games is officially working on bringing its battle royale shooter Fortnite to Linux. The announcement was tucked away at the bottom of Epic's widely mocked blog post revealing the new, "vibe-coded" Epic Games Store, which is also coming to Linux.

At the time, Epic explained it's going to be working with Linux distro maintainers on a set of security requirements needed to run Fortnite. Distros - short for distributions - are operating system variants of Linux, each with its own goals and prioritised system operations. Valve's SteamOS, for instance, is based on Arch Linux. Because of the many discrepancies between Linux distros, each would need to implement Epic's security requirements individually if they want Fortnite to run on their operating system. Fortnite will arrive on Linux via the EGS launcher, but only if a "critical mass of distro maintainers" is reached. Epic didn't specify numbers.

This is big news for several reasons. For one, the move would likely make Fortnite playable on SteamOS PCs and handhelds, including Steam Machine and Steam Deck. Furthermore, even if that "critical mass" is never reached, Epic says its work will be available to other developers interested in bringing their games to Linux.

Perhaps the biggest reason this is a big deal, however, has to do with Easy Anti-Cheat, the Epic-owned anti-cheat service used in Fortnite and dozens of other games - including Dead by Daylight, Rocket League, Apex Legends, and even Elden Ring. While Easy Anti-Cheat does support Linux, including for some of the games just mentioned, many developers choose not to enable it, simply because its "Linux implementation couldn't offer the same level of protection as our Windows implementation," as Epic explained in a separate blog post. This is why many games that use Easy Anti-Cheat aren't playable on Steam Machine or Steam Deck. The post even names Valve specifically, saying the team will "continue its open line of communication" to support Steam hardware devices "to the maximum extent technically possible".

Part of the reason Linux has been a difficult problem for anti-cheat makers to solve is the platform's open nature, meaning certain - required - system-level protections are not guaranteed. On Windows, you can ask users to enable features like TPM 2.0 and Secure Boot, which create that secure environment once hardware requirements are met.

While some of that work can be done on Linux, it requires the interest and participation of distro maintainers. Easy Anti-Cheat plans to use Linux features such as signed kernel, signed kernel modules, and secure boot with attestation to achieve that goal. "These requirements build on work that distribution maintainers already do," explains Epic, "and the more distributions that meet these requirements by default, the fewer players will need to change their setup to play." The goal is to "bring the level of protection much closer to par with Windows".

There is no timeline for any of this at present, but Easy Anti-Cheat will test the announced plans in Fortnite first before making them available to other developers. In other words, if Fortnite gets a release date on Linux, you'll know the work has paid off and we're closer than ever to achieving anti-cheat parity between Windows and Linux.

#4565. The best portable chargers

Updated Oct 26, 2026 10:37 PM EDT

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn more ›

The best portable charger is the Anker Prime Power Bank (26K, 300W) ($229.99 at Anker as of October 2026), because it can fast-charge two laptops and a phone at once, gets back to 80 percent in 40 minutes by Anker’s rating, and stays just under the 100Wh airline limit. If you carry an iPhone, the Anker MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro ($109.99 at Anker) snaps on magnetically and charges at up to 25W with no cable, and if you want a laptop-capable bank for less, the UGREEN Nexode 20,000mAh 130W costs $59.99. Plenty has changed since our last update: our previous budget pick appears to be a model INIU recalled in December 2025, Anker recalled several of its own banks in 2025, Qi2.2 magnetic banks raised wireless charging speeds from 15W to 25W, and airlines tightened power bank rules this spring. This guide is for anyone who wants to keep a phone, tablet, or laptop running away from an outlet; if you need to power appliances or camp for a week, our guide to the best portable power stations fits better.

We’ve used power banks for years to keep phones, cameras, and laptops alive while covering events, traveling, and working away from a desk, and our own testing covered the UGREEN 145W bank here and the predecessor of our travel pick. For this update, we also read the 2026 power bank reviews and roundups from outlets including Macworld, Digital Camera World, Expert Reviews, Engadget, Tom’s Guide, and CleverHiker, logged which products independent testers kept landing on, and noted where testers disagreed with each other or with the maker’s numbers.

Then we checked every finalist against its maker’s current product page and a live retail listing on October 21, 2026. We also checked each brand’s recall history with the Consumer Product Safety Commission, which changed two of our picks. The other seven picks are researched rather than tested by us, and each section says which is which. We have a MagGo 2 Pro on the way for review and will update this guide as our own results come in.

The picks below run from a $30 pocket battery to a $250 bank with a wall outlet built in. Before you buy, know the two numbers that matter most: how much your devices draw when they charge, and whether you plan to fly with the battery. Output figures in the table are the makers’ ratings. Prices were checked on October 21, 2026 at the retailer named in each pick’s section, and power banks go on sale often, so check before you buy.

Anker’s Prime Power Bank (26K, 300W), model A110A, is the one power bank we’d buy if we could only own one. It’s expensive, at $229.99 at Anker as of October 26, 2026 (a sale price; Anker shows $30.01 off), and Macworld found it for $199.98 at Amazon in July. What you get for that is a battery with no real ceiling for everyday gear. Anker rates each of its two USB-C ports at 140W and the bank at 300W total, so it can fast-charge a 16-inch MacBook Pro and a second laptop at the same time and still run a phone off the 22.5W USB-A port.

Most banks make you wait as long to refill them as they took to drain. Anker rates the Prime at 50 percent in 13 minutes and 80 percent in 40 minutes, Anker’s page doesn’t say which setup those figures assume, but its fastest refill uses both USB-C ports at once with two 140W chargers, so expect slower results from a single charger. A built-in display shows the remaining charge and what each port is drawing, and the capacity is tuned for flying. At 26,250mAh, it comes in at 99.75Wh, just under the 100Wh line airlines use for carry-on batteries that don’t need approval. Expert Reviews, Engadget, and Macworld all ranked it at or near the top of their 2026 power bank roundups.

The trade is size. At 1.32 pounds, per Anker, it’s a bag battery, and if you only ever charge a phone, the UGREEN 20,000mAh bank below does that job for a quarter of the price. Check the model number before you buy, too. Anker also sold an older Prime 27,650mAh 250W bank (model A1340), which shows as sold out on Anker’s site, and the A110A is also sold in a bundle with a charging base. This recommendation covers the A110A power bank on its own.

A 20,000mAh battery is the size that covers the most situations without becoming a burden. It’s enough to recharge most phones more than once, top off a tablet, or buy a laptop a couple more hours, and it still slides into a backpack’s laptop sleeve. UGREEN’s Nexode Power Bank 20,000mAh 130W (model PB721) is the one we’d buy at that size. It costs $59.99 at UGREEN as of October 21, 2026, a sale price against a $89.99 list price, and UGREEN rates it for 130W of total output across two USB-C ports.

This is the successor to the 20,000mAh, 100W UGREEN bank that earned our travel award last time, and the predecessor’s results are why it’s back in that slot. In our testing, that model took an iPhone 16 Pro Max from empty to 55 percent in 30 minutes and to full in about an hour and a half, with 66 percent of the bank’s own charge left afterward. It also fully recharged a 2024 iPad Pro in two hours with 36 percent to spare. We haven’t run the same tests on the 130W version yet, so treat those as results from the previous model, not this one.

The feature we’d miss most on any other bank is the screen. It shows the remaining charge plus the voltage and current going to each device, so you can tell at a glance whether your laptop is actually fast charging or trickling along on a bad cable. The bank weighs about 480 grams, per UGREEN, and there’s no built-in cable, so pack a USB-C cable rated for 100W if you plan to charge a laptop with it. UGREEN’s US page names two USB-C ports; some international listings add a USB-A port, so check the listing if you still need one.

A magnetic battery snaps onto the back of an iPhone and charges it with no cable at all, which is why it’s the kind of power bank people actually carry every day. Anker’s MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro (model A110R) is the one to get right now. It costs $109.99 at Anker as of October 21, 2026, and it was $87.99 at Amazon during October’s Prime Big Deal Days. It’s built on Qi2.2, a newer version of the magnetic wireless standard that grew out of Apple’s MagSafe, and Anker rates it at up to 25W wirelessly and 45W through its USB-C port.

That 25W figure only applies to the iPhone 16 lineup and newer. Per Macworld, the iPhone 15 and older, plus the iPhone 17e, charge at 15W and the iPhone Air at 20W, and Anker excludes the iPhone 16e, which lacks MagSafe magnets. Macworld’s reviewer charged an iPhone 16 Pro to 50 percent in 30 minutes with the fan running. Faster wireless charging means more heat, and Anker’s answer is a small built-in fan with Cool and Silent modes. Reviewers at Macworld and Digital Camera World both found it audible up close, and Digital Camera World’s tester noticed the phone still got warm. If it bothers you on a nightstand, Silent mode turns the fan off.

The rest of the package earns the price. A fold-out kickstand props the phone up for video calls or watching something on a flight, the display shows battery level, temperature, and battery health, and 45W input means the bank itself refills quickly. Anker says it’s designed for Apple devices, so Android owners should look elsewhere, even though one reviewer did get a Pixel to charge on it. If you’d rather have a built-in cable than a kickstand, Ugreen’s MagFlow Pro is the rival Macworld compared it against. We have a MagGo 2 Pro on the way for a full review and will update this section with our own results.

Our previous multipurpose pick was the Anker PowerCore Fusion 10000. Anker’s current US lineup leads with a newer version, and the idea is unchanged. The Anker Power Bank (10K, Fusion, Built-In Cable), model A1637, is a 10,000mAh battery with folding wall prongs, so it works as a wall charger when you’re near an outlet and a power bank when you aren’t.

The new version adds a built-in USB-C cable, which fixes a familiar power bank problem: having the battery but not the cord. There’s also a second USB-C port for charging two devices at once, and Anker rates the output at up to 30W. That’s plenty for a phone, earbuds, or a tablet, but slow for a laptop. It costs $52.99 at Anker as of October 21, 2026, on sale, and B&H listed it for $44.99 the same day.

Like the model it replaces, it’s heavier than a normal wall charger, and in a loose hotel outlet, the weight can pull it partway out. If that happens, plug it into a power strip instead. For anyone who travels light, losing a separate charger from the bag is worth the trade.

If you work from coffee shops, libraries, or airport gates, the power bank that matters is the one that keeps your laptop alive until an outlet frees up. UGREEN’s Nexode 25,000mAh 145W Power Bank has been our pick for that since we tested it, and it’s still on sale at $79.99 at UGREEN as of October 21, 2026. UGREEN rates the battery at about 90Wh, which leaves it comfortably under the 100Wh limit for carry-on bags.

After using it regularly for months, it never let us down. It extended a 16-inch MacBook Pro by a few hours and fully recharged an iPhone 15 Pro Max and a Nintendo Switch OLED. It did run warm when a laptop drew power from it for long stretches, though never hot enough to worry us. A side display shows the remaining charge, so you know whether to plug it in before you leave.

One thing to know about the numbers: UGREEN’s product name says 145W, while its own product images list 140W, 65W, and 22.5W for the three ports. Either way, the main USB-C port has enough power to fast-charge most laptops, and the other two cover a phone and earbuds at the same time. The one thing it can’t do is run a laptop that only charges from its own AC adapter. For that, see the Goal Zero below.

Some gear still only charges from its own power brick: older laptops, camera battery chargers, a drone charger. The Goal Zero Sherpa 100AC gives you a real AC outlet, rated at 100W, in a bank that stays under the 100Wh carry-on limit. It costs $249.95 at Goal Zero and B&H as of October 21, 2026. Goal Zero shows that as a discount from $274.85.

The current model is the fourth-generation Sherpa 100AC, part number 22200. Beyond the outlet, it has two USB-C ports rated at 60W and 100W, two USB-A ports, and a 15W wireless pad on top. Goal Zero has sold several Sherpa generations under nearly the same name, and the previous one, model 22050, had a much weaker 5W wireless pad. Make sure the listing says 4th generation or 22200.

Know a couple of things before you buy. The AC outlet is modified sine wave rather than pure sine, which is fine for most chargers but can make some motors and audio gear hum or run poorly. Owner ratings are also mixed: REI showed a 3.9-star average across 63 reviews in October 2026, with some owners describing port and display failures. Goal Zero is now owned by BioLite, which bought the brand in April 2025 and has kept its products on sale. If you need more than about 100W of AC power, a portable power station is the better tool.

Our previous outdoor pick, Goal Zero’s Venture 75, was sold out at Goal Zero and Backcountry when we checked in October 2026, with no successor announced. For hiking and backpacking, we’d now carry the Nitecore NB10000 Gen 4. It’s a 10,000mAh battery that weighs about 5 ounces and carries an IPX7 rating, meaning it’s built to survive brief submersion, so rain, a creek crossing, or a soggy tent won’t kill it. It costs $83.95 at Garage Grown Gear as of October 21, 2026.

Weight is the whole point here. Most of the other 10,000mAh banks in CleverHiker’s 2026 testing weighed between 6.6 and 8.5 ounces, and on a multi-day trip, those extra ounces add up. Nitecore gets the weight down with a carbon fiber composite shell and silicon-carbon cells, and the bank has two USB-C ports with pass-through charging, so you can recharge it and your phone from one wall plug at a trailhead motel. It was the top backpacking pick in CleverHiker’s power bank testing this year.

It’s not a fast charger. Nitecore rates it at 22.5W, which is fine for a phone, a headlamp, or a GPS messenger but slow for a tablet. Like every power bank, it also delivers less than its label suggests, because energy is lost converting the cells’ voltage up to USB levels. If you’d rather recharge from the sun on longer trips, see our guide to the best solar power banks.

Sometimes you just want a cheap battery that won’t let you down. The Anker Zolo Power Bank (10K, 30W), model A1688, costs $29.99 at Anker as of October 21, 2026, and Amazon has sold it for about $22 in sales this summer. It has a 5.4-inch USB-C cable built in, a second USB-C port for another device, and enough capacity to get most people through a long day of heavy phone use.

We replaced our previous budget pick, a 10,000mAh INIU bank with a paw-print light, because it appears to match the INIU BI-B41 that the Consumer Product Safety Commission recalled in December 2025 after reports of overheating and fires. Only certain serial numbers were affected, but we’d rather not recommend a model with a recall on its record. If you already own one, check the serial number printed on the bank against INIU’s recall page.

Anker had recalls of its own in 2025, and the model number matters with this brand. The 10K Zolo we recommend here, the A1688, isn’t on either recall list. The Zolo 20K with built-in cables (models A1681 and A1689) was recalled in September 2025, so don’t assume the bigger Zolo is the same product. Check the model number printed on the back of any Anker bank you already own.

Every pick from our previous version of this guide either changed or left. Here’s what happened to each.

A power bank is a battery plus the electronics that turn its power into something your devices can use. These are the specs that separate a good one from a disappointing one.

Capacity is listed in milliamp-hours (mAh), measured at the cells’ own voltage, about 3.7 volts for most lithium-ion banks. Your phone charges at 5 volts or more, and converting between the two loses energy as heat. That’s why a 10,000mAh bank won’t charge a phone with a 5,000mAh battery twice. In Macworld’s 2026 capacity tests, 10,000mAh banks averaged 174 percent of a phone battery, not 200 percent.

Watt-hours (Wh) are the more useful number, because they don’t depend on voltage and they’re what airlines use. Multiply mAh by the cell voltage and divide by 1,000: a 20,000mAh bank at 3.7 volts is about 74Wh. As a rough guide, 10,000mAh covers a day of phone use, 20,000mAh covers a weekend or a tablet, and 25,000mAh or more is for laptops.

Output, in watts, decides how fast a bank charges your devices. Most current phones fast charge at 20W to 45W, tablets want 30W or so, and laptops need 45W to 140W depending on the model. Look for USB Power Delivery (USB PD) on the USB-C ports, since that’s the standard laptops and iPhones use, and check whether the advertised maximum comes from one port or is split across several. A bank rated for 100W total might only give 60W to each of two laptops.

Wireless power banks use the Qi standard. Qi2 added MagSafe-style magnets that line the phone up with the charging coil, and Qi2.2 raised the speed to 25W. Every iPhone since the iPhone 12 (except the 16e) has the magnets; Android support is spotty, and some phones need a magnetic case. Wireless charging is convenient but less efficient than a cable, so it gets more heat and fewer total charges out of the same battery. Our explainer on wired vs. wireless charging goes deeper, and our MagSafe accessories guide covers the cases, mounts, and wallets that use the same magnets.

Power banks have to go in your carry-on, never checked luggage. The FAA allows any number of batteries up to 100Wh, and up to two between 101Wh and 160Wh with airline approval. Airlines added rules of their own this year. Southwest allows one power bank per passenger as of April 2026, American and Delta cap passengers at two banks of 100Wh or less as of May 2026, and several airlines now bar power banks from overhead bins. International aviation rules adopted in March 2026 also limit passengers to two and ban recharging a power bank in flight. Rules are still changing, so check your airline’s page before you fly. Every pick in this guide is under 100Wh.

Power bank recalls have become common enough that checking is worth a minute. In 2025, Anker recalled more than 1.1 million PowerCore 10000 banks (model A1263) in June and about 481,000 more banks across five models in September. INIU recalled about 210,000 BI-B41 banks in December 2025. Recalls usually cover specific model and serial numbers, which are printed on the bank itself. Search the Consumer Product Safety Commission’s recall site for your brand.

Whatever you own, stop using a bank that’s swollen, cracked, or too hot to hold, and don’t charge one on a bed or couch where heat can’t escape. Buy from brands that sell under their own name and have a working support page, since they’re the ones that issue recalls and replacements when something goes wrong.

The Anker Prime Power Bank (26K, 300W). It costs $229.99 at Anker as of October 2026, can fast-charge two laptops at once, and stays just under the 100Wh carry-on limit. For less money, the UGREEN Nexode 20,000mAh 130W ($59.99) still charges a laptop, and iPhone owners who want a magnetic battery should get the Anker MagGo Power Bank 2 Pro.

As of October 2026, a good 10,000mAh bank from a known brand costs $25 to $60. Magnetic wireless banks cost $60 to $150. High-output laptop banks run $80 to $230, and banks with a built-in AC outlet cost $200 or more.

Yes, in your carry-on only. The FAA allows batteries up to 100Wh without approval, and some airlines, including Southwest, American, and Delta, now limit how many you can bring and where you can stow them. Check your airline’s rules before you fly.

Find the model number and serial number printed on the bank, then search the Consumer Product Safety Commission’s recall site and the maker’s support page. Recent recalls include Anker models A1263, A1257, A1647, A1652, A1681, and A1689 and the INIU BI-B41, though some recalls cover only certain serial numbers.

MagSafe is Apple’s magnetic charging system for iPhones. Qi2 is the open standard built on it, and Qi2.2 raised its top speed to 25W. A Qi2.2 power bank like the Anker MagGo 2 Pro charges an iPhone 16 or newer at up to 25W and most older iPhones at 15W.

No. Your phone controls how much power it accepts, and a power bank from a reputable brand only supplies what the phone asks for. If a bank delivers power badly or develops a fault, recent phones will slow down or stop charging and warn you.

Warm is normal, because some energy is lost as heat while charging, especially wirelessly. Hot is not. If a bank or your phone gets too hot to hold comfortably, unplug everything, move the bank away from anything flammable, and stop using it if it happens again.

Most power banks keep useful capacity for a few years of regular use, and many makers rate their cells for several hundred full cycles. Storing a bank about half charged in a cool place helps it age more slowly. Replace one that swells, no longer holds a charge, or gets unusually hot.

A dead phone at the wrong moment is more than an inconvenience. It can leave you unable to call for help, find your way home, or pull up a boarding pass. Pick a bank sized for what you actually carry, check its model number against recent recalls, and charge it before you need it. Then stop worrying about the battery icon.

#4563. Genshin Impact Version Luna 4 introduces three new playable characters, including the long-awaited Moon Maiden Columbina

Dubbed Traveler on a Winter's Night, Luna 4 introduces three new playable characters, and brings back The Lantern Rite event to celebrate the beginning of the new year.

Finally, you can look forward to the new Classic Mode in Miliastra Wonderland, which lets you invite select Teyvat characters to join you.

#4562. "I was a real anglophile": A Canadian's obsession with Viz magazine is why Grand Theft Auto: London's streets are covered in dogs' eggs

Sadly, there are no vibrating bum-faced goats

The Grand Theft Auto games have a long history of being made by developers who live far from their source material. The original game and its mainline sequels have all been led by Rockstar North, housed in a decidedly not US-based Edinburgh. But, even when it came to a comparatively local GTA mission pack set in London, the team chose instead to ask their newly acquired Canadian team to handle the development work. It didn't matter that only a few people on the team had actually been to London before.

Yet, that distance, as proven across the series grants the developers a more incisive view of a place, one that focuses more on a setting's cultural rendering than its reality.

As evidenced by Grand Theft Auto: London's dog shit.

Ahead of the launch of GTA 6, the London culture-focused publication Shortlist has published a making of Grand Theft Auto: London by Jeremy Peel, that writer what's often spotted hanging around RPS's backdoor looking for a way onto our rarefied pages. It covers the unlikely story of how a newly acquired Canadian studio was renamed Rockstar Canada and put to work turning Rockstar's open world crime 'em up into a game set in London during the swinging sixties.

It's an odd game to look at. Built with the same tools as the original game, the developers managed to roughly recreate the geography of London and make it so cars drove on the left-hand side of the road, with drivers even getting in through the correct side to access a right-hand drive steering wheel, but the streets remain hopelessly American. There are no curves to the roads, instead they are wide lanes with right-angled corners like you'd see in New York and LA.

Still, despite being Canadians working on a British game engine built to make games set in America, there was a cultural export that left its mark on the team: Viz magazine. The long-running parody of The Beano had somehow made its way over to Canada and into the hands of artist Ray Larabie. “I was a real anglophile,” he said. “I knew all about Newcastle upon Tyne and the curry shops and the Kray twins from Viz magazine. I had this twisted view of England. I remember going over there and asking normal people about Viz magazine. And they were like, ‘We’re adults, we don't read that. That is for juvenile young men.’”

I don't know how the creators of the vibrating bum-faced goats, who named their 2012 annual the Cleveland Steamer, or has a long-running character called Johnny Fartpants could be called juvenile. But, then, there's no accounting for taste.

Larabie told Shortlist he managed to get a particular detail from Viz's pages into GTA: London “London was famous for having dog shit everywhere in the 60s. So I put little dogs’ eggs, as they call them in Viz, on the sidewalk. And set it so that if you see a poop on the sidewalk, you can do a handbrake on it and leave a big brown skid mark. That’s my only innovation I ever made in videogames.”

I recommend reading the full making of feature, but it's that globehopping thread of culture that I'm particularly fascinated by. There is certainly an argument that stories should be told by the people closest to them, but something the GTA games have consistently revealed isn't the actuality of the countries they're set in but the cultural lens through which we view them. While also true of the earlier games, from GTA 3 onwards, Rockstar's references to the films they're inspired by become more and more overt. Badfellas posters litter Liberty City in GTA 3, scenes in Vice City are lifted straight from Scarface, and missions in GTA 4 recreate the heists in Heat.

If I had to pick the cultural touchpoints that represent the UK, I wouldn't know where to start and there are so many conflicting choices you could make: Fawlty Towers, Our Friends in the North, Mr Bates vs The Post Office. But an outsider likely has a narrower field to pluck from.

Which makes the fact that the field includes Viz and its long-running joke of dogs' eggs is a real joy.

Sign in and join us on our journey to discover strange and compelling PC games.

#4561. Acura Files Trademarks That Hint at New SUVs and Trim Levels on the Way

With two new SUVs and a redesigned RDX in the works, Acura models might be getting a bit more rugged.

Acura has big plans for its SUV lineup in the coming years, with three new models bound for North America starting in 2028. Two of those models will be expanding the current range, and a new patent filing with the United States Patent and Trademark Office (USPTO) hints at what those vehicles could be called.

Earlier this month, Acura filed patents on four trademarked names: TDX, LDX, TYPE-TRAIL, and TYPE T. Based on the company’s current usage of Type S, it stands to reason that the latter two trademarks refer to potential new trim levels for existing Acura products.

Parent company Honda has been having success with its TrailSport lineup of products, and Acura is likely looking to build out its own off-road-oriented offerings. Along with an all-new replacement for the current RDX, it has also said that a more adventuresome SUV will be joining the fleet in 2028, with a larger eight-passenger SUV sitting above the MDX as the largest Acura ever built.

See all results for used cars for sale near g2g 1r7

Acura’s naming structure is pretty clinical, setting aside the Integra hatchback. If the company ends up going with Type T as a trim for a more off-roadish MDX, then the new off-road-oriented SUV is probably going to be the TDX. And considering that Acura’s flagship sedan used to be the RL, the LDX will likely be the eight-passenger machine.

Powering these vehicles will be a couple of new hybrid engine offerings, one of them a new V-6 hybrid intended for the larger SUVs. There’s also a two-motor hybrid that’s set to debut in that next-generation RDX.

All of this gives Acura an answer to some of rival Lexus’ more rugged offerings, like the Land Cruiser-based GX. Acuras have historically been more about on-tarmac performance, but the market has an appetite for light off-roaders. There probably won't be an Integra Type-Trail anytime soon, but a chunkier RDX is probably on the horizon.

#4560. Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: The Good and the Hard of Life on GLP-1s

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

“Years of diets that worked for a couple of months, then gave all the weight back and more. Now the food noise has gone quiet,” a GLP-1 user shared.

In June, our brilliant News team published a piece about actor Mayim Bialik and her challenging experience with GLP-1 side effects. It did well, like many of the celebrity + health condition + human-interest angle features we create tend to do with our audience (that’s you all reading this!). Not a massive viral hit by any measure, but a strong performer on the traffic front.

Jump forward nearly 4 months; our equally brilliant social media team posts the piece on Facebook, leading with one of Bialik’s quotes from the article: “GLP-1s have helped people in serious need. Of that I am certain. But nobody talks much about what happens when it goes wrong.”

The fuse was lit. Nearly 12k likes, 1k reposts, and 3.5k comments later, the team had their most engaged post of the year by some margin. The irony is not lost on me that Bialik’s quote, about how rarely people talk about side effects, sparked such a deep conversation about that very thing.

GLP-1s are a big topic for Healthline. They drive traffic, they drive revenue, and they offer a rich seam of human-centric storytelling for our teams. Even so, no one predicted the post would catch fire the way it did. Our social team’s read is that a few things stacked up: Mayim Bialik is a name our Facebook audience knows, the topic is one where nearly everyone has a stake, and a busy thread tends to get shown to more people, who then add more comments. That’s the hypothesis. What we’ve consistently seen is that posts that lead with a quote get more people talking than those that lead with a headline. You may have noticed this article takes the same approach.

We also know that the comment sections on social media aren’t always the most hospitable of environments, particularly in the health space. What stood out about the lion’s share of the comments we read was how constructive the discourse was. People were leading with deeply personal and often moving perspectives about the life changing impact (for good and bad) of GLP-1s. In turn, they were met with equally balanced and thoughtful responses.

Many of the comments expanded on Bialik’s experience, detailing long and difficult battles with a range of side effects. But plenty told the other side of the story: years of failed diets and struggles with chronic conditions leading to despondency, side effects that went on to ease with time, and life changing results that were ultimately worth the cost.

The line that opened this piece could have come from any of those commenters. It didn’t. It came from Kitty Hough, who is 75 and lives in London. Eagle-eyed readers will have spotted that she has the same surname as me – that’s because she’s my mom. She’s one of two people closer to the Healthline home who spoke to me for this piece and whose stories echo the Facebook post thread, with the good and the hard bits side by side.

Kitty was a hockey player and distance runner as a girl, the kind who could eat what she liked without it showing. Then came children, and a weight that crept up year after year, with decades of diets that “worked for a couple of months only to be back to my former weight and then some.” In late 2024, after a lot of research, she decided to try tirzepatide.

Since then, Kitty has lost 56 pounds. She says her joints hurt less, which she puts down to reduced inflammation. She is more mobile. The constant chatter about food has faded. She thinks harder before she eats now.

“I am a strong believer that obesity is a disease and in my case certainly not the result of overindulgence or laziness,” she says. “GLP-1s have greatly improved my health and self-esteem. After years of struggling with my weight, I have finally found a way to solve the problem.”

Kitty will also tell you what it cost. Nausea. Constipation. Sulfur-tasting burps and rough nights of indigestion. Initial hair loss that has since grown back, and loose skin. Muscle loss. And she misses some of the true joy of cooking and eating food.

But despite bumps in her journey, Kitty remains convinced that the benefits of GLP-1s for her life and health outweigh any drawbacks she’s experienced. “I am conscious that I will probably be on this drug for a long time if not for life. It is truly a miracle drug.”

I also spoke to Ann Marie Maguire, AGNP-C, RN, TCTCN, a nurse practitioner specializing in transplantation and cellular therapy, the sister of Healthline’s Chief Business Officer Christine Macguire, and someone who has lost over 50lbs since starting GLP-1s in 2022.

“I struggled with my weight since my teens and always believed I should be able to manage it on my own. But in my 30s, my knees were hurting just getting out of bed or walking to dinner. I realized how much my weight was affecting my quality of life. Starting a GLP-1 in 2022, alongside healthier eating and exercise, made a tremendous difference. My only regret is not starting sooner,” said Ann Marie.

As a healthcare professional, Ann Marie also identified cost as a major factor that can’t be ignored regarding GLP-1s and their impact.

“There’s understandable debate about the long-term use and cost of these medications,” she says. “But we also need to consider the health and financial burden of obesity, including cardiovascular complications, hospitalizations, surgeries and ongoing medications. The cost conversation should account for the potential benefits of preventing or reducing those complications.”

Ann Marie points to a frustration many patients will recognize: reaching a healthier weight can become the reason coverage gets cut off. “We don’t automatically stop blood pressure medication because a patient’s readings improve,” she says. The treatment may be what’s keeping the condition controlled. Obesity, she argues, deserves the same approach. “Reaching a goal weight can mean treatment is working, and decisions about continuing it should reflect the patient’s ongoing needs, benefits and side effects.”

It’s important to note that this article looks at the experiences of only a handful of people out of the millions who now take GLP-1s. Like all drugs, these medications are not right for everyone. Side effects are common; some of them can be serious, and both the long-term impact and what happens when people stop are still being worked out. Ultimately, your care team, with knowledge of your medical history, is a far better guide than a comment thread.

Yet the evidence now reaches well beyond the scales: large trials have linked semaglutide to fewer heart attacks and strokes in people with heart disease and slower kidney decline in people with diabetes and kidney disease, and tirzepatide became the first FDA-approved drug for moderate to severe sleep apnea in adults with obesity.

Stories like Kitty’s, Ann Marie’s, Mayim Bialik’s, and those of the 3.5k commenters on the Facebook post aren’t proof of what will happen to you one way or another. But for a lot of people, my mom included, these drugs have changed what an ordinary day looks like, and in many cases changed what their future can look like.

One of the most significant drug developments of the century so far? Hard to argue with.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Wednesday in our Healthline Wellness Wire newsletter.

#4558. Analogue is reviving its Mega SG and Super Nt consoles, and they'll play all your old Sega and Nintendo cartridges with some modern enhancements

Analogue - creator of various fancy retro-focused game consoles - has detailed its newly announced Mega Sg+ and Super Nt+: revised editions of its earlier Mega Sg and Super Nt machines, capable of playing original Sega and Nintendo cartridges with modern advancements.

Analogue's first stab at the Super Nt launched in 2018, while the Mega Sg arrived one year later. Both machines' imminent upgrades feature, as per the company's announcement, "an FPGA designed in-house from scratch and backed by more than a decade of development. Rock solid. No emulation. Purpose-built 1080p for 2D gaming. Zero lag. Total accuracy."

As for compatibility, the Mega SG+ is said to be 100 percent compatible "with the 2,180+ Sega Genesis, Mega Drive, Master System, Game Gear, Mycard Adapter, Mark III/SG1000 game cartridge library", while the Super Nt+ is compatible with over 2,200 Super Nintendo and Super Famicom games.

Both consoles sport a redesigned "industrial" look, and support wireless controllers via bluetooth and 2.4GHz connectivity. Additionally, users can hot-swap cartridges without having to power down the machines - they simply need to quit their current game and switch them out. Furthermore, it's possible to power the consoles on and off using a controller now - and the required USB-C power supply and cable are included in the box.

If your interest is piqued, you'll be able to pre-order them on the 19th October via the Analogue website, with consoles shipping this December. The Mega SG+ is available for $219.99 in three variants - Japan, EU, and US - while the $219.99 Super Nt+ comes in Classic, SF, and Black.

#4557. 14 Best Airbnbs in the South of France for Bucolic Romance (2026)

The South of France has long been shorthand for bucolic romance. The region has drawn painters, film stars, and sun-starved travelers for generations, seducing them with endless vineyards and lavender fields, sleepy medieval villages, and its glittering Riviera. From Provence to the Côte d’Azur, the south of France offers a storybook mélange of rustic farmhouses, centuries-old castles and estates, along with honey-hued stone houses tucked into timeless hilltop towns.

Whether you fancy swimming in a pool besides your own château, late evening pétanque games at a centuries-old castle, gathering a group of friends at a hillside estate, or cozying up in a rustically romantic village apartment, Airbnb has options for turning every south of France fantasy into a reality. Here are some of the most extraordinary homes for rent in the region—spots so tempting, you might just want to cancel your return ticket and settle in for a year in Provence.

Perks: Mountain views, lake access, sauna, balcony, hot tub, indoor and outdoor fireplaces, pool table

There’s plenty of room for up to 10 guests to spread out in this five bedroom home with a generous living space that includes three bathrooms and a pool table. Tucked in the hilly countryside, proximity to both Alpine and Nordic skiing is the property’s main draw for those looking to hit the slopes. After a day spent outside, both the indoor fireplace or sauna provide a perfect spot to warm up, though you could also enjoy the fresh air on the expansive balcony while keeping toasty by the outdoor fireplace or hot tub. From $1,048 per night.

Perks: Private backyard, fireplace, sound system, fully-equipped kitchen, security system, free parking, cleaning available for a fee
