#4556. 2026 Gifts of the Year: Perfect presents for anyone on your list

Published Oct 27, 2026 7:58 AM EDT

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn more ›

Choosing great gifts is a skill. It’s something you only get to practice a few times each year, but we live in every day. The shopping team here at Popular Science prides itself on its ability to scour the broad landscape of products and hone in on the perfect present for just about anyone. And while we evaluate hundreds of products every year, some clearly stand out as ideal gifting candidates. We’ve compiled a group of ideal products that are guaranteed to make the recipient feel at least a little bad because you have thoroughly defeated them in the gift-giving game.

Popular Science‘s sibling brands have also selected unique products for their own Gift of the Year awards. See their best-in-class gift selections for this holiday season at PCMag, CNET, Mashable, Lifehacker, and ZDNET.

Waterproof trail shoes usually look and feel too bulky and—let’s be honest—kind of nerdy. The Speedgoat 7 GTX keeps the regular Speedgoat 7’s supercritical EVA midsole and Vibram Megagrip outsole, then wraps them in a GORE-TEX Invisible Fit membrane and a recycled Cordura upper. It’s a lot of technical materials, but they’re wrapped in a design that actually looks cool with a pair of jeans. Toggle laces work with gloves on, there’s a loop for attaching gaiters (sold separately), and reflective details run all the way around for dark November runs. We recommend going up a half-size from your giftee’s typical number for a better fit.

This little device gives the gift of time that would otherwise be spent doomscrolling. Phone screen-time limits are easy to dismiss because the override button sits right under your thumb. Brick moves the override into another room. The $59 block has no battery, just an NFC chip. You pick the apps to lock in the free iPhone or Android app, tap your phone to the Brick, and those apps stay locked until you walk back and tap again. One Brick works with multiple phones, so a household can share it, and the app includes five emergency unlocks per device for real emergencies. It requires iOS 18 or Android 12 and later. Brick is also HSA and FSA eligible, which makes it a stocking stuffer you can buy with pretax money.

Insta360 made its name with 360-degree and action cameras, and the Luna Ultra is its first pocket gimbal camera, co-engineered with Leica. The main camera pairs a Leica Summicron lens with a 1-inch sensor that records up to 8K at 30fps, and a second telephoto camera on a 1/1.3-inch sensor handles zoomed shots and background blur. A three-axis gimbal keeps footage steady while you walk. The 2-inch OLED touchscreen pops off and works as a wireless monitor and remote, so you can frame a shot of yourself from across the room. It’s a serious content creation tool that’s fun to use, which is a rarity.

You don’t need a massive backyard to have a true outdoor pizza oven. The propane cooker weighs 26.4 pounds and measures 16.9 inches wide, yet Gozney rates it to reach 950 degrees Fahrenheit, which is more than enough to put a perfect char on a true Neopolitan style pie. Gozney’s lateral rolling flame wraps heat over the top of the pizza while a 12mm stone floor stores heat for the crust. An infrared thermometer makes a smart add-on gift, since the oven won’t tell you when the stone is ready. You’ll selfishly also probably get some free pizza out of it.

A 65-inch black rectangle is a lot of nothing to stare at when the TV is off. LG’s Gallery TV LX7B fills that space with art. The 4K Mini LED set has a matte screen that cuts reflections and a snow-white magnetic frame that snaps onto the bezel, and when it’s idle it can display pieces from LG Gallery+, a library of more than 5,000 works (some features require a Gallery+ subscription). It runs webOS 26 with Google Gemini built in. Whether the receiver is a film buff or a die-hard design enthusiast, this TV fits the bill.

This is a product we use every single day. The Theragun Mini Plus is Therabody’s smallest heated massage gun, and its attachment warms up to 131 degrees Fahrenheit across three heat levels. It has three speeds, two buttons, USB-C charging, and a travel lock that keeps it from switching on inside a gym bag. Therabody says heat plus percussion works three times faster than percussion alone, based on its own lab testing. You can run either therapy by itself, too. Don’t think this is only for hardcore gym rats. It’s great for people who just want to relax of even improve their sleep.

If you know a budding streamer or podcaster, this is a gift that grows with them. The Razer Seiren V3 Pro has both USB-C and XLR outputs, so it plugs straight into a PC now and into a mixer later when they upgrade their setup. It’s a dynamic mic with a 30mm cardioid capsule, a type that picks up less of a noisy room than most condensers. Onboard processing runs AI noise removal, a compressor, and a limiter on the mic itself, and Windows users can record 32-bit float audio through Razer Synapse. A tap-to-mute button, an RGB ring that shows mute and gain status, and an adjustable desk arm all come in the box.

The nightstand device, built for kids roughly 4 to 10, walks one child through morning, after-school, and bedtime routines a step at a time with visual prompts that work for kids who can’t read yet. It also handles alarm clock, sound machine, nightlight, and OK-to-wake duty. It has no camera or microphone, and kids can’t browse the web or download apps on it. Skylight sells it for $139.99, and the optional $39-per-year Buddy Plus plan adds rewards, timers, and read-aloud messages.

Fast wireless charging makes a magnetic battery pack hot, and heat makes phones slow their charging down. Anker’s fix in the MagGo 2 Pro is a tiny fan. The 10,000mAh pack charges at up to 25W over Qi2.2, and Anker says the fan keeps its back under about 97 degrees Fahrenheit while it works. Anker rates it to take an iPhone 17 Pro to 50 percent in about 25 minutes. A screen shows battery level and charging speed, a built-in stand props the phone up for in-flight movies, and the USB-C port refills the pack at 45W. It costs $109.99. Older phones, including the iPhone 15 Pro, top out at 15W.

Google hadn’t released a new standalone smart speaker since the Nest Audio in 2020. The Google Home Speaker is built around Gemini for Home instead of the old Assistant, so you can stack several requests into one sentence or correct yourself mid-command. It plays 360-degree sound from a 58mm driver, two of them pair with a Google TV Streamer for surround sound, and it doubles as a Thread border router and Matter hub. It costs $99.99 and includes six months of Google Home Premium, which adds Gemini Live and continued conversation. After the trial, Premium runs $10 per month.

A $20 smart plug makes a surprisingly good energy audit. The Shelly Plug Gen4 measures real-time power draw from whatever is plugged into it, so you can see how much that garage fridge or space heater actually pulls. It connects over Matter, Wi-Fi, or Zigbee, works with Apple Home, Google Home, Alexa, SmartThings, and Home Assistant, and can run locally without touching the cloud. It handles loads up to 1,800 watts, with cutoffs for overheating, overcurrent, and voltage problems. Its built-in LED can change color to reflect current energy use. It costs $19.99 in black or white.

If you know someone constantly struggling to make work from the road work, the Sennheiser MOMENTUM 5 Wireless headphones are an audio oasis they will appreciate for years to come. The hybrid noise cancellation, 8-band graphic EQ, Snapdragon Sound, and other personalization features cut the noise and sculpt the sound, perfect for open-office escapees and in-control commuters. And a long-haul battery life delivers up to 57 hours of runtime, so playlists won’t run out somewhere over the Atlantic. Plus, the battery is user-replaceable, reducing e-waste and extending ownership. This is the perfect gift for music-loving control freqs.

Sonos isn’t the only way to fill a house with speakers that talk to each other. Denon’s redesigned Home lineup runs on HEOS, which can link up to 64 speakers across a home, and the three sizes scale with the room. The $399 Home 200 plays stereo from three tweeters and a 4-inch woofer. The $599 Home 400 adds two upward-firing drivers for Dolby Atmos Music, and the $799 Home 600 packs eight drivers, including dual 6.5-inch woofers, each with its own amplifier. All three take Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, USB-C, and aux. The stone and charcoal finishes look more like something from a furniture store than an electronics aisle.

The Bose LifeStyle Ultra Home Theater Set is perfect for homes that struggle with the volume of content and components. From the soundbar to the wireless satellites and subwoofer, everything is compact and design-conscious, perfect for a minimalist who doesn’t want to sacrifice expression. Bose SpeechClarity uses AI enhancement to make dialogue more audible without having to ride the volume constantly. CustomTune calibrates immersion to the room’s actual acoustics. And when it’s not movie night, stream music via AirPlay 2/Google Cast, no app required. Add more speakers later for a multiroom setup, making this a great gift for someone who wants to scale up a whole-home setup without sacrificing space.

2026 Popular Science Home of the Future Awards

39 products that will improve your everyday life.

Stan Horaczek is the executive gear editor at Popular Science. He oversees a team of gear-obsessed writers and editors dedicated to finding and featuring the newest, best, and most innovative gadgets on the market and beyond.

Tony Ware is the Managing Editor, Gear & Commerce for PopSci.com. He’s been writing about how to make and break music since the mid-’90s when his college newspaper said they already had a film critic but maybe he wanted to look through the free promo CDs. Immediately hooked on outlining intangibles, he’s covered everything audio for countless alt. weeklies, international magazines, websites, and heated bar trivia contests ever since.

#4554. Heartopia captures the hearts of Animal Crossing and The Sims fans to become the No.1 free download across 50 countries

Heartopia is officially the first big mobile launch of 2026, as the game grabs the attention of millions worldwide.

Heartopia, the new cosy life sim from XD Games, has officially arrived on mobile, right on schedule. If you’ve been keeping up with Heartopia news, you’re likely already aware that the game has pushed back its Steam release to an indeterminate point in 2026.

Doesn’t look like it mattered much, however, as this has already been a big launch on iOS and Android — it probably helps that PC players aren't being entirely left out of the fun, since the game is already available there via XD's own cross-device platform TapTap.

XD Games announced today that Heartopia has reached the top spot on the App Store’s downloads page for free games, and it managed to do so in more than 50 countries and regions worldwide.

The developer is celebratig by offering players a lot of free stuff. If you're feeling lucky, you’ll find new outfits and furniture in three gacha banners (Gilded Acorn Exhibitions): Polar Hymn, The Frostguard, and Coolplayism.

XD Games says you can expect regular updates to add more content, through Fashionwaves and Festivals. In fact, the first season - Winter Frost - is already penciled in for sometime in late January, and it’s going to add appropriately wintery activities like ice skating and snowman-building.

In February, a My Little Pony collaboration event will kick off, bringing new inspired outfits, and vehicles to the game.

Heartopia is a clever mix of Animal Crossing, and The Sims. It’s a free-to-play life sim where you can create your own character, dress them up, build a home for them (and decorate it), and go about town visiting and playing with other players’ characters.

The game’s main feature is the ability to take on a hobby, and seven of those are available at launch. You can dig deep into each hobby, either alone or with others, and there’s a progression track dedicated to each one.

Whether you’re among the millions who downloaded the game already, or you intend to so soon, we’ve got a set of helpful guides to get you started. You should check out how to unlock and upgrade hobbies in Heartopia first, before you get deep into finding all cooking recipes. And, of course, this wouldn’t be a big game launch without a bunch of Heartopia codes for some free stuff.

#4553. "Too big a risk": Moonlighter devs delay new game's demo for fear of genAI cloning, calling on platforms to "keep strengthening protections for original work"

Digital Sun "concerned" by recent news about genAI rip-offs

Moonlighter and Cataclismo developers Digital Sun Games have postponed a public Steam playtest for their new game ReVamp, for fear of people using generative AI to clone their work before release.

"So many of you signed up for ReVamp's first playtest on Steam (thank you!), but we've decided to start with something more contained on our Discord, with the public playtest coming a bit later," the devs write on MechaHitler.com. "Why? We've been concerned by recent news of games being reverse-engineered, cloned and published before the original is even out. With launch still months away, a large public build of ReVamp felt like too big a risk."

They're now hoping to run a public playtest in January, with the closed Discord showing starting on October 29th. "Showing games early is how indies get seen and find the fans who keep us going, so we hope platforms keep strengthening protections for original work," the developers conclude, without going into details about the kind of protection they'd like to see.

There's been a flurry of stories recently about clones of in-progress or just-released games. It's gotten to the point that people cloning needle-in-haystack games are accusing other people who clone needle-in-haystack games of cloning their clones.

In response, some developers have announced that they're pulling playtests or demos, to lower the risk of their WIP game being fed to a chatbot. Last week, the creators of Drunken Goddess Reflux cancelled a planned demo for fear that it "could be used to quickly imitate or replicate the game's presentation and gameplay with AI or other tools, and that such works could be released before our launch."

Digital Moon cited our Drunken Goddess Reflux story when they mailed me about their decision to delay the demo, earlier today. If I were being ungenerous, I might say that they saw a chance to get some publicity for ReVamp - they're still running some kind of playtest this year, rather than battening down the marketing hatches till the game's release - but I've read a few comparable reactions from other devs over the weekend. The worry seems pretty genuine.

People have been ripping each other's work off since the dawn of capitalism, of course, and I'm wary of making a case for the kind of punitive copyright culture that sees GTA 5 modders getting their wrists slapped for recreating bits of GTA 4.

You could also argue that the flurry of genAI rip-offs is less an indictment of genAI than of a marketplace that thrives on easily appropriated viral gimmicks – the kind of game streamers love to pounce on. I don't want to categorise all games made with streamers in mind as shallow and easily replicated - streaming has facilitated specific kinds of videogame, including devious and slapstick co-op affairs such as Lethal Company and Peak. And I wouldn't class Digital Sun's ReVamp as opportunistic Tuber-bait, in any case - it's a rearrangeable modular tower defender in which you play Dracula. It seems like a substantial piece of work, and the dev's past action-RPGs and strategy games speak for themselves.

#4552. Tested: 2027 BMW iX5 60 xDrive Proves the X5 Makes a Fine EV

Pure electric propulsion transforms BMW’s long-lived SUV, mostly for the better.

It’s no exaggeration to say that the X5 has been a cash-printer for BMW since its 1999 launch. The mid-size SUV earned a reputation as a performance powerhouse and has been particularly successful in the U.S. across what are now five generations. Now, a new EV version is here to take the nameplate into new territory. Sharing its body with the new combustion-powered X5, the 2027 iX5 certainly looks the part. But does it drive like an X5 should?

The 2027 BMW iX5 60 xDrive shares its platform with the combustion version but promises sharp-end propulsion technology. The iX5 will arrive in the U.S. in 60 form, combining an impressively capacious 144-kWh battery pack with two electric motors. Peak power is 570 hp, this accompanied by up to 593 lb-ft of torque, but the killer stat is 450 miles of EPA-rated range. The new 800-volt battery can also be replenished at rates of up to 460 kW if you can find a DC charger capable of providing that. Like the regular X5, the iX5 benefits from a new double-wishbone front suspension. The EV also gets an adaptive chassis system as standard.

Our expert editors test every vehicle we review. Read more about how we test and review cars here.

Powertrain: Dual electric motors, all-wheel drive

Electric vehicles are hardly novel anymore, but BMW wanted the new iX5 to blend in among its internal-combustion peers. Climb into the iX5, and there are only two physical markers that this electric X5 isn’t like the others—no paddle shifters on the steering wheel and no engine start/stop button. Flick the crystal toggle-switch shifter into drive, and as long as it detects the presence of the key, it is ready to go.

The iX5 behaved as I expected while crawling through Laguna Beach, California, on the Pacific Coast Highway during the morning rush hour. With every adjustable drive mode and suspension setup in Comfort, it rode softly and accelerated gently. Despite the SUV’s strong 570 hp and 593 lb-ft of torque, BMW’s throttle mapping fed power smoothly to both electric motors. BMW’s Adaptive Chassis Control Professional system, which includes two-axle air suspension and active roll stabilization, kept the ride glassy through sandy construction zones and freeway expansion joints. Tight metropolitan maneuvers were easily completed thanks to standard rear-wheel steering.

As speed climbed, the iX5’s 6300-pound curb weight began to show. Dips and divots in the road translated into strong impacts on the air suspension. Active air suspension can work a lot of magic, but it can’t cancel the battle of mass and inertia. And while the steering was impressively direct, immediately translating my inputs into directional change, it lacked the weight I expected from a vehicle this size.

With Sport mode engaged, the steering firmed up a little, but I couldn’t shake a disconcerting disconnect between the rate of directional change and the weight of the SUV. Out in the canyon, it felt like the sticky Michelin tires and state-of-the-art suspension were only complying with my dynamic inputs under duress from BMW’s Heart of Joy control module, which actively manages braking, steering, and acceleration inputs.

With a 0–60-mph sprint of 4.4 seconds, the BMW iX5 is obviously quick, courtesy of an asynchronous motor up front and an externally excited synchronous e-motor on the rear axle. But the same gentle acceleration forces I liked around town felt dampened during performance driving scenarios. Instead of the characteristic EV torque impact, launching the iX5 from a standstill felt a little lacking in drama compared with other rapid battery-powered models. EV enthusiasts won’t find that stomach-churning torque hit, but I welcomed the uncharacteristically linear power delivery.

Acceleration forces built the longer I kept my foot planted on the go-pedal, and they didn’t taper off above rapid highway speeds, like in other EVs. The predictable throttle response made getting back on the power during spirited canyon driving feel easy and rewarding. An engineered sound accompanied hard acceleration in Sport mode, akin to a Tron bike or Star Wars TIE fighter.

Regenerative braking has three selectable strengths. I preferred the lowest for its gentle but consistent deceleration, with the highest being almost a one-pedal drive mode but still the requirement to brake manually to come to a complete stop. There is also a true one-pedal mode.

BMW’s underfloor 144-kWh battery pack offers an estimated 450 miles of range, and my test vehicle displayed impressive efficiency over a 300-mile route. While I didn’t get the chance to charge the iX5 myself, BMW offered a live demonstration of a charging speed of over 400 kW. And BMW’s hands-free semi-autonomous cruise control worked well in busy California traffic.

BMW plans to standardize its interior experience with the infotainment and display architecture that debuted in the iX3. Physical iDrive controls are replaced by a fully touchscreen system, with this using a slightly odd-looking angled 17.9-inch central display and a similarly shaped 14.6-inch passenger’s screen.

On the iX5, everything from climate controls to vehicle settings is manipulated through this interface. Pretty much the only physical control to remain is a scrolling volume roller near the gear shifter. The touchscreen responds swiftly and features a permanently rendered menu with icons for climate control, vehicle settings, navigation, and media inputs. But these are just the start of a multistep process to adjust functions. To switch drive modes, I needed to click through two menus before reaching Sport. Seating position changes are now made with a door-mounted button, but lumbar support adjustments and control of the seat heating and ventilation are made on the screen. That was confusing.

The highlight of BMW’s Panoramic iDrive system is a projected instrument cluster displayed across a span of glass above the dashboard at the base of the windshield—a significant improvement over a bulky digital gauge screen. Essential information such as speed and state of charge was perfectly placed where I wanted it, while extraneous information such as altitude and radio station appeared on the passenger’s side of the screen. My biggest complaint with the new iX5 interior is, frankly, its ugly four-spoke steering wheel. It features two vertical spokes, two disconnected horizontal spokes, and attached pods full of haptic controls. It was a busy thing to look at and an even busier one to use.

The tactile experience in my iX5 test car nearly made up for the technological annoyances. Wide, leather seats comfortably cupped my body over six hours of driving, and excellent sound deadening kept the interior close to silent. Alcantara trim on the dashboard and door handles added an extra level of luxury feel. Rear cargo storage is reasonable at 21.3 cubic feet with the rear seats in place, and rear legroom was similarly generous.

The BMW iX5 competes in not one but two arenas. Choosing between an iX5 and a gas-powered X5 largely depends on your budget and affinity for electric vehicles. Both the gas-powered and all-electric versions offer an identical interior experience and similar on-paper performance given the 1000-pound weight differential and 170-hp delta. At a base price of $81,250, the iX5 is a $10,000 supplement over the base model X5 40 at $71,250.

Shopping outside the BMW brand brings stronger competition, with the Porsche Cayenne Electric and the Lucid Gravity being its closest adversaries. The Porsche Cayenne Electric blows BMW out of the water on performance but is more expensive, while the Lucid Gravity is well matched with an $81,750 MSRP and a base model with 628 hp. Volvo’s EX90 is similarly well appointed, with a competitive $79,000 sticker price, but it lacks dynamic verve. Overall, the iX5 offers a solid balance of luxury, performance, and capability that makes it genuinely competitive, so long as you can stomach the infotainment.

BMW’s approach to its next-generation electric vehicles has centered on range, and the results are impressive. An estimated 450 miles is no small feat, but BMW pushed the iX5 to the limit on a hypermiling drive from San Francisco to Newport Beach. All told, the electric SUV managed over 500 miles on a single charge, with 50 miles to spare.

#4551. Raw Denim vs. Regular Denim: Which Is Actually Worth Buying?

Here’s what to know about both kinds of jeans.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

I COME FROM the denim era where streetwear juggernauts like FUBU and Rocawear reigned supreme, along with mall favorites like Abercrombie & Fitch and Hollister. I was in my preteens rocking all these clothing brands and you couldn’t tell me their baggy-stacking-at-the-shoe jeans weren’t the flyest on the market. Since I began working as a fashion editor, I’ve practically worn every pair of jeans in the denim universe. Slim, relaxed, stretch, washed, distressed—you name it, I’ve probably tried it. I’ve even tried my hand at denim-making in Japan, the mecca of old-school, high-quality denim, where I produced my own pair of selvedge jeans. Denim is one of those rare items in a guy’s wardrobe that never goes out of style.

And after years of being in the menswear space, I’ve gotten familiar with the nuances that distinguish one kind of denim from the next. Two worth knowing are regular denim and raw denim. Raw denim usually looks darker, stiffer, and more uniform in color, while regular denim has a softer feel and a more broken-in appearance with visible fading or whiskering from factory washing and finishing. Those differences also affect how each pair feels when you first put it on and how it changes over the years. Here’s what to know about both and which one deserves a spot in your rotation.

Regular jeans are usually made by weaving cotton yarn into denim, then cutting fabric into patterns and sewing them together to create the finished garment. These jeans have already gone through washing or finishing treatments to soften the fabric and give them that broken-in look, so you don’t have to spend weeks waiting for them to feel comfortable. If I want to throw on a pair of jeans and get on with my day, this is the kind I’m reaching for. I also like that you can find them in practically every fit and wash, whether you’re after a clean, dark indigo pair or something faded and lived-in. Most everyday options from brands like Levi’s and Wrangler run roughly $40 to $100, while designer and premium styles can cost considerably more. For me, prewashed denim makes the most sense when you want that comfortable feel from the jump. There’s nothing wrong with skipping the break-in period and getting straight to the good part.

I like raw denim because you get to see how jeans change the more you wear them. Unlike prewashed jeans, raw denim typically hasn’t been washed after the dyeing and manufacturing process, so it typically arrives stiff, dark, and pretty rigid. One thing to watch out for, though, is dye transfer. Indigo can rub off onto lighter-colored clothes, shoes, and other materials, including suede. I’ve had this happen before, so I'd be mindful of what you wear with a fresh pair. But that’s part of the appeal for denim enthusiasts. As you wear it, the fabric softens and develops fades and creases based on how you move, sit, and live in your jeans. You’re essentially breaking them in yourself, which makes the finished look feel uniquely yours. Prices can start around $60 to $100 for entry-level options and climb to $150 to $300 or more for premium denim, particularly Japanese-made styles. I’ll admit, the break-in period takes some patience, but if you enjoy watching your jeans evolve over time, raw denim is worth considering.

Raw denim and selvedge denim get lumped together all the time, but they actually refer to different things. Raw denim describes the fabric’s finishing state: It’s typically sold without a post-production wash that softens the fabric or creates a faded look. Selvedge, on the other hand, refers to how the denim is woven, creating a clean, self-finished edge you can often spot by rolling up the cuff. You can have raw selvedge denim, washed selvedge denim, or raw denim that isn’t selvedge at all. Having made my own pair of selvedge jeans in Japan, I’ve seen firsthand how the construction differs. Once you know what to look for, the distinction is pretty easy to spot.

At the end of the day, I don’t think you need to drop $300 on raw denim to dress well. After years of working in menswear, I’ve learned that a higher price tag doesn’t automatically make something the right choice for your wardrobe. I’ve seen guys look just as good in an affordable pair of Levi’s as they do in premium Japanese denim. What matters more is finding comfortable jeans that fit your body and work with the clothes you already own. I’m always going to appreciate the quality craftsmanship behind a great pair of raw denim, but there’s no single right way to wear jeans. I think every guy should have one dependable pair, whether that’s raw or prewashed. But ultimately, like most things in style, it comes down to personal preference—and knowing what works for you.

#4550. Getting More Physical Activity May Help With Your Perimenopause Symptoms

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A new study found that exercising more frequently may help alleviate perimenopause symptoms like hot flashes, anxiety, and urinary issues.

The study results were recently presented at The Menopause Society’s 2026 Annual Conference.

Perimenopause is the transitional period leading up to menopause.

Due to hormonal changes that mark the end of a person’s reproductive years, symptoms like hot flashes, mood swings, brain fog, insomnia, and urinary incontinence are common.

Past studies show that lifestyle modifications, such as eating a healthy diet, managing stress, and getting enough physical activity, can help reduce some perimenopause symptoms.

“Physical activity is low cost, accessible, and already known to benefit sleep, mood, and heart and bone health — all areas that matter a great deal during perimenopause,” said study lead and co-author Liudmila Zhaunova, PhD, director of science at Flo Health — the developer of a women’s health tracking app, which was used to collect health data for this study. “But when we looked at the research, there was surprisingly little evidence specific to perimenopause.”

“Hormone therapy isn’t suitable or desired for everyone, so women deserve a broader set of evidence-based options,” Zhaunova told Healthline. “The more we understand about accessible, lifestyle-based approaches, the better equipped women and their healthcare providers are to build a plan that works for them.”

Study researchers recruited more than 1,800 women from around the globe who were using the Flo app.

Study participants were asked to complete online surveys about their physical activity, from not being physically active to exercising more than 5 days a week.

Participants were also asked to report on their perimenopause symptoms in three categories — somatic (body-based issues like hot flashes), psychological (mental health issues like mood changes), and urogenital (issues regarding the urinary or vaginal areas). Researchers rated the severity of these symptoms using the Menopause Rating Scale (MRS).

The researchers found that higher rates of physical activity were associated with fewer perimenopausal symptoms across all three categories.

“Perimenopause symptoms are wide-ranging, and women often experience several at once,” Zhaunova said. “Seeing a lower symptom burden across all three domains suggests that being more active may be relevant to women’s overall experience, not just one symptom.”

Additionally, the scientists discovered that the greatest exercise benefit was observed in the reduction of perimenopausal psychological symptoms.

“Psychological symptoms, such as low mood, irritability, anxiety, and exhaustion, are among the most disruptive parts of perimenopause, and they’re often overlooked or not recognized as related to this life stage,” Zhaunova explained.

“Our findings suggest that physical activity may matter most for mood and anxiety-related symptoms,” she continued.

“This could help women and clinicians have more informed conversations about how to support mental well-being during perimenopause. That said, women with more symptoms may find it harder to exercise, so we need further research to understand the direction of the relationship.”

Sherry Ross, MD, a board certified OB-GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, said that regular exercise could potentially help relieve perimenopause symptoms like hot flashes and mood changes. Ross wasn’t involved in this study, but noted other benefits of exercising during this life stage:

“Exercise releases endorphins, improves blood vessel health, lowers inflammation, helps reset circadian rhythm, and improves the body’s internal cooling system,” Ross told Healthline. “Collectively, these changes ease anxiety, irritability, mood changes, sleep, and fatigue.”

“There were mixed reviews on how exercise benefits hot flashes, but exercise does help with weight control, which may benefit hot flashes,” she said. “Strengthening the pelvic floor and core may help with the urinary symptoms women many experience in perimenopause.”

Prudence Hall, MD, an OB-GYN in private practice in Santa Monica, CA, and author of “Radiant Again & Forever,” said that building a sustainable exercise routine requires consistency, joy, and community. Hall wasn’t involved in the study, but shared some exercise tips for perimenopausal women.

“Too often in midlife, previously stable women suddenly plummet into deep depression and anxiety, only to be prescribed multiple medications with debilitating side effects that never address the underlying biology,” Hall said.

“While hormone optimization remains essential to correct these endocrine deficiencies, exercise serves as a powerful, non-pharmaceutical therapy that directly targets the true root causes by revitalizing our cellular health and restoring overall vitality.”

#4547. Diablo 4's long-awaited Tower and its Leaderboards enter beta today, and there's no PTR funny business this time

Diablo 4's first big moment of 2026 is nearly upon us, and it's one feature many have been looking forward to for actual years.

Diablo 4 is about to deliver on Blizzard’s years-old promise to add a challenging, leaderboard-driven experience to the action RPG. The beta for The Tower, and its associated Leaderboards are about to kick off.

As recently revealed, the new experience launches today at 11am PT, 2pm ET, 7pm GMT. This is a beta version that arrives alongside update 2.5.2.

This early launch won’t offer the full experience, and it’s mainly designed to get it into the hands of players early so that Blizzard could get as much feedback as possible and incorporate it into the final version.

The beta is available to everyone, which is a nice change from the usual rigmarole players would have to go through to get the PTR build and play on a different branch. The Tower, and Leaderboards will be available only in the Seasonal Realm, however. Indeed, the plan is to build on what’s there with each future season, so it’s best to think of it as a seasonal experience.

The Tower is about as simple of an activity as you can get in an action RPG. The goal is to climb as many floors as you can as quickly as you can, all while battling increasingly difficult mobs.

You need to reach and beat the Tower’s final boss before you can call it a run. The Leaderboard will then show your time, and let you see how well you did compared to other players.

The Tower is available to anyone who completed Rank 2 of the current Season Rank. Once done, an Obelisk will appear in Cerrigar, letting you access the challenge.

The Leaderboards themselves will operate on a two-week schedule, all of which will take place during a given season. In this case, they’re going to close the day before Season of the Divine Interversion - the game’s current season - ends.

Blizzard published an updated set of patch notes that show how much has changed since The Tower, and Leaderboards first became playable in the patch 2.5.0 PTR.

Though the two new additions have been anticipated for a while, there’s not been much exciting going on in the world of Diablo 4, apart, of course, from the surprise arrival of The Paladin class, which became available in December last year to coincide with the announcement of Lord of Hatred, the game’s next expansion.

#4546. The Best Dining Room Lighting Ideas to Set Your Space Aglow

As romantic as candlelight dining sounds, it’s simply not practical (way too much wax!). The best dining room lighting ideas are thoughtfully planned: They take your actual eating habits, furniture proportions, room structure, and budget into consideration. They weigh the benefits of pendant lights vs. chandeliers vs. flush mounts and wall sconces, not to mention bulb type.

Lighting, it turns out, gets pretty involved—which is why we’ve tapped interior designers from around the country for their brightest ideas. And though the considerations are complex, modern lighting can be just as romantic as the candlelight option. “The evolving landscape is opening the door to more thoughtful layers and innovative ideas for how lighting can shape a space,” says Mike Shively of Chicago-based AD PRO firm En Masse Architecture and Design. “Lighting and coziness are both things we associate with luxury today, and neither necessarily comes down to price.”

In fashion designer Marysia Reeves’s Venice Beach home, a breezy, lantern-like fixture hovers above the wood dining table.

Pendant lighting gets more playful when each piece hangs at a different height. The look works especially well with a vaulted ceiling, as evidenced by this dining room set in a pavilion at the back of the house. “A cluster of glass pendants hung at staggered heights works alongside a pair of table lamps on the sideboard to create warm, layered light,” says Lucas Goldbach of En Masse Architecture and Design, who worked on the project.

The varying heights of the pendants gives this light fixture some dimension.

In this stunner of an LA home by design collective the Archers, a Le Corbusier–inspired table is lit with simple can overhead lights (definitely dimmable versions).

Lighting options are about more than just form. A more traditional shape can look entirely new when cast in a surprising material. “Our recommendation is to rethink conventional finishes and materials wherever possible,” says Sarah Goesling of Goesling Group, an AD PRO Directory firm based in Chicago. “For a chain-mail fixture, look beyond stainless steel to a striking yellow gold. For braided metal hardware, trade the ruggedness of wrought iron for the refined sheen of polished gunmetal.”

For a dining room in a midcentury-modern home, Goesling Group chose an appropriately retro 1950s-inspired Nabila chandelier by Tooy. And to add a modernized touch through the materials, they went with smoked glass around the bulbs.

A Sputnik-style fixture draws the eye up in this dining room by Goesling Group.

If you find an antique fixture you love, it might be worth rewiring the piece. Source on sites like eBay, Etsy, and 1stdibs, or take a lap around your local flea market. Alternatively, scour relatives’ attics and basements for a fixture with some family history. In the dining room below, designed by En Masse Architecture and Design, the light fixture was originally bought by the homeowner’s parents and was refurbished by Archistoric. “It became the inspiration for many of the metal finishes used throughout the new design,” says Goldbach.

This En Masse Architecture and Design dining space is crowned by an heirloom chandelier, refurbished by Archistoric.

Sconces, floor lamps, even table lamps can do the job of overhead pendant and flush-mount fixtures—so don’t force one if you’re not sure you like the look at all (proof, via Amélie Colombet’s apartment).

Fixtures made from natural alabaster stone are popular because of the warm, glare-free glow they emit. And because the translucent material has natural veining, no two pieces are alike. You can find alabaster in a variety of shapes—including linear forms, mini pendants, and semi-flush-mount ceiling lights—and while it’s generally pretty pricey (ranging anywhere from $300 for a single alabaster pendant lamp to $3,000 for a row of pendants), it’s also a durable material that lasts.

In the dining nook below, designed by Goesling Group, the minimal fixture makes a major impression. “It’s a solid alabaster stone piece lit all the way around the rim that looks like the moon when it glows,” says Goesling.

The pendant sets a striking tone in this cozy dining nook by Goesling Group.

If you have no interest in hardwiring a thing . . .Get an arc floor lampYou can have proper overhead lighting for your dining area without hardwiring a thing overhead. All you need is an arc floor lamp; they plug right into a wall outlet but stand tall, with a long, curved neck that will position the bulb and shade directly over your table. (How’s that for simplified?) The Arco floor lamp by Flos is a classic, but more budget-conscious shoppers can spring for similar styles from retailers like Wayfair (or even Amazon!).

If you’re trying to decide between options . . .Take the shape and style of your table into considerationThe overhead fixture you choose and the table itself “don’t have to [have] a direct geometrical correlation, but there should be some proportional consideration,” says Russell Groves of New York City–based Groves & Co. A long or unusually large chandelier would obviously overpower a short table, and vice versa: A teeny-tiny bulb might look silly above a big table. Same goes for the look: A lovely old farmhouse table would do well with a lovely farmhouse fixture or something super-modern for contrast—but either way, they should be considered together.

If you’d like to jazz up a fixture you already own . . .Customize the lampshadeA reflective material inside the shade will help cast light back down toward the table, Groves points out, while perforated shades will turn any bulb into a twinkly light (if that’s your prerogative). “Textured fabrics like raw silk will add a beautiful warmth and shadow to a room,” he says, but should you require a more directional cast, “an opaque shade wouldn’t give you a lot of glow in the room but would put more on the table.” Just because a fixture has shades you’re not into doesn’t mean it’s the end of the line. Swap them out!

If you’re sick of the typical dining room look . . .Consider less-traditional fixtures“Unreal times call for surreal styles, and lighting is emerging as a particularly compelling canvas for experimentation,” says Goesling. “From blown-glass designs with off-kilter stripes, to chain-mail, tapestry, and wrought iron reimagined through unexpected finishes and geometric patterns, these materials and techniques are taking on a new life. We anticipate seeing this influence extend across lighting, from woven metal sheaths and illustrative shades to sculptural, cast forms.”

A unique accent light sometimes even doubles as art. Such is the case in a Chicago penthouse designed by Kuchar Studio, which features a dining room fixture with a strikingly sinuous form. “Going for the cool, funky light fixture has become the new normal among our residential clients,” says Sarah Kuchar-Parkinson, owner and creative director of the Chicago-based studio. “Not only does it bring brightness and light, but it adds architectural interest and an element of quirk.”

A statement-making light fixture hovers above the dining room table of this Kuchar Studio–designed Chicago penthouse.

When hanging a pendant or a chandelier . . .Take care to hang it at the correct heightToo high and a pendant lamp might look stuck to the ceiling, constricted; too low and you won’t be able to see your guests around it. Somewhere in between is just right, so step back and consider it at various lengths before settling on one. “When you’re seated, you don’t want [the fixture] to be in your face—the same way everyone gets annoyed when flowers are in the way,” Groves says.

If you’re into geometric lines . . .Get creative with the cordWhen hanging a pendant lamp with a very simple silhouette, feel free to get creative with the cord: Zigzag it from wall to wall around a corner, tack it in swags along the ceiling, or loop it over a hook on the wall.

To soften overly bright overheads . . .Add a dimmerDesigner Sam Allen of Sam Allen Interiors, based in Weston, Connecticut, feels strongly about the worth of a dimmer switch: “If your dining room chandelier is not hooked up to a dimmer, call an electrician right now. I mean it. Don’t even finish reading this—call your electrician first.” With the simple pressing of a lever or turning of a knob, dimmable lighting allows you to control the brightness of the bulbs, which is particularly important if you’re dealing with often-harsh overhead lighting. “To me, chandeliers should mimic candlelight,” says Groves—and that warm, dim glow is possible if you just install dimmers. Plus, they’re available in both modern and traditional silhouettes that will add considerably more charm to the walls than those plastic switch plates you hate anyway.

To make the room multifunctional . . .Supplement the lighting solution you chooseIf you end up setting your table with some nice, warm candlelight, you might need to add sconces or downlights to supplement that glow when the room is used for anything other than an intimate dinner. (Dining room tables are very good for DIY projects, we’d like to note.) And to up a room’s cozy factor, lean into lamps. “The more lamps the better,” says Goldbach. “We like using smaller-scale lamps and vintage pieces in unexpected ways to create a warm, layered atmosphere. Multiple light sources can make the space feel cozier and more inviting, especially as the days get shorter.”

If you’re a fanatic about light quality . . .Be selective with the bulbsWarm white light, cool light, bright light—you can get exactly the brightness level you desire with the right bulb. For lots of ambience, try vintage-inspired filament bulbs. If it’s more of a particular shape and style you’re after for an exposed-bulb fixture, try decorative bulbs. And for the ultimate in energy efficiency, invest in LED bulbs, which are up to 90 percent more efficient than traditional light bulbs.

#4545. Helldivers 2's GameGuard cheat policing "wasn't as effective as it could have been", cue a switch to Easy Anti-Cheat as part of a crackdown on cheat mods

"Some non-malicious cosmetic mods" may need updates as a result

Helldivers 2, a game that still appears to be missing in action from Steam here in the UK, is switching anti-cheat software. Developers Arrowhead are keen to tighten up security and crack down on the use of cheat mods that can influence the bug-slaying experience of folks beyond the person who's installed them.

"The implementation of GameGuard for Helldivers 2 wasn't as effective as it could have been," Arrowhead wrote in a post announcing the shooter's impending switch from that cheat-busting software to Easy Anti-Cheat. "In the end we decided to take another path with EAC. EAC is an industry standard [tech] used in over 200 games, and we believe this will be an effective way to protect the Galactic War and will better protect fair gameplay and the integrity of the game."

You might be familiar with Easy Anti-Cheat from the likes of Elden Ring, Fortnite, Rust, and the recently released Gears of War: E-Day. Arrowhead noted that as part of the switch to the Epic-owned software, they'll be putting "additional security tools in place to enhance its effectiveness and improve game integrity protection".

They're also using this as an opportunity to take aim at cheat mods to the extent that some innocent skin swaps might be caught in the crossfire. "We know mods are part of the DNA of PC gaming and gaming communities as a whole," the studio wrote. "In general, we appreciate the creativity behind cosmetic and non-gameplay mods, which players can enjoy without negatively impacting those who play alongside them. Where we need to improve is how we protect players against modifications that create unfair advantages or negatively impact others, which goes beyond software manipulation to tamper with our game.

"These changes mean that some mods, particularly the types that are affecting the experience of other players, may no longer function. It also means that some non-malicious cosmetic mods may require updates from their individual creators in order to keep working."

Arrowhead invited any folks who think the new anti-cheat is "blocking a non-destructive mod or other software" to reach out to their support staff about it via Zendesk.

It's never nice to see headaches created for modders, but as a game that revolves above all else around multiplayer, I reckon there's as much of a case for such a crackdown in Helldivers 2 as there ever really could be. Doing whatever you want to a sandbox that's only ever going to be occupied by you or your mates is fine, but if you're starting to mess around with strangers rather than just rocking up to a fight dressed as Kazuma Kiryu or Heath Ledger's Joker that's pushing it. Taking a glance at the Nexus, I reckon HUD and UI overhauls might end up becoming a contentious battleground in terms of HD2 mods, given they only apply to the user, but some might be construed as offering an advantage by allowing those players to see more or more easily identify threats.

We'll see how things go, with the anti-cheat switch set to take place today, October 20th.

#4544. In 1828, serial killers sold their victims’ bodies to science

Published Oct 27, 2026 8:53 AM EDT

For as long as surgeons cut open living people to keep them alive, they have needed dead people to practice on. Today, donation programs keep medical schools supplied with the bodies they need to teach their students. But in 1820s Scotland, donating your body to science wasn’t legal yet. Nonetheless, one doctor found a way to get the bodies he needed. But it came at a terrible cost. This is the grisly story of the Anatomy Murders.

In the early 1800s, surgery was booming. A few decades earlier, Scottish surgeon John Hunter had helped bring a scientific view to Western medicine, transforming surgery with experimentation and careful observation. The Napoleonic Wars had boosted the demand for trained surgeons, Alun Evans, a retired epidemiologist at Queen’s University in Belfast, tells Popular Science.

One of the leading lights in this burgeoning field was Hunter’s compatriot Robert Knox, who ran an anatomy school in Edinburgh. But by 1827, Knox had hit a roadblock to his teaching ambitions. A change to regulations meant that dissection had become an important part of the medical curriculum. Knox’s school would require more than 400 bodies the following year. What made this such an overwhelming ask was that citizens had no legal way to donate their bodies to medical science. But where there’s a demand, supply will follow.

Edinburgh Medical School first opened in 1728, and for much of the next century, allegations of grave robbing linked to the school had been rife. Newly buried corpses would be exhumed and then presented at medical school doors by so-called “resurrectionists”.

The provenance of these bodies was unimportant to the corpse-strapped medical profession. But families, desperate to protect their loved ones, had taken extreme steps to stamp out the practice. Relatives would stay at their loved ones’ graveside for weeks, waiting for their bodies to lose their medical value. Factories produced devices like mort safes, heavy iron cages placed over newly dug graves. Knox had been forced to source his bodies from further away, even bringing some from across the Irish Sea.

Across the city, two other men also had a body problem. Irishman William Hare owned a lodging house with his wife near Edinburgh’s Grassmarket. One of his tenants was a fellow Irishman, William Burke. Burke and Hare became friends.

One day in early 1828, Hare discovered one of his tenants had died overnight. Even worse, the dead man still owed him rent. The pair came up with a plan to recoup the money, and they carried the corpse to Knox’s door. There, he paid them £7 and 10 shillings; more than a month’s wages.

Their eyes opened by greed, Burke and Hare hatched a plan to get Knox the bodies he desperately needed. Over the next 12 months, they brought him 16 fresh corpses. They had acquired each one by murdering their way through the poor of Edinburgh. They had lured the victims to Hare’s guesthouse, and then suffocated them in the night, lying on their chests to minimize any damage to their valuable bodies. The majority of their victims were women.

Burke and Hare’s rampage was only stopped in November 1828. A tenant at Hare’s guesthouse reported to the police that they had found a corpse under their bed. When the police raided the premises, they only found a single spot of blood where the body had been.

But under interrogation, Burke and his wife gave inconsistent accounts. Eventually, Burke, Hare, and their wives were all arrested.

Despite their equal guilt in their crimes, the two men had very different fates. Hare offered to confess everything to the authorities in exchange for his freedom. His evidence condemned Burke.

Two months later, Burke was hanged in front of a baying crowd. Ironically, his crimes meant that, under the Murder Act of 1752, he was not allowed a burial. His body was instead donated to the Edinburgh Medical School, where his skeleton is still on show today.

Hare disappeared after being freed. Evans says that research suggests he returned to Ireland, where some think he ended his days in Kilkeel Workhouse in County Down.

Knox faced graver consequences than Hare. He was never tried for his link to the murders, but his reputation was buried. He was forced out of a curatorial role at the Royal College of Surgeons and eventually moved to London. He was unable to secure another university teaching post, but continued to work as an anatomist at a hospital in the city.

Evans says the taint of the scandal went beyond Knox. “The Burke and Hare scandal really stymied medicine in Edinburgh considerably, and it explained the rising popularity of the Dublin schools and the Paris schools of medicine,” he says.

One fact that may have haunted Knox is that if he had avoided the lure of Burke and Hare’s corpses for just a few more years, he would have benefited from the Anatomy Act, passed in 1832, which gave surgeons the green light to procure any bodies from hospitals, prisons, or workhouses that had gone unclaimed for 48 hours. Voluntary donation was also legalized at the same time.

Instead, Knox became part of a black legend in Scotland’s history. Burke and Hare’s crimes have become an indelible part of Edinburgh’s grim past.

In That Time When, Popular Science tells the weirdest, most surprising, and little-known stories that shaped science, engineering, and innovation.

In 1934, a doctor brought a dog back to life. He tried to do the same with humans.

#4541. How This Former Weightlifter Lost 80 Pounds After GLP-1s Stopped Working

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

For years, Dana Bichler was a competitive weightlifter. He felt fit and strong competing at 200 to 210 pounds. But wear and tear on his body and aging eventually pushed him to step away from weightlifting. Without the training, he began to gain weight slowly and steadily.

“I tried running, and a few other things, and the weight just crept up,” he told Healthline.

Dieting allowed him to lose about 50 pounds, but he would then regain it. At his heaviest, he weighed close to 300 pounds, which inspired him to visit a weight-loss clinic, where he followed a plan centered on the clinic’s foods.

“It’s a life of deprivation,” Bichler said. “You just can’t live like it.”

About five years ago, he tried a GLP-1 medication and two others a few years later. Each helped him lose weight initially but then stopped working.

“There was really no long-term positive effects or benefits that I saw,” he said.

However, he still takes Mounjaro for blood sugar control because he is prediabetic.

A few years ago, he was diagnosed with fatty liver disease. His condition was tied to obesity and caused scarring on his liver.

Bichler understood the seriousness of the condition as his two younger sisters and his oldest brother also had fatty liver disease. His brother and youngest sister developed cirrhosis, and his brother died after the disease affected other parts of his body.

“I had to do something,” he said. “What I was doing was not working.”

From his home in St. Cloud, Minnesota, Bichler traveled to the University of Minnesota to see a hepatologist, who referred him to a gastroenterologist who had worked with Boston Scientific on the instruments used to perform endoscopic sleeve gastroplasty (ESG).

ESG is a minimally invasive weight loss procedure performed using an FDA-authorized endoscopic suturing system, such as one offered through Boston Scientific’s Endura Weight Loss Solutions.

During ESG, a doctor passes an endoscope through the mouth and uses sutures to fold the stomach into a narrower sleeve. No portion of the stomach is removed, and there are no incisions. The smaller stomach limits how much a person can eat at once.

“Patients typically go home the same day and can return to routine daily activities within two to three days,” Brian Dunkin, MD, chief medical officer at Boston Scientific, told Healthline. “For the right person, that provides another less-invasive option to consider with their healthcare professional.”

While ESG may be a good option for some people who don’t achieve weight loss goals with GLP-1 medications, can’t tolerate them, or don’t want to stay on medication long term, Dunkin said he doesn’t think of it as ESG versus GLP-1 drugs.

“One of the best things GLP-1 medications have done is get more people talking to their doctors about obesity and recognizing it as a chronic disease that deserves treatment,” he said.

Each approach has different benefits, risks, and side effects.

“The goal is finding the right treatment, or combination of treatments, for the right patient at the right time,” said Dunkin.

ESG is indicated for adults with obesity with a BMI between 30 and 50, but Dunkin said BMI is only part of the conversation. ESG may be an option for people who aren’t getting the weight loss results they want, who don’t want to stay on weight loss medication long term, or for whom bariatric surgery may not be appropriate or desired, he added.

“Obesity is a chronic disease that requires long-term care,” said Dunkin. “ESG is one part of that care, which is why I believe so strongly in a multidisciplinary approach. The goal is to look at the whole person and find a care plan they can sustain over time.”

In March 2025, Bichler decided ESG was a good fit for him and received the procedure at Boston Scientific. At the time, he was 67 years old and on Medicare, which pre-approved the procedure.

“I just felt comfortable with it not being invasive, and I really needed to deal with [my weight], so I did,” he said.

When Bichler underwent ESG he weighed somewhere in the 260s. Today, he weighs 178 pounds.

While the procedure went smoothly, he said the following month was difficult. Because bread, pasta, rice, and carbonated drinks were off the table, he had to consume clear liquids, then slowly moved to other liquids, and eventually cream soups.

“I found that extremely difficult,” he said.

What surprised him most was depression. Looking back, he calls it a perfect storm. Working with his weight loss doctor, he had stopped taking his antidepressant about two weeks before the procedure. After surgery, his eating habits changed dramatically, disrupting the social life he’d built around food.

“I was in a pretty deep funk for a while,” he said. “I really was.”

He restarted his antidepressant, and over time, he adjusted to a new way of eating.

“I’ve discovered I like cream soup,” he said. “I still have it for meals.”

Although he’s lost more than 80 pounds, Bichler said the bigger change is how good he feels. He exercises four to five days a week and has more energy and moves more easily than he has in years.

“I haven’t felt this good in 30 years,” Bichler said. “I’m not kidding you.”

He’s able to enjoy things he loves like working on the farm he and his wife own. At the farm, he hunts ducks, turkeys, pheasants, and small game, and runs a trapline, all activities his weight had made nearly impossible for a long time.

“It used to be really hard for me to do because of the physicality of doing some of these things,” he said.

Although he retired from his profession, Bichler said he’s busier than ever. He’s a protodeacon in the Eastern Orthodox Church and also serves as a chaplain for his local police department, a role that has him climbing in and out of squad cars and up and down steps.

“Before I’d be sucking air trying to keep up,” he said.

Most meaningful of all is that he has more energy to give to his family.

“I have a grandson that I can actually do things and be active with now,” Bichler said. “It’s like starting all over. I just feel like a new person.”

#4540. Gameplay footage emerges of reportedly cancelled Assassin's Creed game Destiny

Footage of a reportedly cancelled Assassin's Creed game set in ancient China has emerged online, including stealth kills, the series' leap of faith, and a spinning umbrella weapon.

#4539. Porsche Could Be Planning a $400K 911 in Efforts to Raise Profit Margins

One-offs and Carrera GT retrofits also play a part in Porsche’s planned path back to huge profits.

Porsche might be planning a $400,000 911 model to help make up for recent drops in profit, Automotive News reports.

Porsche product and individualization vice president Alexander Fabig says there’s more money to squeeze from top-end 911 models, in addition to the boost that will likely come from the production version of the recently revealed Mission S concept. The exec said that the 911 S/C, a convertible based on the GT3, is 95 percent more expensive than the most basic 911 on sale today. But “Future products,” he added, “could exceed a 200 percent price premium over the base model.”

Since the basic 911 starts at over $135,000, that would mean a 911 with a starting price of $400,000 or more. The 911 S/T that served as the capstone for the 992.1-generation 911 started at just under $300,000, so this would suggest a serious leap in price for future range-topping models. Previous 911 generations have included a GT2, and other non-911 ranges have been topped by a Turbo GT, so Porsche has a few badges in reserve that could be used for various ultimate forms of its 992.2-generation car even if it does not opt for a new variant.

The renewed focus on profit margin comes after the brand’s volume business struggled with tariffs in the U.S. and new competition from domestic manufacturers in China. That, combined with shifting investments in EVs and internal combustion models, led the company’s operating profits to slide down 99 percent in 2025’s third quarter. New 911 variants are just one of a few options the brand is exploring to grow its high-margin business.

While new supercars and even higher-end 911s are still in the realm of typical new car business, Porsche is not restricted to just selling new things. Owners of existing Carrera GTs can bring their cars to the brand for Bugatti-like recommissions, an endeavor that Fabig says can cost between $500,000 and $800,000. He notes that 11 of these have been done to date. Add in 16 more in the works and 21 waiting behind those, and a healthy percentage of the 1270 Carrera GTs produced will have undergone the factory retrofit.

Porsche is also one of many manufacturers with an in-house team built for customization, a group the brand calls Sonderwunsch. This team, according to Fabig, is currently working on 15 different one-off projects priced from just under $2 million to a massive $15 million. Few-offs—models that are rarer than the typical limited edition but not quite as customized as true one-offs like the Flachbau RS unveiled at Monterey this year—are also an opportunity that Fabig mentions for the future.

While all of those opportunities represent a chance to make more money from ultra-wealthy buyers, Porsche also builds on its profit margin with options. According to an Edmunds figure quoted in the Automotive News piece, the average new Porsche costs $128,663 as configured despite an average starting price of just $81,813. That means $46,850 per car, around $5000 under the average price for any new car sold in 2025.

Aside from the upcoming Mission S supercar, all of these avenues for profitability have existed in some form throughout the 2020s. The big shift here is the emphasis, with each becoming a major priority while the company’s volume sales business becomes less essential to profitability. This should insulate Porsche from some of the risks that come with constant shifts in even higher-end volume markets struggling with electrification, tariffs, and a massive increase in competition. The only question is whether it will be enough to turn the tide back for a company that was the envy of the industry until very recently.

Fred Smith's love of cars comes from his fascination with auto racing. Unfortunately, that passion led him to daily drive a high-mileage, first-year Porsche Panamera. He is still thinking about the last lap of the 2011 Indianapolis 500.

#4538. The Sims developer vows "our values are unchanged," as it teases multiple projects from post-Saudi-owned EA

The team behind one of EA's most diverse franchises has issued a statement about its future under the soon-to-be Saudi-owned publisher.

In their (slightly belated) new years message to players, the developer of EA’s The Sims series of games has lightly addressed concerns about its future within the publisher in a wide-ranging letter to the community.

The letter also serves as something of a light plan for what players can expect soon, and in the future, alongside what the various teams are currently working on.

The developer starts off the yearly check-in with a statement that attempts to address concerns about the future of The Sims, historically one of EA’s most inclusive and culturally diverse franchises.

This, of course, comes in the wake of last year’s bombshell that EA is being taken private by a coalition led by Saudi Arabia’s Public Investment Fund (PIF), alongside investment firms Silver Lake Capital and Affinity Partners. The $55 billion acquisition was approved by EA’s shareholders in December, and is set to close by the end of June.

Understandably, many raised concerns about what EA’s new ownership would mean for its people, and portfolio of games. The news is concerning for The Sims in particular, a series that’s long embraced diversity and inclusivity, and has historically been popular among women, people of colour, and members of the LGBTQ+ community.

“Our Sims team's creative control, guided by our values of inclusivity, choice, creativity, community, and play, has not changed,” the post reads, though without referring to the buyout directly at any point.

“These values inspire the decisions we make each and every day as we plan for today, tomorrow, and the future.”

As for what’s to come in 2026 and beyond, it sounds like The Sims team has multiple irons in the fire. The developer is working on “a family of new Sims experiences” that will find homes on PC, console, and mobile. Crucially, the post says that not every experience will be on every platform, in order for each to focus on delivering something different and interesting.

Future releases, of course, include new The Sims 4 content that focuses on the single-player experience. The social and more multiplayer-driven experiences, however, will instead be found in Project Rene, the mobile-first life-sim currently in development. (Despite early confidence that it was a working title for The Sims 5, it's now confirmed that Project Rene won’t, in fact, be a direct successor to The Sims 4.)

Some of these projects will see more playtesting throughout 2026, so you should probably sign up for The Sims Labs if you want to hear about when/how you can get involved.

#4537. 93,997 Range Rovers Recalled Because Water Can Fry the Backup Camera

The drainage system in the tailgate isn’t doing a good enough job of extracting water, potentially causing the rearview camera to fail.

Land Rover is recalling 93,997 vehicles over a problem affecting the rearview camera. The recall includes Range Rover SUVs from 2022 through 2026 model years, regardless of which powertrain they were fitted with. The population of impacted SUVs left the Solihull, U.K., assembly plant between July 8, 2021, and June 4, 2026, according to documents filed with the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA).

The camera failures are caused by an issue with the water management system for the Range Rover’s tailgate, according to the recall documents. The drainage system apparently may not be doing a good enough job of clearing away water, causing moisture to pool around the rearview camera. Over time, the documents state, this can damage the camera and lead to a distorted image being displayed on the dashboard-mounted touchscreen when the car is put into reverse. It may even cause no image to be shown at all, and either scenario could increase the risk of an injury or a crash.

See all results for new 2026 Land Rover Range Rover for sale near 6130

The fix is pretty straightforward. Land Rover will drill additional drain holes in the underside of the tailgate trim in which the rearview camera is housed, and the camera will be inspected and replaced if needed. This repair will be performed at no cost to the owners. Dealerships will be notified on October 29, while notification letters are set to be sent to owners on or before December 11.

This isn’t the first time we’ve seen a recall because of water taking out rearview cameras. In July, Honda recalled 325,588 Odyssey minivans for a similar issue, in which water was corroding the camera’s circuit board. This had followed a previous recall from 2020 where moisture entered the rearview camera mounting holes, causing the camera to malfunction.

Caleb Miller began blogging about cars at 13 years old, and he realized his dream of writing for a car magazine after graduating from Carnegie Mellon University and joining the Car and Driver team. He loves quirky and obscure autos, aiming to one day own something bizarre like a Nissan S-Cargo, and is an avid motorsports fan.

#4536. Penguin Colony is not messing around with its statement on the Palestinian genocide, banning sale in Israel and calling out the games industry in-game

You might remember 2021's Umurangi Generation, developed by Māori studio Origame Digital. It placed its focus on its first-person photography system as much as its very direct anti-colonial and anti-neoliberal criticism, stemming partly from the Australian government's mishandling of COVID and the bushfires that spread across the country in 2020. Origame's second game, the newly-released Penguin Colony, is just as direct in its anti-colonial theming, with its target being the ongoing Palestinian genocide by Israel, and the games industry at large for its silence.

Penguin Colony is, to boil it down to its simplest nature, a sort of walking simulator in which your playable penguins take a trip through an Antarctic consumed by Lovecraftian nightmares, in a riff on At the Mountains of Madness. It also reframes the academic explorers of Lovecraft's story as German and British Nazi occultists, while celebrating Māori culture and survival, opening with Aboriginal land acknowledgements and noting that the game itself was made on Wurundjeri Woiwurrung land.

What it closes with, though, is the most overt statement on BDS and the Palestinian genocide by a game developer since...well, last week, when House House explained why they're not selling Big Walk on Xbox. Following Penguin Colony's credits, the following message appears:

'The cowardice of the Games Industry during the ongoing genocide of Palestine by Israel will be recorded by the art it makes.'

Also, the game is not, and will not be for sale in Israel (and presumably on Xbox), a member of publishers Fellow Traveler explaining on Steam being that 'In the absence of more organised sanctions, this act is intended to demonstrate solidarity with Palestine. We fully support their decision.'

The ongoing Palestinian genocide is an atrocity which is gradually being recognised as such by more than just those who've been screaming about it for years. Yet, many in games development and games media are unwilling to comment on its reality or join the BDS boycott of Xbox and Microsoft, which BDS have identified as a priority target due to Microsoft's complicity in its ties to the Israeli military through the technology it provides.

Origame Digital making a statement so clear means something. It meant something when House House did it, it meant something when the Game Awards' Future Class wrote an open letter calling for recognition of the genocide and were ignored (and later abandoned entirely). It means something when you and those around you do not support Microsoft and are loud about the boycott, let alone not supporting the corporation while it destroys Xbox and the livelihoods of thousands through insatiable greed.

As Origame developer Naphtali Faluklner says in this Bluesky post regarding Penguin Colony not being sold in Israel: "Everyone should do this. Ask your favorite devs to do this. See how they respond."

#4535. A three-legged cougar named Crimson is thriving in Arkansas

Published Oct 27, 2026 11:06 AM EDT

At first, the video seems to feature an empty habitat. There’s a crate filled with hay, platforms of various heights, and a ball hanging from a rope. Then a furry head peaks out from behind the crate. A few seconds later, a large wild cat bounds into view, rushing toward whoever is holding the camera. The young mountain lion (Puma concolor) appears so happy to see the videographer that you almost can’t tell he has only three legs.

“Crimson was just three weeks old when National Park Service biologists and then California Department of Fish and Wildlife determined he had been abandoned,” Emily McCormack, an animal curator at Turpentine Creek Wildlife Refuge in northwest Arkansas, tells Popular Science. “Using GPS collar data from his mother and camera monitoring, it was confirmed that she had moved to a different den site and was no longer returning to care for him.”

As if the situation could get any worse for the young mountain lion, he also had an injury to his hind foot. Without treatment, the injury ultimately could have killed him. He was rescued and transported to the Los Angeles Zoo, where he received acute veterinary care. Then he was taken to Oakland Zoo by Flying Tails animal air ambulance service. The Oakland Zoo veterinary hospital team eventually amputated the injured leg.

Thankfully, Crimson made an exceptional recovery, but he was too young to be considered for rehabilitation and release. The California Department of Fish and Wildlife decided Crimson would live permanently at Turpentine Creek Wildlife Refuge, a safe haven accredited by the Global Federation of Animal Sanctuaries. And now it seems safe to say that Crimson has settled well into his forever home.

“He is thriving in his new habitat at our sanctuary. We chose a long habitat enclosure with relatively flat ground, where he can explore but also has the ability to fully run a good distance,” says McCormack. “Crimson loves his neighbors, especially a female lion named Lucy. He loves his meal times and can be heard chirping all day to the staff, interns, and guests.”

Mountain lions, also known as cougars or pumas, are native to parts of the Americas and represent the world’s most wide-ranging cat species.
