#4534. 12 Best Historic Hotels and Airbnbs That Feel Like Time Traveling (2026)

There are plenty of impressive stays out there, no matter your destination of choice. But booking a historic hotel or Airbnb rental with a storied past—tales of tenants, centuries-old architecture, or whatever else might land a property on a local registrar—is the difference between a simple spot to snooze at night and your next dinner party conversation. From former artists’ homes and firehouses to castles and celebrity-studded hotels, we’ve curated several beguiling places around the world to lay your head at night. As is the case with older buildings, some require a bit of flexibility—say, an outdoor shower or a less-than-fully-equipped kitchen—while others offer a portal to the past (some spots on this list predate the 16th century) rich with amenities and luxury.

The following historical hotels and homes have a story to tell, and guests get a front-row seat.

Perks: Shared beach access, in-unit washer and dryer, sound system, indoor fireplace, fire pit

Architect Frank Lloyd Wright built a lot of incredible spaces for others, but this is the very one he contributed to a Life Magazine article back in 1938 as part of a feature about dream homes. The four-bedroom estate is full of windows (the 63-foot living room has an entire wall of them), and each space is bursting with midcentury furniture and decor. Walking into each room feels like unlocking a new level of a time capsule, from the shag-carpeted primary bedroom to the brick-walled kitchen. While spring, summer, and fall are beautiful times to visit Wisconsin, the fire pit (and hammock) outside is still inviting come winter and snow. From $841 per night.

Perks: Badminton net, steam room, gym, BBQ grill, EV charger

This is the perfect pit stop on a road trip through the Highlands. Hidden in the depths of a former abbey, this one-bedroom space used to be the “writing room” for the monastery. The bed is surrounded by stained glass windows, which look out over the gardens and Loch Ness (yes, of Nessie fame). Guests have access to the neighboring Highland Club, which houses an incredible indoor pool, sauna, tennis court, and so much more. While the kitchen and appliances are newer additions, you will find pieces of furniture from the original space peppered throughout the rental (a wooden bench here, a tapestry there). From $468 per night.

Perks: Pet friendly, patio, free parking, pool, outdoor shower

If daydreams of your next trip are filled with al fresco dinners, this two-bedroom home will do you one better by adding bird’s-eye ocean vistas to the scene. Once a windmill that used to grind wheat for flour production, it now houses an outdoor kitchen, a bedroom with a view, and an outdoor shower. The scenes from the top bedroom are really something to write home about—wake up to mountain views and the ocean beyond. The pool is the best spot to take in the sunset before retreating to the open-air dining space for good eats and conversation. From $224 per night.

Perks: Pet friendly, Wi-Fi, luggage drop-off, radiant heating, fireplace

This historic Airbnb is known as La Romantica, which should tell you all you need to know. The one-bedroom rental can be found in the oldest section of the property (which dates back earlier than the 1400s), there’s a private entrance, ancient stone walls, antique Italian furniture, and a fireplace that’s perfect for fighting off a chilly night. This is a spot where you do a bit of time traveling, both indoors and out when you decide to explore the surrounding UNESCO heritage town, Teggiano, which is known for its museums (the Diocesan Museum, Herb Museum, and Museum of Folk Traditions, to name a few) and its 13 churches. Guests at this one-bedroom stay are located right in the middle of town and within walking distance of everything you’d need, so slipping out for a quick dinner, coffee at one of the cafes, or a sunset stroll along medieval architecture is always on the itinerary. From $154 per night.

Perks: Pet friendly, air conditioning, private parking available, fireplace, historic decor

Built in 1884, this was Washington, DC’s first fully African American fire company and the set of a Netflix show: Check out Stay Here, season one, episode eight. Located in the Shaw neighborhood, a historic district, there are plays to attend (Howard Theatre), drinks to be had (Blagden Alley), and music to take in (Eighth Street Lounge and Bar). The five-bedroom rental even has the original fire pole and you can see previous tenants in the photography that decorates the walls. From $860 per night.

Perks: Shared beach access, sound system, exercise equipment, fire pit

Step right into the world of painter Reginald Marsh when you stay at this two-bedroom getaway with forest views. A pond anchors one side of the property, and the expansive lawns are landscaped with long, post-dinner strolls in mind. As secluded as it feels, it’s a short walk to the center of Woodstock and all of its restaurants, bars, cafes, and shops. And while you’re not near any national parks, the home’s private waterfall (you read that correctly) borders a public preserve that’s open for exploring. The library inside will pull you back indoors, though, as will the wood-burning stove on chilly nights. The outdoor dining space is great for summertime grilling, but the full kitchen indoors is a delight to work in. From $619 per night.

Perks: Central location, trendy bars and restaurant, 24-hour fitness center, balcony access, Aqua di Parma and Byredo toiletries

Centrally located in Madrid’s Plaza de las Cortes, the historic Palace Hotel reopened its doors in 2025 to unveil property-wide updates—including its main lobby, dining accommodations, and 470 rooms—the result of a two-year renovation project with reimagined interiors by designer Lázaro Rosa-Violán. Dating back to 1912, The Palace has seen notable guests like Salvador Dalí and Ernest Hemingway, and is even credited as the first hotel in the country to have private toilets in its quarters.

Today, guests can appreciate pieces of the property’s historic past through a modern lens. Surrounded by bespoke fixtures custom designed by Rosa-Violán, vintage tapestries hang with contemporary Joan Miro prints in La Cúpula Restaurant. Inside the inviting suites, updated bathrooms feature original red marble stone reminiscent of Paloma Elsesser’s luxurious tub. Just a five-minute walk away, art aficionados can spend long hours wandering through the Thyssen-Bornemisza National Museum and the Prado museum, or take a stroll through El Retiro Park. “The hotel staff at The Palace are some of the friendliest I’ve encountered,” says commerce editor Audrey Lee. “You’ll definitely want to stop by La Cúpula during your trip for an afternoon drink and some bites from the stylish tapas menu. If it’s the right time of day, the room becomes drenched in sunlight through the dome-shaped stained glass ceiling.” From $1,060 per night.

Perks: Spa, fitness center, concierge, Smart TVs, room service

The history of the Waldorf Astoria runs deep. Originally designed in 1893 as two side-by-side hotels built by feuding family members, the collective property was demolished in 1929 to make room for the Empire State Building’s construction. It was reopened in its current Park Avenue location in 1931, breaking records as the largest and tallest hotel in the world (at the time). Its Art Deco architecture and interiors caught the eyes of royals, celebrities, and politicians around the world. The guest book includes names such as Nikola Tesla, Winston Churchill, Zsa Zsa Gabor, the Obamas, and so many more. After being closed for extensive renovations, the property is planning a grand re-opening for Spring 2025. From $1,221 per night.

Perks: Irene Forte spa, indoor lap pool, three on-site restaurants, whisky tasting experience, afternoon tea, views of Edinburgh landmarks

A historic landmark, the Balmoral is widely recognized for its 190-foot clock tower—famously set three minutes fast, except on New Year’s Eve—that has been part of the Edinburgh skyline since opening in the early 1900s. J.K. Rowling even finished the final Harry Potter book on January 11, 2007, in Suite 552—now commemorated with an owl door knocker and an in-room bust of the Greek god Hermes that Rowling signed. “The Balmoral does rest and relaxation like no other through comforting amenities, charming interiors, and hearty cuisine,” says shopping editor Audrey Lee. “After a long day of taking in Edinburgh’s historic sights, there was nothing I looked forward to more than the treats from turndown service and a long soak in the glossy marble tub.” Guests also return for the sprawling bedrooms, some of which frame picturesque views of Edinburgh Castle. The stays showcase cozy design with contemporary wallpaper prints, surprisingly neutral plaid furniture, and ultrasoft wool bed covers. From $399 per night.

Perks: Presidential, royal, and celebrity clientele; iconic Bemelmans Bar; walking distance to Central Park

Nestled on 76th Street off Madison Avenue, the Carlyle first opened its doors in 1930. Since then, presidents, royalty, and movie stars have all sought refuge in the hallowed hotel. “You can have breakfast next to Daniel Patrick Moynihan or Tom Ford,” Los Angeles–based designer and AD100 Hall of Famer Michael S. Smith once described. “It's like a big hotel in a small town, the grande dame of New York.” The interiors blend Art Deco touches and New York eclecticism with classic architecture through a layered mix of vintage-inspired furniture silhouettes, contemporary art, and splashy chandeliers. One of the hotel’s top attractions for locals and hotel guests alike is the iconic Bemelmans Bar, outfitted with whimsical wall murals by Madeline author and illustrator Ludwig Bemelmans. The classic martini is a failsafe order, though you could go for the namesake Madeline's Vesper or Bobby’s Manhattan (an homage to cabaret singer Bobby Short, a longtime fixture of Café Carlyle, another classic destination within the Carlyle). From $1,323 per night.

This year, Hotel Figueroa celebrates its 100th birthday. The Young Women's Christian Association (YWCA) originally financed, owned, and operated the Downtown Los Angeles fixture to function as a safe haven for solo female travelers. The property allowed unaccompanied women to check in without a male chaperone—something most hotels prohibited at the time. Today, Spanish Revival design influences flourish in the space: Expect curated art (exclusively by female artists), sophisticated tilework, and modern furnishings. As you dip in the coffin-shaped pool straight from the 1920s while surrounded by 50-year-old cacti, enjoy craft cocktails and bites from the poolside bar, La Casita. For a more substantial meal, the restaurant-in-residence Florence by the Water serves up Italian classics. From $129 per night.

Perks: Proximity to landmarks, celebrity guest roster, traditional afternoon tea, live music, private tours and experiences available upon request

Widely recognized as London’s oldest luxury hotel, updates like glossy marble bathrooms, vibrant textiles, and mahogany walk-in closets have breathed new life into the historic stay. At the same time, homages to the original architecture—in stained-glass windows, untouched entryways, and wrought-iron façade detailing—forge bonds to its heritage. “You might think the main attraction of Brown’s is its proximity to landmarks like Buckingham Palace and Hyde Park, but it’s actually the inviting staff and dapper design that make this stay more like a home than a stopover,” says Lee. Past literary guests of note include Rudyard Kipling, Bram Stoker, Agatha Christie, and Stephen King; King even outlined his bestseller Misery during one of his stays. Situated in the upscale Mayfair neighborhood, world-class shopping and top London museums ensure your agenda stays full. From $966 per night.

Craving an escape? From colorful carry-ons to cleverly designed packing cubes (how did we ever travel without them?), shop everything you need to make the journey in style—and as streamlined and stress-free as possible.

#4533. The 8 Best Slip-On Walking Shoes, According to Our Editors

Looking to get out the door quicker? Don’t let laces slip you up.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

SLIDING INTO A pair of slip-on walking shoes might be the easiest thing you’ll do all day. This footwear was built for speed and convenience, making it an accessory that deserves valuable real estate by your door.

Slip-on walking shoes have a rocky history—older versions had a looser fit, raising the risk of instability and friction, especially with higher mileage. But newer designs use no-tie laces, sock liners, pull tabs, and collapsible heels to create a custom fit that rivals what you’ll get with traditional laces.

These shoes are an obvious choice if you have dexterity issues, but they're also just convenient for everyone looking for an easy pair of walking shoes. Slip-on walking shoes also offer more structure than other laceless shoe designs, which has earned them a fanbase with podiatrists. “They are typically a much better option than flip-flops or slides because they provide more stability and keep the foot properly secured,” says Melissa Lockwood, DPM, a podiatrist at Heartland Foot and Ankle Associates in Bloomington, Illinois.

Nicole Graf, DPM, founder of Lexington Podiatry and associate professor at the University of Kentucky, also calls slip-on walking shoes a “huge step up” from flip-flops and slides.

Slip-on walking shoes aren’t for everyone. The fit can still throw some people off, and some simply prefer the tighter feel that laces deliver. But if you want travel-friendly shoes you can toss on in a second, these are a solid choice. As people who value quality, supportive footwear, Men’s Health editors have put in plenty of miles in slip-on walking shoes. The below check all the boxes.

The On Cloud 6 is an excellent example of how good a slip-on shoe can be. You can get in and out of this sneaker with no issue thanks to its wide opening, but the shoe’s elastic “speed” laces and sockliner create a snug fit once you’re in. (Those elastic laces can be adjusted to find your perfect fit.)

The sneaker uses Zero-Gravity CloudTec, which is On’s signature lightweight foam with hollow pods below for a cushioned, yet stable, feel underfoot. (Just be aware that those pods are prone to trapping small pebbles and debris.) A built-in flexible plate, called a Speedboard, helps push you forward with each step. This shoe also comes in a waterproof option, if you’re into rainy strolls. With a wide range of color options and an unobnoxious design, you can pair these with just about anything.

It’s tough to find a quality shoe under $100 right now, let alone one from a big brand. But the Adidas Cloudfoam Cuxxion Sock Shoes have some colorways under $50, making them an ideal choice for budget shoppers who don’t want to mess with laces.

This shoe uses Adidas’ signature Cloudfoam technology at the midsole for a plush, cushioned feel. Its Adiwear outsole is durable and grippy enough for most needs, too. But the real standout in this shoe is its elastic sock upper. It molds to your foot to create a surprisingly snug feel, even for narrow feet. The elastic sock collar supports that secure feel, although it may be too tight for people with wider ankles. The engineered knit upper is highly breathable, lowering the odds you’ll end your walk with sweaty feet. We appreciate the oversized pull tabs in front and back, making the process of getting these on and off seamless.

Feet come in all sizes, making it tough to find a slip-on shoe that works for everyone—and putting adjustable features at a premium. The Nike Pegasus 42 EasyOn has a specialized EasyOn lacing and bungee system up top to help you get the level of tightness you want, before locking it into place. (The lock and bungee combination takes some practice to get right at first.)

The shoe has a collapsible heel that springs back into place once you’re in, making the process of getting these on seamless. It’s also crafted with the same ReactX foam and Air Zoom unit as the standard—and popular—Nike Pegasus 42 running shoe, delivering an energetic feel under your feet. Convenient pull tabs at the heel sit flush against the shoe when they’re not in use, adding to the shoe’s overall sleekness.

Read More: Best Walking Shoes for Men Over 50

Stability shoes are designed to guide your foot with each step and prevent too much inward rolling as you go. But choosing a stability shoe usually means sacrificing a high level of cushioning, since a shoe that feels too plush may not be overly supportive. Hoka’s Skyward Laceless manages to create a stable, locked-in feel while still delivering plenty of the brand’s signature cushioning underfoot.

The Skyward Laceless has a stretchy upper that gently hugs your foot, creating a sock-like feel. The ankle and instep are equally snug, which can be a good or bad thing, depending on your foot size and width. This shoe has plenty of foam below for a slightly bouncy feel, but it still corrects your stride as you go. The integrated tongue pull tab is a nice touch, making for easier entry.

The Under Armour SlipSpeed Mega is packed with features you won’t find in most slip-on walking shoes, creating a versatile, unique sneaker. For starters, this shoe converts from a sneaker design to a recovery slipper with the help of a collapsible heel. (Under Armour refers to this as going from “speed mode” to “slip mode.”)

The shoe skips elastic laces or a sockliner upper in favor of Under Armour’s specialized BOA fit system, a 12-point dial that you can turn to adjust the width across the midfoot. The wide base creates a stable feel, while lots of foam cushioning underfoot delivers a plushness you’ll appreciate. A heads-up: This shoe is slightly heavier than others at 11.7 ounces. That’s a difference you may start to feel on long walks.

After a grueling workout, it feels borderline pampering to slide into the right recovery shoe. The Kane Footwear Active Recovery Shoe is armed with features that should make it a fixture in your gym bag.

This slip-on has strong arch support and a slight rocker shape to help ease pressure on the bottom of your feet as you walk. Internal nodes in the footbed help to promote blood flow and circulation down below, while the shoe’s surprisingly secure design helps prevent slipping. The Active Recovery Shoe is water-resistant with an airy design that allows you to take this in and out of the gym shower without ending up with sopping feet. Heel tabs in back make it simple to get in and out.

Merrell is known for its rugged, outdoor footwear and hiking shoes. While the Merrell Jungle Moc isn’t exactly a hiking shoe, it’s a solid option when you want good traction, whether you’re navigating rocky ground or a slippery surface. The Jungle Moc has been a bestseller for years, with its simple slip-on design and high level of cushioning.

Dual side stretch panels and an exaggerated heel tab make it simple to get in and out of these shoes. The suede upper is breathable, but water-resistant to keep you dry in nearly all conditions. The Jungle Moc has a cushioned EVA midsole for stability and comfort, too. While these shoes are easy to get on, the curved heel makes it harder to slide out—just something to be aware of.

Clogs admittedly aren’t the right style for everyone—some offer less support than sneakers and may slip around more than a standard shoe. But the sleek Crocs Dylan Clog is a solid option for people who want a clog style without some of the common issues that come with it.

The Dylan Clog has a deep heel cup to help lock your foot in, lowering the risk it’ll move around as you step. It’s also made from one piece of Croslite (Crocs’ signature material) to prevent wear and tear over time. This shoe looks like leather thanks to its subtle pebbled texture, but it’s water-resistant and dries quickly when wet. The only major downside is that it’s not very breathable in front. If you’re prone to sweaty toes, this may not be the shoe for you.

Not all shoes are created equal, but the best slip-on walking shoes have all of the supportive features you’ll find in standard lace-up walking shoes. “You mostly get what you pay for in these,” says Richard H. Graves, DPM, a podiatrist at Sol Foot & Ankle Centers in Long Beach, California. He suggests taking a pass at dirt-cheap slip-on walking shoes. “The cheaper ones are not going to provide enough support in the long run, even if they might feel good at first,” Graves says. “There should be enough structure to support your arch and the midsole should be firm and not too flimsy.”

Many high-quality slip-on walking shoes provide similar comfort to regular sneakers. Look for elements like a breathable upper, pull tabs to make them easy to get on and off, and a roomy toe box. Structured heel panels and extra padding in this area help to keep your foot secure, even without standard laces.

Traction for slip-on walking shoes will vary depending on the type of shoe you choose. Most will offer decent traction—meaning, enough so you don’t feel like you’re slipping on wet or damp pavement. But a more rugged shoe like the Merrell Jungle Moc has a grippier sole to help provide extra traction over rockier ground.

Yes, slip-on walking shoes can provide arch support. If this is an important quality for you, look for features like contoured footbeds and a stable midsole. You’ll also want a shoe that fits well and stays put once it’s on to ensure your arch support is consistent. “Make sure that you're not getting them too loose just to make it easier to slide on and off—then you'll be sacrificing support,” Graves says.

To find the best men’s slip-on walking shoes, MH editors tested the most recent and popular pairs on the market, evaluating each for support, comfort, and traction. Because these shoes are designed for walking, not just comfort, durability was also a key factor. To round things out, we consulted three podiatrists for their input on how to evaluate a slip-on walking shoe, along with the value of this style. Based on their advice and our experience, we chose these slip-on walking shoes.

#4532. Hormone Therapy May Boost Weight Loss on Wegovy, But Not as Much on Zepbound

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Females using menopause hormone therapy (MHT) alongside GLP-1 medications such as Ozempic or Wegovy may experience greater weight loss, according to a study presented at the 2026 Annual Meeting of the Menopause Society.

Weight gain is a common concern among middle-aged women. While much of this is associated with aging, hormonal changes during menopause can also contribute to changes in body fat and its distribution.

GLP-1 medications have been shown to be effective for weight loss. However, the results may vary by sex and reproductive stage.

For example, studies have found that women and adults under age 55 may experience greater weight loss with these medications.

A previous small observational study in postmenopausal women found that using MHT alongside semaglutide, the active ingredient in Ozempic and Wegovy, was associated with greater weight loss.

However, whether hormone therapy influences weight loss outcomes with different GLP-1 medications remains unclear.

To investigate this question, researchers compared weight loss in middle-aged women receiving either semaglutide or tirzepatide, with or without MHT. The findings have not yet been published in a peer-reviewed journal.

The study included 3,855 women with an average age of 57 and an average body mass index (BMI) of 36 kg/m2.

The semaglutide cohort included 2,379 individuals, while the tirzepatide cohort included 1,476 individuals.

The average follow-up in the semaglutide cohort, both with and without MHT, was 26 months. The average follow-up in the tirzepatide group was 17 months for those using MHT and 16 months for those not using it.

The researchers found that in the semaglutide cohort, those using MHT alongside the GLP-1 medication had significantly greater weight loss at 9 months than those not using MHT.

These differences were sustained at 12 and 15 months and at the final follow-up. Those using MHT were also more likely to reach up to a 30% total body weight loss than those not using it.

In the tirzepatide cohort, those using MHT had a modestly greater weight loss at 3 months and were more likely to achieve more than 5% total body weight loss by the final follow-up. However, no significant difference in weight loss was observed at later time points.

These findings suggest that MHT use may be associated with greater weight loss among middle-aged women taking semaglutide. However, the researchers did not observe a sustained, statistically significant difference in weight loss among those taking tirzepatide.

Betul Hatipoglu, MD, Director of the Diabetes and Obesity Center at the University Hospitals Cleveland Medical Center, who was not involved in the study, told Healthline that the findings are encouraging.

However, she also cautioned that people should be careful not to interpret them as evidence that hormone therapy should be added to GLP-1 treatment specifically to enhance weight loss.

“This is an association, and the study provides an opportunity to discuss how menopause, aging, changes in body composition, and metabolic health can be present,” she said.

It is important to keep in mind that the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has approved certain hormone therapy medications to treat menopause symptoms such as hot flashes, night sweats, and vaginal dryness. However, these medications are not FDA-approved for weight loss or obesity management.

The study authors noted that larger studies are needed to confirm their observations and to inform individualized strategies for middle-aged females.

Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB-GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who was not involved in the study, noted that weight gain is one of the most common concerns she hears from women during menopause. Beyond affecting self-esteem and quality of life, excess abdominal fat can increase the risk of type 2 diabetes and heart disease.

“Finding effective, sustainable ways to manage weight is important for women’s long-term health and healthy aging,” she said.

“I would like to see prospective, randomized studies examining GLP-1 medications with and without hormone therapy to determine whether hormone therapy directly improves weight loss and helps women maintain it over time. In the meantime, treatment decisions should be individualized and guided by a healthcare professional.”

Ross advised that the decision should be based on your individual symptoms, health history, and treatment goals.

“Hormone therapy can significantly improve disruptive menopausal symptoms, including hot flashes, sleep disturbances, and mood changes. By helping women feel better and more energetic, it may also make it easier to exercise and manage their weight,” she said.

Hatipoglu noted that GLP-1-based medications, on the other hand, are effective treatments for obesity and can improve important aspects of metabolic health. Their use should be based on an individual’s weight-related health risks, medical history, and treatment goals.

“For a woman who has both bothersome menopausal symptoms and obesity, it may be entirely appropriate to consider both treatments. But I would not recommend starting hormone therapy solely to improve the weight-loss response to a GLP-1 medication based on the evidence currently available,” she said.

“It is also important to remember that successful weight management during and after menopause is about more than the number on the scale. Preserving muscle mass, maintaining bone health, improving metabolic health, and supporting long-term physical function are equally important.”

You should speak with your healthcare professional about the risks and benefits of all treatment options.

#4531. Steam's Achievement Sets beta aims to stop new content breaking your completion status

Valve is beta testing Steam Achievement Sets, a feature that lets developers group a game's achievements into collections.

In an announcement, the company said that games "live longer than ever, continuing to receive updates through new DLC, expansions, and seasons", but that achievements added with new content "can be hard to discover and can accidentally break the completion status of players who have already finished the game".

That's why developers can now specify the number of sets and the purpose of each "to match their game's specific needs", so one game might group its achievements into single-player and multiplayer groups, while another could use eight class-based sets. Players still unlock achievements as usual, and Steam then sorts what they've unlocked into the developer-created sets, which Valve says makes it "easier to see what you have completed and to find other related achievements you can unlock next".

Each set has its own progress bar, and completing one adds a "set complete" trophy to the player's profile. If new achievements are later added to a set, completion is "preserved".

Developers can also create new sets as a game grows, which Valve says makes it "easy for returning players to see what new things they can unlock, while also preserving their prior completion status until they play again".

There are two types of set. Base Game sets "contain achievements that can be earned by all players who play a game", with progress and trophies shown in blue, while DLC sets group achievements that can only be earned once additional content has been bought and are shown in purple.

Interestingly, DLC achievements "are not included in your overall game progress until you have access to the content", so players can track what they have unlocked in new content separately.

Developers can also archive individual achievements or entire sets, which Valve says could suit "season-based games or enabling time-limited holiday achievements". Players who had already unlocked an archived achievement or set "will still see it on their profile", but archived achievements "won't show up on the profile pages of users who missed the unlock nor block their progress toward completing a set or game".

Alongside the sets comes an updated achievements page, reachable from the Games List on the web and from games in the Steam Library, and Search now has filters for specific sets and for locked or unlocked achievements. Players can also compare their progress with "up to three friends, including rare achievements, completed sets, and progress in DLC sets".

Elsewhere, the Perfect Games tab is now called Completed and "expands to show both completed games and games with completed achievement sets", games played via Family Sharing now appear in the Games List, and friends who played a game that way are eligible for friend comparison. Store and Library pages will also show if a game has achievement sets.

The beta is available to players and developers, and Valve says it will "continue making improvements during the beta", adding that "as developers begin using the new tools, you'll start seeing these changes appear for supported games".

Games that have already added achievement sets are Against the Storm, Age of Wonders 4, American Truck Simulator, Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4, Crusader Kings 3, Dredge, Euro Truck Simulator 2, Forza Horizon 5, Flock Around, He is Coming, LEGO Batman: Legacy of the Dark Knight, Left 4 Dead 2, Portal 2, PowerWash Simulator, PowerWash Simulator 2, Team Fortress 2, and Victoria 3.

Valve's PC digital store and social platform set a new user concurrent record last Sunday, clocking up a staggering 43,505,724 users. Meanwhile, Epic Games isn't lifting its foot off the gen-AI pedal, having vibe-coded a new Epic Games Launcher in an attempt to outrun Steam, by far the most popular digital games marketplace on PC.

#4529. Abiotic Factor's second year of updates confirmed to include official mod support and a DLC taking science to the tropics

Following its 1.0 release last July, Half Life-esque sci-fi survival game Abiotic Factor has been having a pretty good run of post-early access updates. That run of is set to continue through to 2027, as developers Deep Field have announced the game's year two roadmap including full support for community mods, a winter update with smaller additions, and the previously teased paid DLC, Entropic Break.

The mod support is pretty exciting, as it means a free mod launcher alongside the regular executable, as well as proprietary tools to create them, and a mod browser to share mods and host game instances affected by mods. Potential moddables include items, weapons, gear, pets, enemies, and "more", which might refer to maps themselves, if "modded sessions" is anything to go by. The update that mod support arrives with, Community Update 5, will also introduce a new 'foggy friend' pet (really hoping that's as cute as it sounds and isn't secretly horrifying, as many beings in Abiotic are), a new multi-quest system to collate objectives in the journal, and various balance adjustments.

The next free update is the considerably smaller winter update, with the most meaningful addition seeming to be the ability to grow trees in soil plots, allowing for self-sustaining farming of apples and "other stuff". That, alongside new recipes befitting the most wonderful time of the year and a brewing bench, which specifies 'fun and festive brews' but might allow for recipes outside of those seasonally adjacent.

The other big highlight here is the Entropic Break DLC, a paid expansion that takes the science team out of their usual claustrophobic nightmare and onto the pale sands of the Leaf Test Facility, which undoubtedly will be just as claustrophobic nightmare once you find your way into another underground lab gone wrong. A collection of new items, enemies, sidequests, pets, boats, fishing, and crossovers (something new to Abiotic Factor) are on the way with this update — the crossovers are paired with the tagline "It'll have you hooked!", so I have to wonder if it'll be another of Dredge's many crossovers. Several new portal worlds are also listed to be added, with only one being named as 'our biggest portal world yet'; from the trailer, it appears to be some kind of Mystery Flesh Pit National Park-like eldritch meat-based dimension. Groovy. I played a little of Abiotic Factor earlier this year, and had a pretty wonderful time struggling to survive and being made fun of by my then-partner for my utterly horrific survival instincts, so I can certainly recommend it, especially with these updates on the way.

#4528. Why Some Drivers Did or Did Not Exclude Themselves From NASCAR’s Most Popular Driver

Chase Elliott follows in his father's footsteps, taking his name off the NMPD ballot so Kyle Busch can win posthumously.

It’s been 36 years since a NASCAR driver without the last name Earnhardt or Elliott has won Most Popular Driver. Talladega, Chase Elliott removes himself from contention so Kyle Busch can win.

Elliott has held the spot for seven seasons, winning every year since Dale Earnhardt Jr. retired from the NASCAR Cup Series. Earnhardt won it every year since Chase’s father, Bill Elliott, retired in 2002.

The elder Elliott won it every year from 1991 to 2002, except in 2001.

In 2001, following the shocking death of Dale Earnhardt Sr. on the last lap of the Daytona 500, Elliott removed himself from contention out of respect. This year, his son did the same, taking his name off the docket so Kyle Busch could win posthumously.

The NASCAR community tragically lost Busch in the spring, the Thursday before the Coke 600 at Charlotte Motor Speedway, to sepsis.

Ever polarizing and often hated by fans of his competitors, Busch was never the most popular driver. But in losing him and mourning him, the NASCAR community has come together, with begrudging respect, to see the importance of his legacy.

“When Dale passed in the early 2000s, dad removed his name,” Elliott said. “Ultimately, I was a young child, but looking back on that, I just always thought that was a really cool and admirable nod to, frankly, one of his biggest rivals.”

For the younger Elliott, Busch was less of a rival and more of an inspiration. Watching him dominate from week to week as a kid coming up at the racetrack. He was a fan.

“Yeah, I was a fan,” Elliott said. “Really anybody my age and probably a little younger that were coming along at that point in time and got to see his dominance. I mean, if somebody sits there and tells you that they didn't want to be like that in some way, shape, or form, they are lying to your face.

“The guy was killing it. He was out there and he was unbeatable at some places. And I feel like he kind of embraced that role, and he loved that character that he took on and kind of being that villain role. But as a racer and a guy coming along, it was really hard not to admire just his craft and how good he was behind the wheel, and just some of the things that he changed the game with from a race car driver's perspective and coming from somebody who loves the discipline of what we do and the craft behind the wheel that ultimately makes somebody better than the next a lot. “

Elliott was allowed to remove his name from contention as his dad did, but other drivers were advised not to.

With the reasoning of Kyle wouldn’t want to win by default, drivers like Kyle Larson and Carson Hocevar were told to stay on the ballot.

Even though their names are on the ballot, they’re asking their fans not to vote for them.

“It's crazy what a driver that was probably super hated or not appreciated enough,” Hocevar said. “He's super, super appreciated now after his passing. And I think it just feels right. And I texted somebody, and I was like, ‘Hey, just go ahead and take my name off.’ And they used to be PR really close to Kyle, and they're like, "Hey, he wouldn't want to win it by default.”

Larson didn’t ask to be taken off the ballot because he knew NASCAR wanted to minimize the number of guys removing themselves.

“So yeah, hopefully everybody votes for him,” Larson said. “I would pull my name off the ballot if I was allowed to, but it didn't sound like we were allowed to pull my name off the ballot.”

Larson, Elliott and Hocevar have cast their votes for Busch to win this year. It's reasonable to assume every other driver will follow suit.

Victoria Beaver is a nomadic sports writer who spends her time hopping between race tracks and hippie farms. She’s covered every corner of motorsports that will let her in from 410 Sprints to NASCAR to Supercross. Her daily driver is a 2010 Subaru that she refused to do the smallest amount of preventative maintenance on. Instead, she spends her free time and money building a 42-foot Skoolie to one day travel the country full time.

#4526. 2027 BMW i7

See all results for new 2027 BMW i7 for sale near ?

How do you make BMW's 7-series flagship sedan even more luxurious? Try fitting it with an electric powertrain that doesn’t make a peep. That's exactly what the 2027 BMW i7 has to offer. From its imposing presence to its world-class interior environs, everything about it screams premium. Inside, the i7 is a blend of Buckingham Palace and the Millennium Falcon, fusing traditionally opulent materials with bleeding-edge tech.

The 2027 i7 range will launch with two models: the 50 xDrive and the 60 xDrive. Both come with a dual-motor, all-wheel-drive electric powertrain. As part of an extensive update this year, the i7 implements styling cues and improved battery and drive-motor tech from BMW's latest Neue Klasse vehicles, resulting in faster charge times and an improved EPA-estimated driving range of up to 373 miles.

Expert Tip: The Mercedes-Benz EQS is the i7's fiercest rival, also merging classically luxurious interior accommodations with ultra-modern tech. It's worthy of a look if you're shopping for ultra-high-end EV sedans.

Compare the 2027 BMW i7 with the top vehicles in this segment with our new compare tool.

The BMW i7 has been comprehensively reworked for the 2027 model year, now wearing revised styling and integrating technologies from the company's Neue Klasse vehicle lineup. The i7's front kidney grilles are now a bit slimmer, and the split-tier headlight design on last year's model has been ditched in favor of a single-strip look. Around back, its taillights have been extended further into the center of the trunk lid, mirroring the rear-end design of the iX3 and i3.

Inside, the i7 gets a fully revamped tech suite, complete with a new 17.9-inch hexagonal infotainment screen and a width-spanning instrument cluster that projects vehicle information on a thin strip at the bottom of the windshield.

The i7 has also been overhauled under its sheet metal, now featuring a larger battery pack with better energy density than the prismatic cells found in last year's car. Its electric motors have also been reworked and optimized for better packaging and efficiency.

If this year's model is largely unchanged and you're comfortable with a lightly used car, you may want to consider shopping previous model years. A one-to-three-year-old model will likely have a warranty and it'll cost less than buying new.

Our new comparison tool provides a comprehensive, side-by-side comparison of up to five vehicles of your choice.

No matter which model you choose, all i7s come with a dual-motor powertrain that grants all-wheel drive. The entry-level 50 xDrive makes 449 horsepower, while the top-dog 60 xDrive pumps out a total of 536 horsepower. When we drove the 2027 i7, we found it to offer a similarly smooth and plush experience as the gas-powered models, only with slightly quicker acceleration response.

In Comparison: The previous i7 we tested did the 60-mph sprint in 4.4 seconds. Even if the new model improves upon that time, it's likely to remain slower than its chief rival, the Mercedes-Benz EQS580 4Matic, which managed to hit the mile-a-minute mark in just 3.7 seconds.

Under its revised sheet metal, the 2027 i7 uses a 113-kWh modular battery pack which features improved cylindrical cells that boast a 20-percent-higher energy density than before. This enables the new model to drive further and charge faster. EPA range figures for the new model are up to 373 miles for the 50 xDrive, and up to 362 miles for the 60 xDrive. Those figures are with the standard 20-inch wheels with summer tires, and opting for the all-season tires or the larger 21-inch wheels drops the estimates slightly.

The maximum charge rate is now 250 kW, and BMW claims that the i7 can be charged from 10 to 80 percent in 28 minutes under optimal conditions on a DC fast charger. All i7s come with an NACS charge port as standard, in addition to an array of adapters for several other charger types.

The EPA estimates the i7's fuel economy as high as 94 MPGe city and 96 MPGe highway. Those figures are for the 50 xDrive model, and the 60 xDrive is slightly less efficient. MPGe stands for miles per gallon of gas equivalent, an EPA metric that estimates PHEV and EV fuel efficiency. For more information about the i7's fuel economy, visit the EPA's website.

First Row: The i7, like its gas brother, takes the high road, offering comfortable leather seating with heat, ventilation, and 18-way power adjustments as standard. A variety of wood, metal, and carbon fiber trim materials are available to choose from. The i7 has a funky and futuristic steering wheel, which features spokes at 12 and 6 o'clock. It's an objectively weird design, but the thumb cutouts make it comfortable to hold, and its touch-sensitive buttons are easy to reach.

Second Row: The i7's rear seating is limo-like and cosseting, offering standard heat and individual climate control. The rear passenger compartment can also be optimized for chauffeur-driven travel with available massage and ventilation. The 7's star feature, however, is the available rear-seat entertainment system featuring a large 31.3-inch 8K screen that drops from the ceiling and stretches across the entire rear passenger compartment.

Cargo Capacity: The i7's trunk capacity is 18 cubic feet, giving up just one cubic foot to its gas-powered 7-Series doppelganger. The Porsche Taycan S has 14 cubic feet of luggage room. In our testing, the i7's trunk held six carry-on suitcases.

Instead of using a traditional digital gauge display located behind the steering wheel, key vehicle information is projected onto a strip of darkened glass at the base of the windshield that spans from A-pillar to A-pillar. The speed readout and battery information are located directly ahead of the driver, while media information and an avatar for BMW's AI-powered digital assistant float above a new 17.9-inch hexagonal touchscreen display that takes on infotainment duties in the center of the dash. All i7s now also come with a 14.6-inch front passenger display for some in-transit entertainment.

Many driver-assistance features are standard, with more advanced tech optional. Available features include a hands-free driving mode for use on the highway and a parking assistant system that allows the driver to exit the vehicle and park the car via a smartphone app.

For information about the i7's crash-test results, visit the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) and Insurance Institute for Highway Safety (IIHS) websites.

The i7 comes standard with the same warranty coverage as BMW's other electrified models, which means basic coverage for four years or 50,000 miles and more generous protection for the electric-vehicle components. All new BMWs also come with a three-year complimentary maintenance plan.

#4525. Sega pulls Total War: Shogun 2 - Complete Edition from sale amid AI accusations, admitting it has "fallen short"

Sega and Creative Assembly are temporarily removing Total War: Shogun 2 - Complete Edition from sale just days after release.

In a statement shared on Twitter/X, the companies said they are aware the game "has fallen short of both your expectations and our own standards".

"We've seen your feedback, comments, and reviews. We agree that the current experience is not where it needs to be," the statement continued.

The companies are working "with platform holders to temporarily remove the game from sale and to reinstate the original game while we investigate the issues".

"Existing owners will still be able to access and play the game," the statement added.

Sega and Creative Assembly say they are also "working closely with our development partner to identify the causes of these problems, deliver improvements", with the aim that the Complete Edition meets "the quality standards players expect from Total War".

The Complete Edition launched on 13th October. The statement does not detail the issues or say when the game will return to sale.

The statement does not use the term AI at all, but many fans believe it's at the root of the "issues", pointing out "hokey upscaling" and what they believe to be evidence of AI in the Complete Edition.

Within days of release, Shogun 2 - Complete Edition's Steam score plummeted to "overwhelmingly negative" with just 15 percent of the game's more than 4,000 reviews leaving something positive. If you head to the game's Steam page now, you're warned that the game "is no longer available on the Steam store".

Last month, we reported that the CEO of Professor Layton developer Level-5 apologised after confirming that generative AI was used in the studio's recent showcase. Level-5 is not alone, of course - many other game companies, including Krafton, Square Enix, EA, and more, have invested heavily in AI tech.

#4524. Battlefield 6 Season 2 delayed like many of us expected, but we're getting a new update and a bonus battle pass path

Battlefield Studios has released its first official communication in 2026. Rather than unveil the second season of Battlefield 6, however, the developer instead announced a delay. The currently ongoing Season 1 was initially meant to end on Tuesday, January 20, hence why the developer had to communicate something this week.

Season 1 will now end on Tuesday, February 17, which is also when Season 2 will kick off. Given the current sentiment around the game, this news isn’t surprising in the least.

Escalating the fight with new threats on the battlefield.Season 2 begins February 17. pic.twitter.com/fGMegJJuTj

Though the latest blog post may be devoid of any real details on Season 2, BF Studios confirmed that Battlefield Labs will play a larger role in offering a preview of what’s to come, so it’s entirely possible that the testing initiative will soon open its doors to players with an early look at some of the maps and other content planned for Season 2.

#4523. The latest game event to fall afoul of US visa rules is a live concert for horse girl racer Umamusume: Pretty Derby

Umamusume: Pretty Derby has turned out to be one of the most popular games in the world, partly due to being a gacha game and partly due to making pretty girls out of very real Japanese racehorses (which introduces a unique mourning when the real horse passes away and the anime girl lives on). A new kind of mourning faces the Umamusume fanbase now, as an upcoming stop on the game's The Stage: World Tour at Los Angeles' Peacock Theater has been cancelled right before it was supposed to open this week. The apparent cause? The US visa system.

First announced in July, the concert was supposed to begin on Wednesday, but developers Cygames have unfortunately had to confirm that it cannot run as planned. The reason for this is stated to be that "although we have coordinated repeatedly with all parties concerned and explored every possible option in our efforts to make this event happen, we were unable to obtain the necessary visas for entry into the US." In a prior post, they stated that they had been working with lawyers and promoters to acquire visas for "over six months", but had yet to receive a response.

This isn't the only recent example of a Los Angeles game concert being affected by struggles to obtain US visas, as a performer on Wuthering Waves' tour, Tarokiki, was also unable to perform in California due to not acquiring a visa in time. And it certainly isn't the first time that game events, more generally, have been affected by the US's increasingly outsider-hostile political climate. This year's GDC quite infamously saw a large number of international and/or queer developers elect not to attend, to prioritise their own safety.

The prospect of not being able to see the voices behind the horses in the flesh, nor to enjoy the soundtrack live in concert, seems to have been a sort of breaking point for some in the fanbase, as there have been quite a few posts from fans along the Community-esque lines of "I can excuse fascism, but I draw the line at cancelling the horse concert." One much-joked about poster stated, seemingly quite earnestly, "This will actually radicalize me if this gets cancelled, fuck my country." It is insane to think that there are genuinely people who might have begun to hop off an alt-right pipeline and learn class/cultural consciousness as a result of an Umamusume concert, but that's the world we live in. Everyone has a breaking point, I suppose; if it takes some people being affected by losing their precious treats to be radicalised against fascism, I'll take it as better than not being radicalised at all.

#4522. How to avoid the iPhone’s notorious ‘silent alarm’ bug

Updated Oct 27, 2026 12:54 PM EDT

You might’ve seen recent reports of users complaining that they’ve slept through alarms set on their iPhones–or even encountered it yourself. But it’s not a new bug. The silent iPhone alarm issue has plagued users for at least a couple of years, and still keeps affecting people (even though Apple has previously promised to fix it).

The bug causes alarms to go off, but silently—there’s no audible ring, and no vibration. That’s not ideal if you need to get up for work, a flight, or anything else you have to do. So what exactly is going on? Here’s what we know about the so-called silent alarm bug on the iPhone and what you can do about it.

You can rule out Silent mode and Do Not Disturb. According to Apple, your alarm still sounds when either one is turned on.

If you set an alarm, it’s important that it actually alerts you at the right time. But these silent alarms that users have been noticing on their iPhones don’t make any sound and don’t trigger any vibrations. They do appear on screen as normal, but they’re completely silent.

As for what’s causing it, no one seems certain. Back in 2024, Apple said it was fixing the issue, without going into much detail about what was behind it—but users are finding that it’s still happening. Sometimes it seems that a particular iOS version will patch the problem, only for it to show up again in a subsequent version.

Given that it’s been happening for a long time, and affecting a substantial number of users (but not everyone), it’s possible that there are multiple causes. Apple hasn’t explained what’s behind it, but its alarm support page lists troubleshooting steps, and there are other things you can do to minimize the likelihood of silent alarms.

First, make sure your alarms are set as intended from the Alarms tab of the Clock app. Tap on an alarm and you can check the Sound option to see the noise it triggers and the Repeat option to check the frequency—it may be that your alarm is set to go off some days and not others. If a specific alarm isn’t ringing, try deleting it and creating it again.

You can get to the audio settings for your iPhone by opening up Settings, then choosing Sounds & Haptics. Look at the volume slider under Ringtone and Alerts to make sure it’s high enough. You might also want to turn off Change with Buttons to make sure you’re not adjusting the alert volume accidentally. Alarms aren’t affected by the Silent Mode toggle or the Ring/Silent switch, so you don’t need to change either one.

Connected headphones aren’t likely to be the culprit, either. Apple says that if wired or wireless headphones are connected, your alarm plays through the iPhone’s built-in speakers as well as the headphones.

Some users have found they can overcome the silent alarm bug by turning off the Attention-Aware Features toggle switch, which you’ll find under Face ID & Passcode in Settings. This changes certain iPhone behaviors, including the level of alarm sounds, if it thinks you’re looking at the screen. It could explain certain silent alarms—though it should only ever lower the alarm volume level, not mute it completely.

If your alarm makes a sound but doesn’t vibrate, check whether your iPhone was in StandBy, the full-screen clock view that appears when the phone is charging on its side. Apple turns off alarm haptics in StandBy, so you’ll only get the sound.

Do Not Disturb and any other modes you’ve set up in Focus in Settings don’t silence alarms either. They control notifications from apps and people, so they’re unlikely to be behind the problem.

Apple also warns that manually changing the time on your iPhone (a trick some people use to skip game timers) can affect alarms. Leave Set Automatically turned on under General > Date & Time in Settings.

Finally, be sure to keep your iPhone up to date with the latest version of iOS. While it seems as though this bug is persisting in the latest software releases, hopefully at some point Apple will squash it for good, and when that happens you’re going to want to get the update as soon as possible.

Not every missed alarm is the silent bug. When iOS 26 launched, Apple redesigned the alarm screen with large Stop and Snooze buttons sitting close together, which made it easy to stop an alarm when you meant to snooze it. In iOS 26.1, Apple replaced the Stop button with a Slide to stop gesture, while Snooze is still a single tap.

If your alarms are ringing but you keep switching them off half-asleep, open Settings and go to Accessibility > Touch. If Prefer Single-Touch Actions is turned on, your iPhone swaps the slider back for a one-tap Stop button. Turn it off to get the slider back. The setting changes other sliders across iOS too, not just alarms.

Something else you can do to try and avoid silent alarms is to install a third-party alarm app, and there are lots to choose from. Alarmy actually prides itself on the loudness of its alarm alerts, so you should have no problems waking up. You can get some extra premium features and remove the ads by paying $4.99 a month, but the basic functionality is free.

Sleep Cycle is another app worth considering. Not only will it wake you up, it also has a smart alarm feature that aims to rouse you at the most beneficial time in your sleep cycle (within a preset window). It’ll track your sleep too—there’s a lot to it. You get the basics for free, with a pile of premium features (including weather reports and more sleep stats) available for a $2.99-per-month subscription.

Or you can invest in a smart alarm clock. Or just an old-fashioned alarm clock with the big red numbers and real buttons.

#4521. This Is How Fast You Should Walk to Help Lower Your Risk of Death

  • A study found that walking speed can help predict people’s risk of dying from any cause.
  • Both a clinical test and a test done in daily life were able to predict mortality risk.
  • Experts say walking speed predicts death risk because it’s an indicator of overall health.
  • If your walking speed slows, it’s a good idea to talk with your healthcare professional.

New research shows that how fast you walk, whether measured in a clinical test or through daily activity, can help predict your risk of dying from any cause.

The study compared two ways of measuring walking: a short walking speed test conducted in a clinic and the step rate people take in their everyday lives, measured with a wrist-worn device.

Both measures were linked to mortality risk and showed similar ability to predict it.

The findings, published on October 23 in JAMA Network Open, could help doctors and researchers choose the best method for assessing health risks in different settings.

Clinical walking speed vs. everyday walking

The study was part of the Whitehall II accelerometer substudy in the United Kingdom.

It included 4,067 participants, mostly males, with an average age of about 69 years. About one-third of the subjects were female.

The researchers collected data from January 2012 to December 2013 and followed up through August 2024.

To measure clinical walking speed, participants completed a standard walking test. They walked a marked 2.44-meter course at their usual pace. The test was repeated three times, and the average speed in meters per second was used for analysis. Participants who normally used a cane were allowed to use it during the test.

For free-living walking measurements, participants wore a wrist-worn GENEActiv accelerometer for 9 consecutive days. This device recorded movement at a high frequency and used special software to detect walking episodes and count steps.

From this data, two key measures of walking cadence were calculated: the median walking cadence (the middle value of steps per minute during walking periods) and the 95th-percentile walking cadence (a higher step rate indicating brisk walking). The study also calculated the average number of daily steps.

The researchers additionally gathered information on factors known to affect mortality, such as:

  • age
  • sex
  • education
  • race and ethnicity
  • marital status
  • smoking
  • alcohol use
  • diet
  • body mass index (BMI)
  • hypertension
  • chronic diseases

National death records were used to identify deaths during the follow-up period.

Statistical methods were then used to estimate the risk of death associated with walking speed and cadence while accounting for other risk factors.

The models were adjusted step-by-step: starting with no adjustments, then adding sociodemographic factors, behavioral factors, and finally health-related factors.

The ability of walking measures to predict mortality was assessed using a statistical measure.

The study also examined differences between males and females.

The researchers additionally conducted analyses to test how well the results held up when they excluded early deaths, looked at subgroups by age and health status, and used different definitions of walking cadence.

Faster walking speed linked to 23% lower risk of death

During a median follow-up of nearly 12 years, 739 participants died.

The average clinical walking speed of participants was 1.2 meters per second. The median walking cadence during daily life was about 75 steps per minute, and the 95th percentile walking cadence was about 112 steps per minute.

The study found only a weak correlation between clinical walking speed and free-living walking cadence, meaning the two measures do not strongly reflect the same walking behavior. However, both measures were independently associated with a lower risk of death.

After adjusting for age, sex, lifestyle, and health factors, for every standard increase in clinical walking speed, the risk of death was 23% lower.

Similarly, higher median and 95th-percentile walking cadences were associated with 17% and 18% lower risks of death, respectively.

When examining how well these walking measures predicted mortality, each on its own showed modest predictive ability.

Adding clinical walking speed to a model with other known risk factors slightly improved mortality prediction, while adding free-living walking cadence also improved prediction, albeit to a similar degree to that of clinical walking speed.

Combining clinical walking speed with free-living walking parameters provided a small boost in predictive ability, but the effect was not significant after accounting for detailed health factors.

The study also found that walking speed and cadence had similar predictive power for both men and women, although the overall prediction models performed slightly better for women.

Several sensitivity analyses supported the main findings. Results were consistent when excluding early deaths, focusing on healthier or older participants, and using different ways to define walking cadence.

Overall, this study suggests that clinical walking speed tests and free-living walking cadence measurements from wrist-worn devices are both useful in predicting all-cause mortality risk. They provide similar information but reflect different aspects of walking behavior.

The choice between methods may depend on the purpose of the assessment, available resources, and the context in which the measurement is taken.

Faster walkers may live longer because they are healthier

Neha Sabharwal, PT, DPT, a physical therapist and director of rehabilitation specializing in geriatric mobility and functional decline at Vintage Faire Nursing and Rehabilitation Center in Modesto, CA, said that walking speed helps predict longevity because it’s an indicator of someone’s overall health.

“Walking is something most of us do without thinking about it, but it requires several body systems to work together,” she told Healthline. “Your heart and lungs have to keep up, your muscles need to produce enough force, and your brain has to coordinate each step while keeping you balanced.”

Sabharwal, who wasn’t involved in the study, said that in her work, she pays attention to when someone starts walking more slowly than before.

“It is easy to dismiss that change as part of getting older, but there may be something else going on,” she said. This could include muscle weakness, pain, reduced endurance, balance problems, or underlying medical conditions.

According to Sabharwal, the current study is valuable because it suggests that useful information about people’s movement can be obtained outside a clinical setting.

However, she cautioned against misinterpreting the findings to mean that simply walking faster will make you live longer.

“People who naturally walk faster may already have better underlying health,” said Sabharwal.

“I would be less concerned about whether someone walks faster than their neighbor and more interested in whether they are walking slower than they did six months ago,” she said.

What to do if your walking speed slows

Theodore Strange, MD, chairman of medicine at Northwell Staten Island University Hospital and senior vice president of the Metro Market Region at Northwell Health, shared some insights for those who may notice their walking speed has declined. Strange wasn’t involved in the study.

“I mean, if it’s slowing down because a week or two ago, you were walking up a flight of steps with no problem, and now, you’re having some shortness of breath, I think it should be evaluated,” he told Healthline.

Strange added that if you’re experiencing aches or pains that weren’t there before, you should consult a healthcare professional, as they can make exercise more difficult.

It’s also essential to manage any chronic diseases that you have so that you can exercise as well as possible within the constraints of your condition, he said.

“And I think slow and steady wins the race here,” said Strange. He suggests monitoring your walking with a device like a pedometer and progressively increasing your steps.

“Everybody talks about 10,000 steps a day. I think the most recent studies show probably 7,000 to 8,000 is adequate,” he said. You can start where you are and work your way up gradually.

Strange further noted that physical therapy can sometimes be helpful if you have issues with gait mechanics, balance, or strength. Additionally, you might benefit from exercise that is tailored to your condition, for example, if you’ve had a stroke.

Finally, Strange advised stretching before and after exercise to keep your muscles moving.

“I go back to adequate nutrition and hydration being important,” he said. You want to make sure you are adequately supporting your body’s ability to exercise, said Strange.
